# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Greiff, M. (2013). Rewards and the private provision of public goods on dynamic networks. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that in a repeated public goods game with rewards (where giving rewards is costly but beneficial to the receiver), the emergence of a social institution prescribing prosocial behavior (contribute and reward contributors) can lead to efficient public good provision, provided that rewards are only given to contributors and the network structure evolves quickly enough to allow exclusion of non-contributors. If rewards are also given to defectors, or if players update strategies too quickly, the system converges to low contribution and low efficiency. The model does not analyze punishment directly, but exclusion from rewards via network dynamics acts as an endogenous sanction. The findings are theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward mechanisms, especially in dynamic network settings. It provides strong theoretical evidence that reward-based institutions can achieve high efficiency if rewards are targeted only at contributors and if the network allows for exclusion of non-contributors. However, it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it compare reward to punishment directly. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that endogenous exclusion (a form of sanction) can substitute for explicit punishment, but does not quantify the effect of punishment per se. The model's results are most applicable to games with dynamic partner selection and reward, not to standard fixed-group punishment treatments.

- source: Chen, Q., & Chen, T. (2020). Group rewarding can promote cooperation and save costs in public goods games. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that group rewarding, when combined with a blame/shame feedback mechanism, can promote high levels of cooperation and total contributions in PGGs, often at a much lower cost than traditional individual rewards. The effectiveness and cost-efficiency of the mechanism depend on the synergy factor (mpcr equivalent), the cost of being an initiator, and the reward adjustment coefficient. There is an optimal region for the reward parameter where contributions are maximized and costs minimized. The model demonstrates that, in large populations, group rewarding can be a scalable and cost-effective way to promote cooperation. The presence of the blame/shame mechanism is critical for maximizing the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that group-based reward mechanisms, especially when combined with non-monetary social feedback (blame/shame), can increase efficiency in PGG-like environments. While it does not directly study punishment, the findings suggest that soft social sanctions can substitute for costly punishment in promoting cooperation. For prediction tasks, the results indicate that enabling group reward (with social feedback) in a PGG with known control efficiency will likely increase efficiency, especially if the reward parameters are tuned to an optimal intermediate value. The model's focus on cost-effectiveness is particularly relevant for predicting the net efficiency impact of interventions. However, direct predictions about monetary punishment are not possible from this paper.

- source: Zhang, X. Y., Chen, T., Chen, Q., & Li, X. Y. (2020). Will you cooperate in case the payoff can be guaranteed?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing an insurance-like incentive mechanism in a PGG leads to higher cooperation rates and increased group efficiency (net profit, NP), provided that the extra-charge (insurance premium) and incentive rate (compensation multiplier) are set at moderate levels. Too high an extra-charge or too low an incentive rate reduces the effectiveness of the mechanism. The model demonstrates that the mechanism can eliminate second-order free riders and allows organizers to save on incentive costs compared to standard reward mechanisms. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that insurance-like incentive mechanisms can increase efficiency in PGGs, especially when the parameters are set appropriately. While it does not study punishment directly, it offers insight into how alternative incentive mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing a well-calibrated incentive mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) can increase group efficiency relative to a control without such mechanisms. However, the findings are based on simulation, not empirical data, and may not transfer directly to real-world or experimental PGGs with punishment.

- source: Kitts, J. A. (2006). Collective action, rival incentives, and the emergence of antisocial norms. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective action and group efficiency in public-goods-like environments are shaped by a three-way interaction between the value of selective incentives (rewards), the rivalness of those incentives, and the strength of peer influence (cohesiveness). When incentives are nonrival, increasing their value or increasing peer influence both increase participation and group welfare. When incentives are rival, however, strong peer influence can lead to the enforcement of antisocial norms that discourage contribution, reducing group efficiency. The model demonstrates that under high rivalness and high incentive value, increasing peer influence or incentive value can actually decrease collective action and group welfare. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are most applicable to small groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, rival selective incentives and norm enforcement) on group efficiency is highly contingent on the design dimensions of the incentive system and the strength of peer influence. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling strong peer enforcement or increasing the value of selective incentives will not always increase efficiency—if the incentives are rival, these interventions can backfire and reduce group welfare. The model is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward-based incentives, especially when those rewards are rival. While the paper does not model punishment per se, the logic and results are highly informative for any system where sanctions or rewards are rival and peer enforcement is possible.

- source: Schnell, E., & Muthukrishna, M. (2024). Indirect reciprocity undermines indirect reciprocity destabilizing large- scale cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a nested PGG environment with both local and global public goods, indirect reciprocity (reputation-based conditional aid) can stabilize cooperation at a single scale, but when multiple scales are present, local cooperation is more stable and can invade global cooperation. This occurs even when the global PGG has a higher theoretical maximum payoff. The model demonstrates that the presence of multiple, potentially conflicting reputations leads to a situation where local cooperation undermines global cooperation, reducing overall efficiency at the larger scale. The more fragmented the population (more local groups), the harder it is for global cooperation to be stable. Thus, indirect reciprocity, as typically modeled, cannot by itself sustain efficient, large-scale cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of group membership and the scale at which reputational incentives operate are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from indirect reciprocity-based 'punishment' (here, conditional aid/reward) in PGG-like environments. Specifically, if the game design allows for multiple, nested groups and reputations can be built at the local level, enabling local cooperation, then enabling indirect reciprocity (punishment/reward) may not increase, and can even decrease, efficiency at the global (large-group) level. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of multiple group scales and the ability to build local reputations are key negative moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment/reward mechanisms. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the control (no punishment/reward) game is inefficient, but the addition of indirect reciprocity does not guarantee higher efficiency at the largest scale.

- source: Forsyth, P. A. I., & Hauert, C. (2011). Public goods games with reward in finite populations. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in finite populations, allowing individuals to reward contributors to the public good can initiate and maintain a significant minority of cooperators, even though the majority of the population is typically composed of non-cooperating shirkers. Reward is effective at jump-starting cooperation but is unable to stabilize it in the long run due to exploitation by second-order free-riders. The average group payoff (efficiency) is increased relative to pure defection, but full efficiency is not achieved. The effectiveness of reward is largely independent of group size when key parameters are scaled appropriately. The paper does not provide new results on punishment, but notes that punishment is more effective at stabilizing cooperation once established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on the effects of reward (not punishment) in public goods games. It shows that positive incentives can increase efficiency above the baseline of mutual defection, but do not achieve full efficiency due to the persistence of second-order free-riders. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, reward alone is insufficient to fully stabilize cooperation and maximize efficiency. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with reward enabled, but only indirectly inform predictions about punishment. The analysis of group size, MPCR, and reward parameters is informative for understanding how these dimensions affect efficiency with reward. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for cases where the treatment is reward (not punishment), and less directly informative for punishment-enabled games.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2015). Collective action problem in heterogeneous groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that group effort and efficiency in public goods games and group contests depend strongly on group size, within-group heterogeneity, and the nonlinearity of cost and benefit functions. In linear models, group effort typically decreases with group size and increases with heterogeneity, while in nonlinear models, these patterns can reverse. The largest contributors are those with the highest stake or lowest cost, but under some conditions, these individuals may end up with lower net payoffs than free-riders. The review also summarizes experimental findings, noting that punishment can increase contributions but may not always increase total earnings or efficiency, especially in heterogeneous groups. Theoretical models predict that group selection, between-group competition, and synergicity can all increase group effort and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and synthetic evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group heterogeneity, cost/benefit structure, and group selection affect efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency, as punishment is only discussed in the context of prior literature and experiments. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is mainly useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the moderating role of group structure, but not for estimating the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2015). Antisocial pool rewarding does not deter public cooperation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the introduction of both antisocial and prosocial pool rewarding does not deter the evolution of cooperation or reduce group efficiency. Instead, prosocial rewarding continues to promote cooperation, and the aggregation required for rewards to be effective leads to higher group payoff and efficiency. The critical threshold for cooperation to dominate is lowered as the effectiveness of rewarding increases, even when defectors can also reward each other. In well-mixed populations, antisocial rewarding can prevent cooperation unless cooperators are initially predominant, but in structured populations, local aggregation allows cooperation to thrive. The results are robust to increased randomness in the interaction network.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public goods games with pool rewarding, the presence of antisocial rewarding does not reduce the efficiency gains from prosocial rewarding in structured populations. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling reward mechanisms (even if both cooperators and defectors can use them) will generally increase efficiency compared to control, provided the population structure allows for aggregation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings with spatial or network structure, group size of 5, all-or-nothing contributions, and no punishment. The results are less applicable to well-mixed populations or to games with punishment rather than reward.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Benefits of intervention in spatial public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional intervention, modeled as a central authority forcing some defectors to contribute, can increase the efficiency (average total payoff) of a spatial PGG, especially when the synergy factor is high and the intervention cost is controlled. Weak intervention can paradoxically reduce cooperation and efficiency, but moderate intervention can lead to higher group incomes than the standard PGG. Excessive intervention may reduce cooperation. The effect of intervention is distinct from peer punishment, as the cost is shared by all contributors and there is no direct punisher. The findings are based on simulation results and analysis of average payoffs and cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in spatial PGGs, the introduction of an institutional intervention mechanism (analogous to centralized punishment) can increase group efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate intervention degree/intensity and high synergy factor (mpcr). The effect is non-monotonic: weak or excessive intervention can reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency, one should consider the strength and cost of the intervention and the underlying mpcr. The results are most applicable to settings with spatial structure, no peer punishment, and institutional (not individual) enforcement.

- source: Kurokawa, S., & Ihara, Y. (2017). Evolution of group-wise cooperation: Is direct reciprocity insufficient?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper shows that in n-player repeated prisoners' dilemma games with non-rivalrous public goods, direct reciprocity alone can support the evolution of cooperation (and thus high group efficiency), especially as group size increases. This holds even when individuals sometimes make mistakes or have imperfect information, provided they use sufficiently optimistic conditional strategies. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is the condition under which cooperative strategies are selectively favored over defection, which is directly tied to group payoff and efficiency. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated efficiency values, but gives analytic thresholds for when cooperation is favored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with non-rivalrous benefits, increasing group size can make cooperation (and thus high efficiency) more likely, even in the presence of mistakes or imperfect information, as long as players use optimistic conditional strategies. However, the paper does not model punishment, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, information, and error, but not for the effect of punishment. The paper is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated public goods games with direct reciprocity, but not for predicting the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Unemi, T. (2011). Replicator dynamics in public goods games with reward funds. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a compulsory public goods game with a pool-rewarding mechanism, cooperation and high efficiency can be maintained if the net benefit from the reward fund exceeds the cost of contributing to the public good. Specifically, if c2*(r2-1) > sigma (where sigma is the net cost of contributing to the public good), the population can converge to all rewarders (full efficiency) or exhibit stable cycles with high average payoff. If this condition is not met, defectors dominate and efficiency collapses. The model also shows that introducing second-order sanctions (reducing rewards to second-order free riders) can stabilize full cooperation. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGG-like games with reward mechanisms, but only indirectly inform about punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with reward funds, the efficiency outcome depends critically on the relative effectiveness and cost of the reward mechanism compared to the cost of cooperation. The explicit condition c2*(r2-1) > sigma can be used to predict when enabling a reward mechanism will increase efficiency relative to the control (no reward) case. However, the paper does not analyze punishment, so its direct value for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency is limited. The model structure and results may be informative by analogy for understanding how the cost and effectiveness of sanctions (punishment or reward) interact with game parameters (group size, MPCR, etc.) to determine efficiency outcomes.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2018). Seven rules to avoid the tragedy of the commons. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games with three players and implementation error, it is possible to construct deterministic memory-3 strategies that achieve mutual cooperation (efficiency) and defensibility (no player can be exploited), as well as distinguishability (outperforming unconditional cooperators). For n-person games, memory length at least n is required for such strategies. The strategies do not involve explicit punishment actions or costs, but use conditional cooperation and defection rules that can serve similar functions. The findings are theoretical and do not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment mechanisms on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated public goods games, it is possible to achieve high efficiency through carefully designed deterministic strategies with sufficient memory, even in the presence of errors. However, it does not study the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (such as costly punishment or sanctions) on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare games with and without punishment. Therefore, while the results are highly relevant for understanding the potential for cooperation and efficiency in repeated PGGs, they do not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in experimental or empirical settings. The paper is most useful for understanding the theoretical limits of strategy design rather than the causal effect of punishment interventions.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2014). Institutions and Cooperation in an Ecology of Games. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model demonstrates that reputational exclusion, when information about behavior is reliable and flows quickly, allows cooperators to achieve much higher payoffs relative to defectors than capacity constraints. However, if reputation is slow to converge or cooperators are rare, capacity constraints are more effective. The ability of cooperators to join many games under reputation is key to their advantage. The model also finds that increasing the productivity of the public goods game (higher multiplier) increases the relative payoff to cooperators under reputation, but not necessarily under capacity constraints. Limiting the number of games an agent can play reduces the effectiveness of reputation-based exclusion. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning (costly punishment), so direct evidence on punishment is not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it models only exclusion-based institutions (capacity constraints and reputation-based entry) rather than costly punishment. However, it is highly informative about the effects of group entry rules and reputation mechanisms on efficiency and cooperation. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only adjacent, but it provides strong evidence that exclusion and reputation can be powerful alternatives to punishment for promoting efficiency, especially when information is reliable and cooperators are common. The findings suggest that the design of group entry and information flow can be as important as punishment in determining efficiency outcomes.

- source: Stewart, A. J., Parsons, T. L., & Plotkin, J. B. (2016). Evolutionary consequences of behavioral diversity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in iterated public goods games, increasing the number of available contribution levels (behavioral diversity) can either increase or decrease group efficiency, depending on the ratio of public benefit to individual cost (mpcr). With low mpcr, more choices allow for more robust cooperative strategies and higher mean payoffs. With high mpcr, the abundance of suboptimal robust strategies causes populations to get stuck at lower efficiency. The ability to punish (by reducing one's own contribution in response to defection) is necessary for two-choice strategies to be robust, but explicit costly punishment is not modeled. The paper provides analytic conditions for when simple strategies can resist invasion by more complex ones, and uses evolutionary simulations to show the impact of choice set size on mean payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the number of contribution options (all_or_nothing, player_count, mpcr, etc.), but it does not model explicit costly punishment as a separate mechanism. Instead, 'punishment' is operationalized as the ability to reduce one's own contribution in response to others' defection. The findings suggest that increasing the number of contribution options (behavioral diversity) can either help or hurt efficiency, depending on mpcr. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that more behavioral options do not always increase efficiency, and that the effect depends on the underlying game parameters. However, because explicit costly punishment is not present, the results are only adjacent to the core prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2021). Coevolution of actions, personal norms and beliefs about others in social dilemmas. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, in repeated public goods and related games, the long-term average group action and payoff converge to the Nash equilibrium based on material payoffs when there is no external authority. Social and cognitive factors (such as conformity, cognitive dissonance, and beliefs about others) affect the distribution and variance of actions and beliefs, but do not shift the group average away from the material-payoff optimum in the absence of authority. When an external authority promotes a particular action, group behavior can shift toward the promoted standard, sometimes increasing or decreasing efficiency depending on the alignment of the promoted action with the material optimum. The model does not include explicit peer punishment or sanctioning as a treatment variable, so it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical baseline for predicting efficiency in public goods and related games without explicit punishment mechanisms. It shows that, absent external authority or explicit sanctions, efficiency is determined by material payoffs, and social/cognitive factors mainly affect variance and convergence speed. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that, unless punishment is modeled as a material cost for non-conformity (as in the 'disapproval by peers' term), enabling punishment would require additional modeling or empirical evidence. The model is most useful for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency and for understanding the role of soft normative pressures, but does not provide direct evidence for the effect of enabling explicit punishment on efficiency.

- source: Kurokawa, S., Wakano, J. Y., & Ihara, Y. (2010). Generous cooperators can outperform non-generous cooperators when replacing a population of defectors. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player public goods games, reciprocators who are 'generous' (i.e., tolerate some defection before retaliating) can outperform less generous reciprocators and defectors in terms of average payoff and fixation probability, especially when group size is at least 4. The optimal generosity level depends on the benefit-to-cost ratio and group size. Generosity facilitates the emergence of cooperation (replacement of defectors by reciprocators) but does not help maintain cooperation once established. The model provides explicit conditions for when generosity is advantageous and quantifies the optimal generosity for maximizing the likelihood of cooperation's evolutionary success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated public goods games with n ≥ 4, the design of reciprocation strategies (specifically, the degree of generosity/tolerance for defection) can significantly affect the efficiency outcome when cooperation is replacing defection. While the model does not include explicit punishment mechanisms (such as costly punishment), it analyzes a form of contingent cooperation that functions as a sanctioning mechanism (withholding cooperation from defectors). The findings suggest that, for predicting efficiency in games with repeated interaction and contingent cooperation, allowing some tolerance for defection (generosity) can increase the likelihood of efficient cooperative outcomes, especially in the emergence phase. However, the model does not address explicit costly punishment or its direct efficiency effects, so its relevance to games with explicit punishment is adjacent rather than exact.

- source: Hauert, C. (2010). Replicator dynamics of reward & reputation in public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the opportunity to reward contributors (with or without reputation) can encourage the emergence of cooperation if rewards are sufficiently appealing and visible (via reputation). However, reward alone cannot stabilize cooperation, as second-order free-riders (who cooperate but do not reward) undermine the system, leading to cycles of cooperation and defection. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and explicit payoff calculations for different strategy types. The paper contrasts these findings with the literature on punishment, noting that punishment can stabilize cooperation but is less effective at establishing it. Efficiency (average population payoff) is analyzed as a function of the strategic composition of the population, but the paper does not provide direct results for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the mechanisms by which reward and reputation affect efficiency and cooperation in public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but its discussion of the contrasting roles of reward and punishment can inform predictions: reward can increase cooperation and thus efficiency in the short term, but cannot maintain high efficiency due to second-order free-riding. Punishment, as discussed from prior literature, can stabilize high efficiency once cooperation is established. The explicit payoff equations and analysis of group size, reward cost/benefit, and reputation can inform parameterization of models, but direct quantitative predictions for punishment effects are not provided.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Gavrilets, S., Qin, C. Z., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). Kinship can hinder cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods and multi-player PD games with heterogeneous cost-benefit ratios, increasing genetic relatedness does not always increase cooperation or efficiency. For low relatedness, direct reciprocity (punishment via GRIM) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency. For high relatedness, cooperation is a dominant strategy and efficiency is high. However, for intermediate relatedness, some individuals become unconditional cooperators (AllC), which undermines the effectiveness of punishment and allows others to defect (AllD), making full cooperation and maximal efficiency unsustainable. This results in a non-monotonic, U-shaped relationship between relatedness and efficiency. The findings are supported by both subgame perfect equilibrium analysis and agent-based evolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated public goods games with heterogeneity in cost-benefit ratios, the effect of mechanisms analogous to punishment (i.e., direct reciprocity via GRIM) on efficiency depends critically on the population structure and relatedness. When relatedness is low, punishment can sustain high efficiency; when relatedness is high, cooperation is self-sustaining. However, at intermediate relatedness, the threat of punishment is undermined, and efficiency drops. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity and the degree of relatedness (or analogous parameters) can cause non-monotonic effects of punishment on efficiency, and that punishment is most effective at low relatedness. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated PGG-like games with heterogeneity, but less so for one-shot games or games with explicit costly punishment mechanisms.

- source: Zschache, J. (2012). Producing public goods in networks: Some effects of social comparison and endogenous network change. *Social Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on networks, the ability of agents to change their social ties in response to social comparison (i.e., to seek out like-minded cooperators and avoid defectors) can sustain or increase cooperation and thus the production of public goods. Low network density and low costs of network change (low cohesion) allow cooperators to persist by forming supportive subgroups, leading to higher efficiency. High initial cooperation and low segregation also promote full cooperation. When network change is costly (high cohesion), cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model shows that both forward-looking and backward-looking (adaptive) agents can achieve high efficiency under favorable network conditions, but backward-looking agents require even more favorable starting conditions. Explicit costly punishment is not modeled; instead, social comparison provides a form of endogenous, non-costly social pressure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that endogenous network change and social comparison can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game designs allowing for dynamic partner selection or network rewiring (low cohesion/costs) will see higher efficiency, especially when initial cooperation is high and the network is not highly segregated. However, the absence of explicit costly punishment means the results are most applicable to settings where social comparison or endogenous social pressure is the main incentive, not where formal punishment is enabled. The results are directly informative for predicting efficiency in networked PGGs with endogenous tie change, but less so for classic PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2019). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in the public goods game with pool exclusion strategies. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper shows that introducing prosocial pool exclusion into a PGG leads to higher average payoffs and can stabilize cooperation through periodic oscillations or fixation to full cooperation, depending on whether second-order exclusion is present. Antisocial exclusion can destabilize cooperation but does not fully prevent the effectiveness of prosocial exclusion. Compared to punishment, prosocial pool exclusion is a more robust and effective mechanism for sustaining high efficiency in the population, even in the presence of antisocial strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in infinite well-mixed populations, enabling a prosocial pool exclusion mechanism (where excluders pay a fixed cost to exclude defectors from the public good) increases group efficiency relative to the standard PGG without exclusion. The effect is robust to the presence of antisocial exclusion, and even more pronounced when second-order exclusion is allowed. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a pool exclusion mechanism should be expected to yield higher efficiency than punishment, especially when exclusion is institutionalized and second-order exclusion is possible. However, the results are derived from deterministic replicator dynamics and may not directly translate to finite or experimental settings.

- source: Bergstrom, T. C. (2002). Evolution of social behavior: Individual and group selection. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in linear public goods games (multiplayer prisoners' dilemmas), cooperation cannot be sustained in equilibrium under nonassortative (random) matching and individual selection; only defectors survive, leading to low group efficiency. However, cooperation and higher group payoffs can be sustained if (a) matching is assortative (e.g., kin selection, spatial structure, partner choice), (b) the game is not a prisoners' dilemma (e.g., stag hunt), or (c) the game is repeated and social norms (including punishment) are enforced. The paper discusses the theoretical role of punishment in repeated games as a means to sustain cooperation and efficient outcomes, but does not provide direct analysis or predictions about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical context for the importance of game structure (e.g., matching, repetition, group size, payoff parameters) in determining whether cooperation and high efficiency can be sustained in public goods games. It highlights that punishment can, in theory, support efficient cooperation in repeated games, but does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about the magnitude or direction of punishment's effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of repeated interaction and enforceable punishment norms are key moderators, but does not provide parameter-level guidance for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in specific game designs.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation in costly institutions exhibits Red Queen and Black Queen dynamics in heterogeneous public goods. *Communications Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that introducing a costly institution (with a participation fee) alongside a free institution in a public goods game can increase cooperation and group efficiency. For low enhancement factors (low profitability), only the costly institution supports cooperation, and average payoffs remain close to the entrance cost (i.e., not much higher than the baseline). For higher enhancement factors, cooperation emerges in both institutions, and group payoffs increase above zero. The costly institution is more likely to be populated by cooperators, as defectors avoid the entrance cost unless there are enough cooperators to exploit. The model also shows that the effect of the entrance cost is context-dependent: it is beneficial for cooperation and efficiency at low profitability, but can be detrimental at high profitability if the cost is too high, as it reduces participation. The results hold in both well-mixed and structured populations, though spatial structure removes some bistability and further supports cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that introducing a costly institution (entrance fee) in a public goods game can increase efficiency and group payoff, especially when the baseline profitability (mpcr/enhancement factor) is low. The entrance cost acts as a filter, keeping defectors out and allowing cooperators to achieve higher payoffs. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly institution (or, by analogy, a punishment mechanism with a cost to participate) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such cost, particularly when the marginal per-capita return is low. However, if the cost is too high and the game is already highly profitable, the effect may reverse. The model does not include explicit punishment, but the costly institution's effect is adjacent to punishment in that it deters free-riding through a cost barrier. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where institutional participation costs or costly precommitment mechanisms are present, rather than direct peer punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Wang, T. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games under a risk-transfer mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a risk-transfer (coalition) mechanism into a spatial public goods game can enhance or reduce cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient (r) and the coalition closeness (alpha). In two-strategy systems, coalitional cooperators are more competitive than traditional cooperators, and coalitional defectors are less competitive than traditional defectors. In four-strategy systems, increasing coalition closeness (alpha) generally increases cooperation, but can also lead to non-monotonic effects where too strong coalitions reduce cooperation due to the breakdown of indirect territorial competition. The mechanism acts as a wealth redistribution system, strengthening cooperators at the boundary of clusters but sometimes making them vulnerable to invasion by other types. The model does not include punishment or reward, but the coalition mechanism is compared to these in the discussion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides informative theoretical evidence about how payoff-redistribution mechanisms (such as coalitions or risk-sharing) can affect group efficiency and cooperation in spatial PGGs. The findings suggest that mechanisms which redistribute payoffs among players can have complex, sometimes non-monotonic effects on efficiency, depending on the structure of the game (e.g., synergy coefficient, spatial structure, coalition closeness). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of any payoff-redistribution mechanism (including punishment, if it were present) may depend strongly on the details of the mechanism and the spatial or network structure. However, direct quantitative prediction of punishment effects is not possible from this paper.

- source: Suzuki, S., & Akiyama, E. (2007). Evolution of indirect reciprocity in groups of various sizes and comparison with direct reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-person public goods games with indirect reciprocity, both cooperative (discriminating) and defective strategies can be evolutionarily stable, but the threshold for cooperation increases sharply with group size. As group size increases, it becomes much harder for cooperation to evolve and persist, leading to lower expected group efficiency/payoff. The reputation mechanism (standing vs. image scoring) is a strong moderator: standing allows for cooperation to be stable under a wider range of parameters. The presence of implementation errors (failures to cooperate) further undermines the stability of cooperation. When unconditional cooperators are present, cooperation is only stable for group sizes greater than two and under certain parameter ranges. The results are derived analytically from payoff and replicator dynamics equations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing group size in public goods games with indirect reciprocity (and no explicit punishment mechanism) makes it much harder for cooperation and high efficiency to evolve. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in large groups, the baseline efficiency is likely to be low, and the marginal effect of punishment may depend on whether it can overcome the group size barrier. The paper also highlights the importance of the reputation mechanism: if the punishment or sanctioning system is more like 'standing' (justified punishment does not harm reputation), cooperation is more likely to be stable. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, error rates, and reputation mechanisms, but do not directly model explicit costly punishment as a game option.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2016). Competition of tolerant strategies in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing diverse tolerant strategies into the spatial public goods game can significantly increase group efficiency, sometimes leading to defector-free, fully cooperative states. The effect depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of monitoring (gamma), and the system size. In structured populations, coexistence of multiple tolerant strategies can create synergistic effects that outperform single-strategy or uniform-tolerance scenarios. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing which strategy combinations are stable under different parameter regimes. The main efficiency gains occur when tolerant strategies with different thresholds form alliances, crowding out defectors and maximizing group payoff. The results highlight the importance of diversity in tolerance and the structure of interactions for achieving high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the introduction of conditional strategies (tolerant players) that abstain from cooperation when defection is high can substantially increase efficiency, especially when multiple tolerance thresholds are present. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of such conditional strategies (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be as or more effective than standard punishment in raising efficiency, particularly in structured populations. The results are most relevant for games with spatial structure, optional participation, and where monitoring costs are not prohibitive. The paper does not directly manipulate or analyze explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, so its direct applicability to punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled prediction is limited, but it provides a strong adjacent signal about the power of conditional sanctioning and abstention. For prediction, the key moderators are the synergy factor (mpcr), the cost of monitoring (analogous to punishment cost), and the structure of the population.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhang, C. Y., & Cao, M. (2015). How insurance affects altruistic provision in threshold public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing insurance for contributors in threshold public goods games increases the provision of the public good and can improve the efficiency (group payoff) of the system. The effect is strongest when the insurance compensation is high and the required threshold for provision is high. The model shows that insurance can shift the evolutionary dynamics to favor contributors over defectors, depending on parameter values. The allocation rule for surplus contributions (parameter omega) also moderates the effect. The paper does not analyze punishment or sanctions, but provides a theoretical basis for how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can promote cooperation and efficiency in threshold public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that risk-mitigation mechanisms (insurance) for contributors can increase efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the risk of loss is high and insurance compensation is generous. While it does not study punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in games where contributors face risk and can be protected from loss. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, adding insurance or similar mechanisms can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The model is most applicable to threshold public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, no communication, and no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of the threshold, insurance compensation, and allocation rules for surplus contributions.

- source: Kristensen, N. P., Chisholm, R. A., & Ohtsuki, H. (2025). Many-strategy games in groups with relatives and the evolution of coordinated cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that kin selection (homophily) can facilitate the initial invasion of coordinated cooperation in public goods games with nonlinear (sigmoidal) benefits, even when such cooperation cannot invade in a well-mixed population due to cognitive or coordination costs. Once established, coordinated cooperation can persist as homophily declines, provided that the benefit function is sufficiently nonlinear and the switching gain from non-contribution to contribution at the coordination point exceeds the cost. If this condition is not met, some level of homophily must be maintained to prevent the invasion of defectors or 'liars' (who commit to contribute but do not follow through). The framework provides analytical and numerical tools for predicting evolutionary outcomes (including efficiency and group payoff) as a function of group size, relatedness, benefit function nonlinearity, and strategy set. The results are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but do not address punishment or sanctions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a general theoretical framework for predicting efficiency and group payoff in public goods games with arbitrary group size, number of strategies, and non-additive (e.g., sigmoidal) benefit functions, under varying levels of kin structure (homophily). It is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR (via the benefit function), and group structure. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The framework could, in principle, be extended to include punishment as a discrete strategy, but this is not done in the present paper. Therefore, the paper is highly informative for predicting baseline efficiency in PGGs with kin structure and nonlinear payoffs, but provides only indirect or contextual support for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2014). The role of emotions in the maintenance of cooperative behaviors. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a stress-based cost for defectors in a public goods game can make cooperation evolutionarily stable if the stress intensity exceeds a calculable threshold. This threshold is lower for larger populations and higher public goods multipliers, and higher for larger group sizes. The model provides explicit formulas for the fixation probability of cooperation and the conditions under which cooperation can invade and dominate a population of defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency, as they show under what conditions the group can achieve higher payoffs through sustained cooperation, even in the absence of explicit external punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that internalized costs for defection (modeled as stress or moral emotions) can substitute for explicit punishment in promoting cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. While it does not study explicit punishment, it shows that any mechanism that imposes a cost on defection (whether internal or external) can shift the evolutionary dynamics toward cooperation, provided the cost is above a threshold determined by game parameters (group size, population size, and MPCR). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of any effective cost for defection—whether via punishment or internalized emotion—can increase efficiency, and that the required cost decreases with larger populations and higher MPCR. However, the model does not address explicit punishment technology, cost, or identification, so its direct applicability to those dimensions is limited.

- source: Hauert, C., De Monte, S., Hofbauer, J., & Sigmund, K. (2002). Volunteering as Red Queen mechanism for cooperation in public goods games. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, allowing voluntary participation (the option to become a 'loner' and receive a fixed outside payoff) leads to persistent coexistence and oscillations among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This mechanism can sustain cooperation even in large groups and without repeated interactions, memory, or identification of defectors. The average population payoff can, depending on parameters, be higher or lower than the loner's payoff. The paper contrasts this with punishment and reward mechanisms, which require identification of defectors. The findings are robust across well-mixed and spatially structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it focuses on the alternative mechanism of voluntary participation. However, it provides important context: it notes that punishment and reward can establish cooperation if defectors can be identified, but that voluntary participation can also sustain cooperation without identification or repeated interaction. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a voluntary participation (loner) option is a strong moderator of efficiency and cooperation, and that the absence of punishment does not necessarily doom cooperation if such an option exists. The paper does not provide parameterized predictions about punishment effects, so its direct value for predicting efficiency with/without punishment is limited.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Optional participation only provides a narrow scope for sustaining cooperation. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with optional participation, cooperation can only be sustained (and thus group efficiency improved) when nonparticipants have positive outside payoffs and do not harm the public good. The classic 'loner' model is a special case of this. If nonparticipants harm the public good or have negative outside payoffs, cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and thus does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings highlight the limitations of bottom-up, voluntary participation schemes for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. However, it is highly informative about the baseline efficiency and cooperation levels in optional participation (loner) variants of the public goods game, which are sometimes considered as alternatives to punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency in control conditions where only optional participation is available, but not the effect of enabling punishment. It also clarifies that optional participation alone only supports cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) under narrow conditions.

- source: Dong, Y. T., He, Z. X., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Constructive agents nullify the ability of destructive agents to foster cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, destructive agents (who harm others at a cost to themselves) can induce cyclic dominance among strategies (cooperation, defection, destruction), which can sustain cooperation and thus increase group efficiency relative to the baseline. However, when constructive agents (who benefit others at a cost to themselves) are introduced, they do not by themselves promote cooperation or efficiency. If both types are present, constructive agents can nullify the ability of destructive agents to sustain cooperation if their benefit exceeds the harm from destructive agents. The efficiency outcome thus depends on the relative magnitude of constructive and destructive effects. The paper provides explicit replicator dynamics and invasion analyses to support these findings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with additional positive or negative externalities (constructive or destructive agents), but only indirectly relevant for classic punishment-based games. The 'destructive agent' is adjacent to punishment: it is a non-participant who harms others, but not a targeted, strategic punishment mechanism. The efficiency effects of destructive agents are positive (via cyclic dominance) when their harm is moderate, but are nullified if constructive agents' benefits are larger. The findings suggest that the presence and magnitude of positive and negative externalities can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, because the model is theoretical and does not include classic peer punishment (where players pay to reduce the payoff of specific others in response to their actions), direct quantitative predictions for standard punishment treatments should be made with caution. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented as a non-strategic, non-targeted externality.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of spatial public goods games with insurance mechanisms. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that insurance mechanisms in spatial PGGs can both promote and inhibit cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient, insurance premium, coverage, and the structure of the population. At low synergy coefficients, insurance (especially when purchased by defectors) can sustain cooperation through self-organized cyclic states, increasing group efficiency relative to baseline defection. However, at high synergy coefficients or when insured cooperators are prevalent, insurance can hinder cooperation due to indirect territorial competition. When insurers are allowed to adapt their strategies (two-layer network), the cooperation-promoting effect of insurance is often reduced, as insurers may refuse to transact with defectors, limiting the spread of insurance and altering the evolutionary dynamics. The model provides detailed phase diagrams mapping these effects, with efficiency interpreted as the prevalence of cooperative strategies and the resulting group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how insurance-like risk management mechanisms (adjacent to punishment) affect efficiency in spatial PGGs. While not a direct study of punishment, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in games with sanctioning or risk-mitigation features. The results suggest that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency is highly sensitive to game parameters (e.g., synergy coefficient, insurance cost/benefit, network structure) and the ability of third parties (insurers) to adapt. For prediction tasks, the paper highlights the importance of considering not just the presence of a sanctioning or insurance mechanism, but also who can access it, its cost-effectiveness, and the possibility of coevolution with external agents. The evidence is most directly applicable to spatial, structured populations with risk-mitigation options, and less so to standard punishment in classic PGGs.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., & Lenaerts, T. (2017). Evolution of commitment and level of participation in public goods games. *Autonomous Agents and Multi-agent Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a commitment mechanism—where players can propose binding agreements before the PGG, with compensation for defection—substantially increases both cooperation and average payoffs, provided the cost of arranging commitment is not too high. The effect is robust across group sizes and compensation levels, as long as compensation is above a threshold. The optimal participation threshold (number of committers required) depends on the severity of the dilemma and the cost of commitment. The model shows that commitment mechanisms can transform the efficiency of the PGG, making group payoffs much closer to the fully cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling credible commitment mechanisms in PGG-like environments can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of arranging commitment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of a commitment mechanism (even if not a classic punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency, with the effect size moderated by the cost of commitment, the mpcr, and the participation threshold. The results are robust across group sizes and compensation levels (above a threshold). However, the model does not directly analyze classic punishment (costly peer punishment), so direct transfer to punishment-enabled PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2015). Benefits of tolerance in public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The presence of tolerant players, who monitor and respond to defection by switching to a loner strategy only when defection exceeds a threshold, can substantially increase the average payoff of the group in public goods games. This effect is strongest at intermediate levels of tolerance and is robust across both well-mixed and spatially structured populations. The model shows that neither unconditional cooperation nor immediate withdrawal (loner) is optimal; instead, a moderate tolerance threshold allows the group to maintain high efficiency, even under conditions where cooperation would otherwise be unsustainable. The paper does not include punishment or reward as explicit mechanisms, but positions tolerance as an alternative to these approaches.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games, the introduction of a tolerance-based strategy (where players cooperate unless defection exceeds a threshold) can dramatically improve group efficiency compared to standard models with only cooperators, defectors, and loners. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms allowing conditional withdrawal (tolerance) can substitute for or outperform punishment in raising efficiency, especially when the cost of monitoring is not too high and the tolerance threshold is moderate. However, because the paper does not include explicit punishment or reward treatments, its direct applicability to predicting the effect of punishment is limited. Instead, it provides a benchmark for the potential of alternative mechanisms to improve efficiency.

- source: Kang, H. W., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., Huang, Z. Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). From eligibility to suitability: Regulation and restriction of reputation-based access system on free-riding behavior in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based exclusion mechanisms, which filter out low-reputation (likely free-riding) individuals before the PGG round, can significantly increase cooperation and total system payoff in spatial public goods games. The homogeneous exclusion model requires higher exclusion probability to match the performance of traditional (post-game) exclusion, but both converge to high cooperation and payoff at high exclusion probability. The heterogeneous exclusion model, which tailors exclusion probability to the reputation gap, outperforms both the homogeneous and traditional models in promoting cooperation and system payoff at high exclusion intensity. Both reputation-based models are especially effective at suppressing second-order free-riding, a limitation of traditional exclusion. The models are simulated on a 100x100 lattice with three strategies (C, D, EC), and results are robust across a range of synergy factors and exclusion costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that pre-game, reputation-based exclusion mechanisms (a form of access control) can increase group payoff and cooperation in spatial PGGs, especially when exclusion is sufficiently strict. The findings suggest that, for games with spatial structure and reputation tracking, enabling such exclusion mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency relative to control (no exclusion or punishment). The effect is stronger for the heterogeneous (adaptive) exclusion model. The results are most directly applicable to spatial PGGs with continuous contribution, reputation tracking, and exclusion as the main sanctioning mechanism. The paper does not study classic costly punishment (where players pay to reduce others' payoffs post-game), so direct transfer to standard punishment-enabled PGGs should be cautious. However, the results are highly informative for predicting the effect of exclusion-based access control on efficiency, especially in spatial/reputation contexts.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that linkage strategies, which exclude defectors from indirect social exchanges (ISEs), can maintain high levels of cooperation and group payoff in n-person social dilemmas, even in the presence of perception and implementation errors. Among the norms tested, the Kandori norm is the most robust: linkage strategies using Kandori maintain a payoff advantage over non-linkage strategies and are not invaded by defectors or non-linkage cooperators, even when errors are present. This means that, in agent-based simulations, the average group payoff (efficiency) is higher and more stable with Kandori-based linkage than with other norms or with no linkage. The results generalize across different ISE game structures (giving game vs. mutual aid game). The findings suggest that costless exclusion (linkage) can substitute for costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency, provided the right norm is used.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public-goods-like environments, costless exclusion of defectors from indirect social exchanges (linkage) can achieve and sustain high efficiency, especially when the Kandori norm is used for reputation. The effect is robust to perception and implementation errors, which often undermine other mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling a Kandori-based linkage mechanism (a form of costless, reputation-based exclusion) will increase efficiency relative to control (no linkage or no punishment), and that the effect is robust to errors and does not require costly punishment. The findings are most directly relevant to settings with large groups, repeated interactions, and full information about others' histories. The results are less directly applicable to standard costly punishment treatments, but they show that costless, norm-based exclusion can be a powerful alternative for sustaining efficiency.

- source: Chiba-Okabe, H., & Plotkin, J. B. (2024). Can institutions foster cooperation by wealth redistribution?. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) A prosocial institution (maximizing average population payoff) can increase cooperation and efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently forward-looking (low discount rate); if it is myopic, it fails to intervene and the outcome is the same as anarchy. (2) A pro-cooperator institution (maximizing cooperator payoff) always increases cooperation and efficiency above anarchy, even if it is myopic. (3) A selfish institution (maximizing its own revenue) can sometimes increase efficiency above anarchy if it is forward-looking, but if it is myopic, it exploits the population and reduces efficiency below anarchy. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for when each regime leads to higher or lower efficiency than the no-institution baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of institutional interventions (taxation and redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the effect of 'punishment' or institutional intervention is not universally positive or negative for efficiency; rather, it depends on the institution's objective and its ability to value future outcomes (discount rate). For prediction tasks, the model suggests that institutional efficiency (analogous to punishment/reward effectiveness), the institution's foresight, and the alignment of institutional objectives with group welfare are critical moderators. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the 'punishment' is implemented as a tax-and-redistribute mechanism, rather than direct costly punishment between individuals.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Uchida, S. (2014). Rewards and the evolution of cooperation in public good games. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, voluntary reward funds can evolve and lead to efficient cooperation, overcoming coordination problems that typically prevent the evolution of cooperation in large, anonymous groups. The model shows that, regardless of initial conditions, rewarders can invade a population of defectors if the reward fund's return is sufficiently high, leading to a cascade where cooperators eventually dominate. The resulting group payoff and average fitness are higher with rewards than without, and the system can reach full cooperation or stable mixed equilibria depending on the risk and threshold parameters. The paper contrasts this with institutional punishment, which is argued to be less effective or more difficult to initiate in large groups.
