  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a reward mechanism in threshold public good games will increase group efficiency and average payoff, especially in settings where coordination is otherwise difficult. While it does not directly analyze punishment, it offers indirect evidence that reward mechanisms can be more effective than punishment in some coordination contexts. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient effectiveness and sustainability) should increase efficiency relative to control. The model's explicit treatment of group size, threshold, risk, and reward parameters can inform how these dimensions moderate the effect of rewards on efficiency.

- source: Pal, S., & Hilbe, C. (2022). Reputation effects drive the joint evolution of cooperation and social rewarding. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputation effects are crucial for the evolution of cooperation and social rewarding in PGG-like environments. When individuals can observe or infer others' rewarding strategies (i.e., reputation is possible), social rewarding and cooperation co-evolve and are stable, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. Without reputation, cooperation and rewarding do not emerge, and antisocial rewarding may arise under certain conditions (e.g., high assortment or mutually beneficial rewards). In multiplayer (PGG) settings, similar results hold: reputation enables stable cooperation and social rewarding, while its absence leads to defection and low efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation and rewarding are payoff-maximizing, and shows that the presence of reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling reputation effects (e.g., making rewarding behavior observable) in PGG-like games will increase efficiency when social rewarding is possible. The model directly links game design dimensions such as group size, marginal per-capita return, reward cost and effectiveness, and information structure (reputation) to the emergence of efficient cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of observable rewarding (reputation), sufficiently high reward effectiveness relative to cost, and the ability to target rewards to cooperators are critical for efficiency gains from reward mechanisms. The absence of reputation or the presence of antisocial rewarding can undermine efficiency. While the paper does not model punishment directly, it provides indirect evidence that the structure and observability of incentives (including punishment, by analogy) are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Skarzhinskaya, Y. M., & Tsurikov, V. I. (2021). Organizational change: from community to firm. *Rossiiskii Zhurnal Menedzhmenta-russian Management Journal*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper develops mathematical models of a self-governed collective where individual effort generates aggregate income, distributed by pre-negotiated shares. In the baseline, self-interested behavior leads to an inefficient Nash equilibrium. When a coalition forms and coordinates effort, the coalition's and its members' payoffs increase. If the coalition offers contracts to non-members (who then forego their share in exchange for a reward per effort), the organization transforms into a hierarchical structure, and under certain conditions, all agents' payoffs increase, reaching the communal optimum. The paper provides theorems showing that expanding the coalition always increases the sum of payoffs for coalition members and the new joiner, and can also increase payoffs for non-coalition members. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with no empirical or experimental results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms enabling coordination, selective incentives, or contracts (analogous to institutionalized punishment or reward) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not model explicit punishment, it analyzes how selective incentives and contracts can overcome inefficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing mechanisms that align individual and group incentives (such as contracts or selective rewards) can increase group efficiency, even in the absence of explicit punishment. The findings are most relevant for settings where coalition formation or contracting is possible, and less directly informative for standard PGGs with simple punishment options.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2019). Three-player repeated games with an opt-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a three-player repeated public goods game with an opt-out option (no explicit punishment), both tolerant and intolerant cooperators can survive, but their coexistence is not an evolutionary outcome. The equilibrium frequency of cooperators (and thus average payoff) increases with the benefit-to-cost ratio and decreases with the probability of group dissolution. The model shows that (partially) cooperative stable equilibria can exist, but the all-defector equilibrium is always asymptotically stable, making the initial evolution of cooperation difficult without additional mechanisms. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding the effect of voluntary partner refusal (opt-out) on cooperation and efficiency in small-group public goods games, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. It provides analytic results on how group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and group dissolution probability affect equilibrium cooperation and payoffs. However, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present. Its results may be useful as a baseline for comparison to punishment-enabled games, especially regarding the efficiency achievable through partner choice alone.

- source: Ohtsuki, H. (2004). Reactive strategies in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity settings with public-goods-game-type payoffs, discriminating strategies (which conditionally cooperate based on others' past behavior, akin to punishment of defectors) are not stable evolutionary outcomes. Instead, populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation (All C) or defection (All D), depending on whether the expected benefit of cooperation (modulated by information transfer and continuation probability) exceeds the cost. Discriminators can promote cooperation transiently by increasing the average 'discriminatoriness' in the population, but are ultimately outcompeted by more generous strategies. The paper also shows that errors (in perception or implementation) further destabilize discriminating strategies, making unconditional cooperation or defection more likely. The main outcomes are expressed in terms of average payoff (efficiency), and the model provides explicit threshold conditions for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-game-like environments with indirect reciprocity, strategies that punish defectors (by refusing to cooperate with them) do not lead to stable increases in group efficiency or payoff. Instead, such strategies are only transient promoters of cooperation, and populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation or defection depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio and information transfer. The model does not include explicit costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments, so its direct predictive value for the effect of enabling punishment in a PGG is limited. However, it suggests that punishment-like strategies may not robustly increase efficiency unless additional mechanisms (e.g., standing, justified punishment) are present. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that simply enabling punishment (in the form of conditional cooperation) may not reliably increase efficiency, especially in the presence of errors.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Strategy intervention in spatial voluntary public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This simulation study finds that a centralized intervention mechanism (distinct from peer or pool punishment) can increase the average total payoff of the group and reduce income inequality, provided the intervention is sufficiently strong (i.e., the product of intervention degree and intensity exceeds a threshold). Weak intervention can actually reduce cooperation and group payoff compared to no intervention. The model provides explicit parameter ranges for when full cooperation and maximum efficiency are achieved. The intervention cost is shared by all, and the mechanism is implemented by a central authority, not by individuals punishing each other.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that centralized, institutionally-enforced interventions (akin to pool punishment but implemented as forced contributions rather than direct payoff reduction) can increase efficiency in spatial voluntary PGGs, but only if the intervention is strong enough. The effect is non-monotonic: weak intervention can be counterproductive. The findings are most relevant for settings with voluntary participation, spatial structure, and centralized enforcement. The explicit mapping between intervention parameters and efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of institutional punishment-like mechanisms, especially when the control (no intervention) efficiency is low. However, the mechanism is not standard punishment, so direct transfer to peer punishment settings should be cautious.

- source: Nyborg, K. (2018). Reciprocal climate negotiators. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that reciprocal preferences can, under certain conditions (strong and widespread reciprocity), stabilize the grand coalition in a public-goods-like coalition game, leading to high group welfare (efficiency). If only a minority of countries are reciprocal, or if reciprocity is weak, the only stable coalitions are small and provide little welfare improvement over the non-cooperative baseline. The model demonstrates that the presence of reciprocal preferences can shift the game from a pure Prisoner's Dilemma to a coordination game in utilities, but this does not guarantee high efficiency unless reciprocity is strong and common. The paper does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning institutions, but reciprocity can act as a form of endogenous social sanctioning. The welfare/efficiency effects of reciprocity are thus mixed: potentially large if conditions are right, but often minimal in realistic parameter ranges.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that social preferences (reciprocity) can, in principle, increase efficiency in public-goods-like coalition games, but only if they are strong and widespread. In the absence of explicit punishment mechanisms, reciprocity alone is not a reliable path to high efficiency unless the population is highly reciprocal. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling social preference mechanisms (like reciprocity) may only improve efficiency if the relevant parameters (strength and prevalence of reciprocity) are high. The model does not address explicit punishment, so its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled games is limited, but it does show that endogenous social preferences can sometimes substitute for formal sanctions in supporting cooperation and efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented via social preferences rather than institutionalized mechanisms.

- source: Castro, L., & Toro, M. A. (2008). Iterated prisoner's dilemma in an asocial world dominated by loners, not by defectors. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated N-person Prisoner's Dilemma games, the presence of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary opt-out for a fixed payoff) changes the evolutionary dynamics: loners can displace defectors, and conditional cooperators (TFT0) can invade a population of loners if the benefit of cooperation is high enough and the number of repeated interactions is sufficient. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be established and maintained in sizable groups without the need for explicit punishment, provided that individuals can opt out. The paper does not model or test punishment directly, but discusses its potential role as an additional mechanism for sustaining cooperation in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that voluntary participation (the loner strategy) can enable high-efficiency outcomes in repeated public-goods-like games, even without punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, as punishment is not modeled. The findings suggest that in environments where voluntary participation is possible, the need for punishment to sustain cooperation may be reduced. For prediction, this paper is most informative about the baseline efficiency in control games with voluntary participation, but not about the incremental effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2023). Evolution of spite in an n-player game with an opting-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player games with an opting-out option, spiteful (costly harming) strategies can sometimes persist, but only under restrictive conditions: low cost-to-harm ratio, small group size, and specific opting-out rules. As group size increases, it becomes harder for spiteful strategies to achieve higher payoffs than nonspiteful strategies, and the basin of attraction for all-nonspiteful populations grows. The opting-out rule (how easily groups disband) is a key moderator: when homogeneous groups (all spiteful or all nonspiteful) are forced to disband, spite can persist more easily, but this is a somewhat artificial rule. The model is mathematically explicit about when spiteful strategies can dominate, and these conditions almost always correspond to lower group efficiency compared to all-nonspiteful populations. The analysis is directly about payoff and efficiency, not just behavior.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like n-player games with an opting-out mechanism, the introduction of costly punishment (here, spiteful harming) generally reduces group efficiency unless the cost-to-harm ratio is extremely low and the group size is small. As group size increases, the conditions for punishment/spite to persist become more restrictive, and group efficiency is more likely to be maximized when punishment/spite is absent. The details of the opting-out rule (e.g., whether homogeneous groups are forced to disband) can moderate this effect, but in most realistic settings, punishment/spite is not efficiency-enhancing. The model is not an exact PGG with standard punishment, but the payoff structure and evolutionary logic are closely related, and the results are directly about payoff/efficiency. Thus, the paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment/spite in large groups with standard cost structures will not increase efficiency, and may reduce it.

- source: Fontanari, J. F., & Santos, M. (2024). Solving the prisoner's dilemma trap in Hamilton's model of temporarily formed random groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the 'others-only' n-person prisoner's dilemma (a strong altruism scenario), cooperation cannot evolve under asexual reproduction or sexual reproduction with selfing, but can evolve and become stable under biparental sexual reproduction if the group benefit multiplier (r) is sufficiently large relative to group size (n) and the cost of cooperation (c) is not too high. The model does not include punishment or reward, and the main mechanism enabling cooperation is the genetic structure imposed by biparental reproduction. The main outcome is the probability that the population reaches the all-cooperator state (fixation probability), which is a direct measure of group efficiency or welfare. The results are summarized in phase diagrams showing regions where cooperation is stable, bistable, or unstable, as a function of c, r, and n. The findings are robust to demographic noise and extend to dioecious populations. The paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment, communication, or other institutional design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments that lack punishment or reward mechanisms, especially when the game structure is the 'others-only' variant of the n-person prisoner's dilemma. It shows that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (measured as fixation of cooperation and group payoff) is only achievable under specific genetic and demographic conditions—namely, biparental sexual reproduction, sufficiently high group benefit multiplier (r), and not-too-large group size (n) or cost (c). The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is generally low unless these conditions are met. However, the paper does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency difference between control and punishment-enabled conditions. Its main value is in establishing a baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is generally low except under the specified genetic/demographic conditions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment.

- source: Sugaya, T., & Wolitzky, A. (2023). Bad apples in symmetric repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in large repeated public goods or similar games, the possibility of sustaining cooperation and high efficiency depends critically on the ability to punish defectors specifically. If the only available incentives are rewards or withdrawal of provision (i.e., voluntary mechanisms), then cooperation collapses and efficiency is low when there are even a few commitment types (bad apples) who always defect. Involuntary punishment (such as fines) that can be targeted at defectors restores the possibility of cooperation and high efficiency. The main technical result is that if a 'population dominant' action exists (i.e., defecting yields a higher payoff than the population average, regardless of others' actions), then equilibrium efficiency converges to the inefficient outcome as the population grows. If no such action exists, and punishment can be targeted, then high efficiency is possible. The results are general and apply to a wide class of symmetric repeated games, including public goods games with or without transfers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods and related games. It implies that enabling targeted, involuntary punishment (e.g., fines for non-contributors) is necessary for sustaining high efficiency in large groups with possible commitment types. If only rewards or voluntary mechanisms are available, efficiency will be low in the presence of even a small fraction of defectors. The paper's results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is symmetric, the population is large, and there is a possibility of commitment types. For the prediction task, the presence or absence of targeted punishment (punishment_tech), the nature of the incentive (reward_exists, punishment_cost), and the ability to distinguish defectors are critical moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) will be low in large groups with commitment types unless involuntary punishment is available; enabling such punishment can dramatically increase efficiency.

- source: Schonmann, R. H., & Boyd, R. (2016). A simple rule for the evolution of contingent cooperation in large groups. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that contingent cooperation in large groups can invade and persist if relatedness is above a threshold that depends sensitively on the threshold fraction of cooperators required for cooperation to be sustained. The required relatedness increases rapidly as the threshold for cooperation increases. The number of repeated interactions (iterations) required for cooperation to invade also increases rapidly with the threshold and decreases with relatedness. The model shows that, under realistic levels of relatedness, only strategies that require cooperation from a small fraction of the group can invade; strategies that require near-universal cooperation are unlikely to evolve. The paper argues that contingent punishment (not formally modeled here) is a more plausible mechanism for the evolution of widespread cooperation, because even a small fraction of punishers can induce high levels of cooperation. The results are supported by mathematical derivations and numerical simulations of evolutionary dynamics in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, relatedness, and the threshold for contingent cooperation affect the evolutionary success (and thus efficiency) of cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It does not directly model or analyze punishment, but its results suggest that punishment-based mechanisms are more likely to support high efficiency in large groups than pure contingent cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper's formulas can be used to estimate the conditions under which cooperation (and thus high efficiency) can be sustained, given the group structure and behavioral rules. However, because the paper does not model explicit punishment or compare efficiency with and without punishment, it provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Ember, C. R., Gelfand, M. J., Jones, E. C., Skoggard, I., Toutee, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2024). Cultural tightness and resilience against environmental shocks in nonindustrial societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that group efficiency (average payoff) and cooperation are maximized at intermediate levels of cultural tightness when facing severe environmental shocks. Loose cultures (low tightness) are more resilient and efficient under mild, slow-onset shocks, while very tight cultures often fail to adapt to new shocks and experience lower efficiency. However, very tight cultures can maintain high efficiency in the face of regular, severe shocks if cooperative norms are already established. Increasing group size undermines cooperation and efficiency unless benefit parameters scale accordingly. Synergistic effects between different types of collective action (public goods) enhance both cooperation and efficiency. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning, but norm compliance is enforced via conformity in the utility function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with norm-based conformity (but no explicit punishment), efficiency is maximized at intermediate levels of norm strength (tightness) under severe shocks, and at low tightness under mild shocks. The absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled, but the findings are highly informative for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, benefit structure, and norm strength. The model's structure and results are most relevant for public goods games with strong norm compliance or conformity-based incentives, and less so for games where punishment is the main enforcement mechanism. The model also shows that increasing group size reduces efficiency unless benefits scale up, and that synergy between collective actions increases the range of conditions for high efficiency.

- source: Foster, K. R. (2004). Diminishing returns in social evolution: the not-so-tragic commons. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes mathematical models of social evolution in group-structured populations, focusing on how the functional form of returns to investment (linear vs. diminishing) affects group performance (efficiency) in the face of selfish competition. It finds that, under diminishing returns, the negative impact of competition on group efficiency is greatly reduced, and group performance can be close to the optimum even with low relatedness and no explicit enforcement. The paper provides examples from social vertebrates, insects, and microbes, arguing that diminishing returns are common in real biological systems. Explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are not modeled, but enforcement is discussed as one of several mechanisms that can limit the tragedy of the commons.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of selfish competition in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the shape of the returns to investment. In prediction tasks, if the underlying game or biological system features diminishing returns to cooperation or competition, the efficiency loss from selfish behavior (and by extension, the potential efficiency gain from adding punishment) may be much smaller than predicted by linear models. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the paper does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of enabling punishment in a PGG. It does, however, suggest that in environments with diminishing returns, the baseline efficiency may already be high, potentially reducing the incremental value of punishment.

- source: Heintzelman, M. D., Salant, S. W., & Schott, S. (2009). Putting free-riding to work: A Partnership Solution to the common-property problem. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals form partnerships that share revenue equally, the resulting free-riding reduces excessive effort in common-property problems, potentially achieving the social optimum in aggregate payoff. The number of partnerships is a key design parameter: too many partnerships (everyone solo) leads to excessive effort, while too few (everyone in one group) leads to under-provision. An intermediate, endogenously determined number of partnerships can induce the efficient outcome. The model shows that this solution is stable under certain conditions (e.g., when solo production is sufficiently disadvantageous). The Partnership Solution can outperform standard regulatory interventions in terms of participant welfare. The paper generalizes the result to various sharing rules and to cases with heterogeneous agents.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional design—specifically, the structure of group formation and revenue sharing—can have large, predictable effects on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not study punishment per se, it shows that endogenous free-riding within partnerships can substitute for external sanctions in curbing over-extraction and maximizing welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that efficiency can be increased by mechanisms that induce free-riding when the baseline is excessive effort, and that the number and size of groups (player_count, partnership structure) are critical moderators. However, the absence of explicit punishment means the results are most directly relevant to settings where group structure, not punishment, is the main intervention.

- source: Richter, A., & Grasman, J. (2013). The transmission of sustainable harvesting norms when agents are conditionally cooperative. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of conditional cooperators in a common-pool resource setting can lead to complex and sometimes abrupt transitions between cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria. Efficiency, measured as resource stock and group profit relative to the maximum economic yield, is highly sensitive to social dynamics. When temptation to defect increases or when group size increases, the system can shift from high-efficiency (cooperative) to low-efficiency (defective) states, sometimes with hysteresis or limit cycles. The model highlights that social norm enforcement via moral persuasion can sustain cooperation, but is vulnerable to collapse if conditional cooperators lose motivation. The absence of explicit punishment means that norm enforcement is 'soft' and collapses are possible even without changes in external parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like common-pool resource games, the efficiency of group outcomes is highly sensitive to the social composition (intrinsic vs. conditional cooperators vs. defectors) and to the strength of norm enforcement mechanisms. While it does not model explicit punishment, it suggests that in the absence of strong enforcement (e.g., costly punishment), efficiency is fragile and can collapse due to endogenous social dynamics. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that enabling or strengthening norm enforcement (potentially via punishment) could stabilize high-efficiency outcomes, especially in larger groups or when temptation to defect is high. However, the model also warns that even with some enforcement, efficiency can be lost if social dynamics shift. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is absent or weak, and thus provide a baseline for understanding the potential value of adding punishment.

- source: Richter, A., van Soest, D., & Grasman, J. (2013). Contagious cooperation, temptation, and ecosystem collapse. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a closed community harvesting a renewable resource, cooperation can be sustained through social norm contagion, but is vulnerable to collapse if the temptation to defect becomes too strong (e.g., due to increased population or technology). The system can flip from a high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibrium to a low-efficiency (defective) equilibrium, with substantial welfare loss. Hysteresis is present: reversing the parameter change that caused collapse is not sufficient to restore cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for welfare under cooperation and Nash equilibrium, showing large efficiency losses after collapse. The presence of social norm diffusion (but not explicit punishment) can sustain cooperation up to a tipping point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding the efficiency dynamics of common-pool resource games with social norm diffusion, but it does not model explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is maintained by social pressure, persuasion, or contagion, rather than by costly punishment. The model shows that efficiency can be high and robust up to a tipping point, after which collapse is sudden and efficiency is lost. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence about the role of population size, technology, and social norm strength in determining efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the effect of enabling or disabling punishment. It is best used as a theoretical baseline for environments without explicit punishment.

- source: Iwasa, Y., Uchida, T., & Yokomizo, H. (2007). Nonlinear behavior of the socio-economic dynamics for lake eutrophication control. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the interplay between social pressure (which increases with both the number of cooperators and the level of pollution) and individual cost-benefit decisions can lead to complex group-level outcomes. Depending on parameter values, the system can settle into a high-cooperation/clean-water equilibrium, a low-cooperation/polluted equilibrium, or exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. The model shows that increasing the effectiveness of pollution-reducing technology can paradoxically worsen water quality if it reduces the perceived need for cooperation. Conservatism (slower behavioral change) can stabilize cooperation and maintain higher group welfare. The model does not include explicit punishment, but social pressure is discussed as a possible proxy for reputational or sanctioning effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how social feedbacks and behavioral dynamics can affect group efficiency in public-goods-like settings. While it does not directly model punishment, it suggests that mechanisms increasing social pressure (which could include punishment, reputation, or social norms) can promote cooperation and improve group outcomes, but may also lead to multiple stable states or complex dynamics. The findings support the idea that the structure and strength of social incentives (including, potentially, punishment) are critical moderators of efficiency. However, the absence of explicit punishment or experimental manipulation limits the direct applicability of the results to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in empirical PGGs.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taylor, P. D. (2010). Joint evolution of multiple social traits: a kin selection analysis. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when social traits such as public good production and exploitation co-evolve, their interactions can lead to complex evolutionary dynamics, including bistability between efficient (social) and inefficient (asocial) outcomes. The efficiency of the group (measured as total payoff or welfare) depends on the joint equilibrium of these traits, which is sensitive to relatedness, the presence of independent public good production, and the nature of trait interactions (synergy or interference). In some cases, higher relatedness increases both cooperation and exploitation, with ambiguous effects on efficiency. The model highlights that multidimensional social dilemmas can have multiple stable outcomes, and small changes in parameters or initial conditions can lead to large differences in group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the design dimensions of a public goods game—especially those affecting trait interactions, relatedness, and the possibility of multiple social traits—can lead to non-linear and sometimes counterintuitive effects on group efficiency when punishment-like or enforcement traits are present. While the model does not directly analyze punishment in the standard PGG sense, it shows that enforcement or exploitation traits can interact with cooperation to produce bistability or runaway dynamics. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment or enforcement mechanisms may not always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the interaction structure, initial conditions, and whether the system is in a region of parameter space that supports social or asocial equilibria. The findings caution against simple extrapolation from one-dimensional models and highlight the importance of considering multidimensional trait interactions.

- source: Liu, Y. Y., Liu, L. J., Guo, R. Q., & Zhang, L. (2023). N-player repeated evolutionary trust game under government management. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a government-managed punishment and reward mechanism in a repeated N-player trust game can sustain trust and investment, leading to higher group payoffs. There is a critical threshold of punishment intensity: below it, untrustworthy behavior dominates and payoffs are low; above it, trust and investment are maintained, and payoffs increase. The model also shows that as punishment intensity increases further, the system can reach a stable coexistence of strategies with high trust and investment. The results are robust across different group sizes and parameter values, and are supported by both theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments with group structure, introducing or increasing the intensity of punishment (and associated rewards for reporting) can substantially increase group efficiency/payoff, provided the punishment is above a critical threshold. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so the qualitative prediction that punishment increases efficiency is likely to transfer to PGG-like settings, especially those with repeated interactions and third-party enforcement. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented by an external authority and is contingent on reporting.

- source: Antoci, A., & Zarri, L. (2015). Punish and perish?. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary success and efficiency impact of punishment in a prisoner's dilemma with explicit punishment and reward options depend on the type of strong reciprocity present. Altruistic punishment of defectors (SNR) can support cooperation and high average payoff, but only if punishers are sufficiently numerous; otherwise, defectors dominate and efficiency collapses. Altruistic rewarding (SPR) leads to persistent coexistence of types and fluctuating efficiency. Symmetric strong reciprocity (punishing defectors and rewarding cooperators) is maladaptive: it leads to the extinction of cooperators and punishers, resulting in universal defection and minimal efficiency. Punishing non-punishing cooperators (PNP) or both defectors and non-punishing cooperators (HSNR) can robustly enforce cooperation and maintain high efficiency, with the PNP strategy being especially robust. The presence of unconditional cooperators who do not punish can undermine the evolutionary stability of punishment-based cooperation. The model shows that the efficiency effect of punishment is not universally positive and can be negative or fragile depending on the sanctioning structure and population heterogeneity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public-goods-like environments is highly contingent on the details of the punishment and reward mechanisms and the behavioral heterogeneity of the population. In prediction tasks, the presence of explicit reward options, the type of punishment (who is punished), and the initial population composition are critical moderators. Punishment of defectors alone can increase efficiency if enough punishers are present, but this equilibrium is fragile. Adding reward can undermine the effectiveness of punishment and reduce efficiency. Punishing non-punishing cooperators (second-order free riders) is more robust in sustaining cooperation and efficiency. Thus, when predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, it is essential to consider not just the presence of punishment but also the structure of available sanctions and rewards, and the possibility of second-order free riding. The findings caution against assuming that enabling punishment will always increase efficiency; under some conditions, it can be maladaptive.

- source: Han, T. A. (2022). Institutional incentives for the evolution of committed cooperation: ensuring participation is as important as enhancing compliance. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional incentives (punishment or reward) can promote the evolution of cooperation and commitment compliance in a social dilemma with a prior commitment stage. Reward is generally more effective than punishment at promoting cooperation, especially when the cost of commitment is high or the benefit-to-cost ratio is large. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation in the commitment (not just compliance) further increases cooperation, particularly when participation is costly. The presence of noise in participation decisions can also stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are based on explicit payoff calculations and evolutionary stability analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in social dilemma games with a prior commitment stage, enabling punishment (or reward) increases group efficiency (cooperation frequency) compared to no incentives, provided the incentive budget is sufficient. Reward is more effective than punishment, especially as the cost of participation increases. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation (not just compliance) further increases efficiency, especially when participation is costly. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with a commitment or opt-in stage, and the model's explicit payoff calculations can be mapped to efficiency outcomes. However, the model is based on a two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a commitment stage, not a standard repeated public goods game, so predictions should be transferred with caution to multi-player, repeated settings.

- source: Janus, T., & Lim, J. J. (2009). Sticks and carrots: Two incentive mechanisms supporting intra-group cooperation. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper proves that in a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma, the introduction of either collective punishment (Nash reversion by all after any defection) or reward mechanisms (transfers contingent on cooperation) can sustain full cooperation as a subgame perfect equilibrium, even when bilateral incentive compatibility constraints are violated. This means that group efficiency (welfare) can be maintained at the cooperative level under broader conditions than in the absence of such mechanisms. The results are general and apply to settings where players interact repeatedly and can observe each other's actions, and where the game is either infinite or has an unknown end point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency by sustaining cooperation, even when cooperation would not be sustainable without such mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially collective punishment with Nash reversion) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, provided players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor) and can observe actions. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma), so the results are most directly applicable to small-group, repeated, fully observable settings. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes or parameterized predictions, but it does establish that the direction of effect is positive for efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Jia, S. H., & Wang, Z. Y. (2025). An evolutionary game model with reputation threshold and reputation score to promote trust in the sharing economy. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that implementing a dynamic, reputation-based reward and punishment mechanism in a trust game on a square lattice network increases the proportion of trustworthy participants and can increase total group revenue (efficiency) compared to a baseline without such mechanisms. The effect depends on the reputation threshold: moderate thresholds maximize both trust and total revenue, while very high thresholds reduce revenue due to excessive punishment. The model demonstrates that, for a wide range of initial conditions, appropriate punishment/reward settings can drive the system to full trust and higher efficiency. The results are robust to population size. The model is not a standard PGG but is adjacent, and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in networked trust games with dynamic reputation-based punishment and reward, enabling punishment (and reward) can increase group efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, provided the punishment/reward parameters are set appropriately. The effect is non-monotonic: too much punishment (high threshold) reduces efficiency, while moderate levels maximize it. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and calibration of punishment/reward mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency gains from sanctions. The model is adjacent to PGGs but the efficiency outcome is directly relevant.

- source: ELLISON, G. (1994). COOPERATION IN THE PRISONERS-DILEMMA WITH ANONYMOUS RANDOM MATCHING. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that contagious punishment strategies can sustain cooperation and near-efficient payoffs in large, anonymous, randomly-matched repeated prisoner's dilemma games. The equilibrium is robust to small amounts of noise (players making mistakes) and does not require excessive patience except in very large populations or when the gain from defection is very small. The efficiency loss due to noise can be made arbitrarily small as the noise probability decreases. The results hold even without public randomization devices, though the required patience may increase in some parameter regimes. The analysis provides explicit conditions (in terms of discount factor, population size, and gain from defection) under which efficient cooperation is possible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment mechanisms (here, contagious punishment) can enable high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even under severe informational constraints (anonymity, random matching, no identification, no communication). The results are most directly applicable to repeated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure and sufficient patience, enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency relative to the baseline (no punishment) case. The paper also provides explicit formulas and numerical examples for the minimum patience (discount factor) required for cooperation at different population sizes and temptation levels. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that punishment can enable near-full efficiency in repeated, anonymous, randomly-matched games, provided the population is not too large, the gain from defection is not too high, and players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to small noise and do not require public randomization, though the patience requirement may increase in those cases.

- source: Haag, M., & Lagunoff, R. (2006). Social norms, local interaction, and neighborhood planning. *International Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of repeated cooperation enforced by punishment depends critically on the network structure and the distribution of individual patience (discount factors). In maximally connected cliques, punishment is most effective due to perfect monitoring, and full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is sustainable if all individuals are sufficiently patient. However, when some individuals are impatient (low discount factor), cliques can lead to cascades of defection and lower welfare. In such cases, less connected networks (e.g., grids) can isolate the effects of uncooperative types, allowing more tolerant individuals to absorb free riding and maintain higher overall welfare. The optimal network design thus balances the benefits of effective punishment (which requires connectivity and observability) against the costs of social conflict from connecting intolerant and uncooperative types. The paper provides explicit formulas for expected welfare under different network designs and type distributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in repeated local public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma environments as a function of network structure, monitoring, and heterogeneity in patience. It shows that punishment is most effective (and efficiency is highest) in small, maximally connected groups with perfect monitoring and homogeneous patience. As heterogeneity increases, or as monitoring becomes more local/imperfect, the efficiency gains from punishment can be reduced or even reversed, with less connected networks sometimes yielding higher welfare. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by network connectivity, monitoring structure, and the distribution of patience among players. However, the results are theoretical and assume rational, forward-looking agents using trigger strategies; they do not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Zhou, X., Belloum, A., Lees, M. H., van Engers, T., & de Laat, C. (2022). Costly incentives design from an institutional perspective: cooperation, sustainability and affluence. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with institutional (third-party) incentives, pure punishment is always sustainable and, when sufficiently strong, increases both the market size and the accumulated wealth of participants and the third party. Light punishment can lead to market shrinkage or collapse. Mixed incentives (reward plus punishment) can maximize the sum of accumulated wealth for both parties at moderate strength, but their sustainability depends on both incentive strength and agent rationality. Pure reward increases participant wealth but is unsustainable for the third party in this model. The results are robust across different levels of agent rationality, though higher rationality makes it easier to achieve high cooperation and affluence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated pairwise social dilemmas with institutional punishment, increasing the strength of punishment (i.e., higher fines for defection) leads to higher efficiency (measured as accumulated wealth and market size), provided the punishment is not too light. Light punishment can reduce efficiency by shrinking the market. Mixed incentives can further optimize total efficiency at moderate strength. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar institutional settings, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to defection. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, so results should be transferred with some caution.

- source: Han, T. A., Duong, M. H., & Perc, M. (2024). Evolutionary mechanisms that promote cooperation may not promote social welfare. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with peer or institutional incentives, punishment mechanisms (both peer and institutional) are often detrimental to social welfare, even when they increase cooperation. Peer punishment increases cooperation more than peer reward, but peer reward leads to higher social welfare. For institutional incentives, reward achieves positive social welfare across a broader range of parameters than punishment, even when both achieve similar cooperation levels. There is often an optimal, moderate level of incentive impact for maximizing social welfare, rather than maximizing cooperation or punishment strength. These findings are robust across mutation rates, selection intensities, and game configurations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments (here, the Prisoner's Dilemma) is likely to reduce efficiency (social welfare) compared to reward mechanisms, even if punishment increases cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of model parameters. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially with high impact-to-cost ratio) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency relative to control, unless the punishment is extremely cost-effective and moderate in impact. The findings suggest that maximizing cooperation via punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and that reward mechanisms are generally preferable for social welfare. The results are based on theoretical models, not empirical data, but are directly relevant to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Mohlin, E., Rigos, A., & Weidenholzer, S. (2023). Emergence of specialized third-party enforcement. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that specialized third-party punishment can enable and sustain high-efficiency cooperative equilibria in social dilemmas where direct and indirect reciprocity fail. When enforcers are incentivized (via reputation and meta-enforcement) to punish defectors, producers cooperate, and both groups achieve higher payoffs. The efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the cost of information, and the destructiveness of enforcer conflict. If punishment is too costly or weak, or if enforcer conflict is not destructive enough, the system collapses to a low-efficiency defection equilibrium. The model also shows that the cooperative equilibrium is robust to the presence of alternative (non-cooperation-enforcing) reputation systems, but only if the key parameter thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment by specialized third-party enforcers can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but only if the punishment is sufficiently severe, not too costly, and enforcers are themselves disciplined (e.g., via meta-enforcement and reputation). The model clarifies which game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, information cost, severity of punishment, and the structure of enforcement interactions) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. For prediction, the presence of third-party punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, provided the relevant costs and incentives are favorable. However, if punishment is weak, costly, or undisciplined, efficiency gains may not materialize.

- source: Friehe, T., & Tabbach, A. (2018). A comparison of simple action-based and outcome-based policies for emergency-like situations. *Mathematical Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis shows that action-based punishment/reward policies are superior to outcome-based policies in generalized volunteer's dilemma settings (where more than one volunteer is needed and costs are private). Action-based policies can uniquely induce the efficient (second-best) outcome and prevent the no-participation equilibrium, while outcome-based policies always allow for a no-participation equilibrium, which is stable and potentially focal. The required level of punishment/reward under action-based policies is lower than under outcome-based policies. Combined policies that include punishment for non-participation can also rule out the no-participation equilibrium. The analysis is grounded in social cost minimization and group welfare, not just participation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that punishment (when implemented as action-based or combined with action-contingency) will have a more positive effect on efficiency (group welfare, social cost minimization) than outcome-based punishment in public-goods-like environments where more than one participant is needed. The results suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (action-based vs. outcome-based) is a critical moderator: action-based punishment can uniquely induce efficient outcomes and prevent social traps, while outcome-based punishment may fail to improve efficiency if the no-participation equilibrium is selected. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings with binary (all-or-nothing) participation, private costs, and no coordination. However, the results are theoretical and based on a generalized volunteer's dilemma, which is adjacent but not identical to standard PGGs.

- source: Lippert, S., & Spagnolo, G. (2011). Networks of relations and Word-of-Mouth Communication. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in networks of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the ability to transmit information (word-of-mouth) and the use of forgiving, targeted punishment strategies (Multilateral Repentance) allow for cooperation to be sustained under a wider range of conditions (i.e., lower discount factors) than under traditional contagion (Grim Trigger) strategies. This leads to higher efficiency and welfare, especially during punishment phases, as only defectors are punished and cooperation among others continues. The structure of the network (closure, density) and the speed of information transmission further moderate these effects. The results are formalized in terms of equilibrium payoff conditions and sustainability criteria.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling targeted, forgiving punishment (as opposed to all-or-nothing breakdowns) and facilitating information transmission (communication) in networked repeated social dilemmas increases the efficiency of cooperation. The results suggest that, all else equal, punishment regimes that are more targeted and allow for communication will yield higher efficiency than those that rely on contagious, permanent breakdowns. The paper also highlights the importance of network structure (closure, density) and information speed, which may inform how design dimensions like communication, punishment technology, and information feedback affect efficiency. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Langlois, C. C., & Langlois, J. P. P. (2004). Provisions for noncompliance and treaty value: A game theoretic perspective. *International Studies Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with possible unintentional noncompliance (noise), the design of punishment schemes has a large effect on long-run efficiency (treaty value). Probabilistic trigger schemes, which are analogous to mutual punishment phases after a deviation, are self-punishing and reduce efficiency because both the perpetrator and the victim suffer during punishment. Contrite tit-for-tat (CTFT) schemes, which allow for redress (compensation to the victim) and delay retaliation (generosity), achieve much higher efficiency by minimizing the time spent in mutual defection and ensuring that only the guilty party is punished. The paper provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for how efficiency declines with noise under each scheme, and shows that CTFT dominates trigger schemes in most of the relevant parameter space.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of the punishment scheme (especially whether punishment is self-punishing or allows for redress) is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated social dilemma games. If the punishment mechanism is costly to the punisher and involves mutual punishment phases (as in standard trigger schemes), efficiency is substantially reduced, especially as noise increases. If the punishment mechanism allows for redress (compensation to the victim) and avoids mutual punishment (as in CTFT), efficiency is much higher. The paper also shows that the speed of retaliation (trigger probability) and the expected duration of punishment phases are important: swifter, shorter punishments are more efficient, but only if they still deter defection. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment based on the punishment technology, cost structure, and whether redress is possible.

- source: Chassang, S., & Zehnder, C. (2016). Rewards and punishments: Informal contracting through social preferences. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that informal punishment and reward schemes, when based on social preferences and ex post moral judgment, can partially or fully internalize externalities and improve efficiency, but the effect depends on the type of justice (intent-based vs. outcome-based), the quality of information, and the nature of the externality (positive or negative). Intent-based justice can achieve high efficiency with good monitoring but is less robust and can result in multiple equilibria, including inefficient ones. Outcome-based justice is more robust but less efficient, as it does not exploit side information and always incurs transfer costs. The model provides explicit formulas for the efficiency loss under each regime and characterizes when punishment will or will not be used.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions about how the structure and effectiveness of punishment (and reward) mechanisms, the cost and technology of punishment, and the quality of information affect efficiency in environments with externalities. While not a direct PGG, the model is highly informative for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled, especially in settings where a principal or arbitrator determines sanctions based on observed outcomes and beliefs. The distinction between intent-based and outcome-based justice, and the explicit efficiency bounds derived, can inform predictions about the likely efficiency gains or limits from punishment in related experimental or field PGGs, especially when considering the role of monitoring precision, transfer costs, and the possibility of multiple equilibria.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Incentives and opportunism: from the carrot to the stick. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that negative incentives (punishment) can stabilize cooperation and lead to higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population, provided that players have enough information about others' reputations. Rewarding (positive incentives) is not stable in the long run but is important for catalyzing the initial emergence of cooperation, especially when information is limited. When both punishment and reward are available, the population transitions from defection to cooperation more quickly, and the stable outcome is a cooperative, high-payoff equilibrium enforced by punishment. The results are robust to population size and mutation structure, and the authors discuss how the findings generalize to n-person public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency (average group payoff), especially when players can observe or infer others' incentive-providing behavior. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is more pronounced when information about others is available, and that the presence of reward mechanisms can accelerate the transition to cooperation but does not affect the long-run efficiency as much as punishment does. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, two-stage games but are argued to generalize to n-person PGGs. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when information/reputation is high, and suggests that the marginal benefit of adding reward is mainly in catalyzing cooperation rather than sustaining higher efficiency in the long run.

- source: Feng, T. Y., Tai, S. L., Sun, C. S., & Man, Q. P. (2017). Study on Cooperative Mechanism of Prefabricated Producers Based on Evolutionary Game Theory. *Mathematical Problems in Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper models cooperation between two prefabricated producers using evolutionary game theory. It finds that, in the absence of external mechanisms, both mutual cooperation and mutual defection are stable equilibria, and the outcome depends on initial conditions. Introducing a punishment mechanism (where defectors are fined and the fine is transferred to the cooperator) or a sufficiently large incentive (reward) for cooperation changes the game so that only mutual cooperation is stable, provided the punishment or reward exceeds a threshold related to the cost of cooperation. This leads to a win-win outcome and maximizes group efficiency. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide empirical data, but it directly addresses the effect of punishment on group payoff and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in a two-player repeated game with payoff structure similar to a stag hunt or coordination game, introducing a sufficiently strong punishment mechanism (where defectors are fined and the fine is transferred to the cooperator) ensures that mutual cooperation is the only stable outcome, maximizing group efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar settings, enabling punishment (with sufficient strength) will reliably increase efficiency from potentially low (if initial conditions favor defection) to maximal (full cooperation). However, the model is limited to two players, does not include stochasticity or empirical heterogeneity, and assumes rational updating. The findings are most applicable to small, symmetric, repeated games with clear punishment transfer.

- source: Mihm, M., & Toth, R. (2020). Cooperative networks with robust private monitoring. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms can sustain efficient cooperation in networked repeated games, but the effectiveness of punishment—and thus the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes—depends critically on the monitoring structure. With perfect or partial monitoring (players can observe at least their neighbors' neighbors), punishment can be immediate and efficient networks are possible. With local (private) monitoring (players only observe their own ties), punishment is delayed and only highly clustered networks (with triadic closure) can robustly support cooperation. The paper provides formal incentive constraints (PIC, LIC, RIC) that characterize when efficient or near-efficient networks can be sustained. Efficiency is maximized when monitoring allows for rapid punishment, and is reduced when monitoring is local and punishment is delayed or less credible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the monitoring structure (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' and 'show_punishment_id'), network clustering, and the immediacy/effectiveness of punishment affect the efficiency of public-goods-like games. If punishment is possible and monitoring is sufficiently rich (players can observe others' actions beyond their own ties), then enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency or at least sustain cooperation. If monitoring is strictly local, only highly clustered (triadically closed) networks can sustain cooperation, and efficiency gains from punishment are limited. The paper also shows that the cost and effectiveness of punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech) interact with network structure to determine whether efficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with bilateral ties, but the logic extends to public goods games with networked or local interaction structures.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Lenaerts, T., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Mediating artificial intelligence developments through negative and positive incentives. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment (sanctions) and rewards can increase group welfare and promote safety compliance in a repeated innovation race game, but their effects depend on the underlying risk and incentive parameters. Punishment is generally more effective at suppressing unsafe (defective) behavior and increasing group welfare in the 'dilemma' region where individual and collective interests diverge, but if punishment is too strong or too efficient, it can lead to over-regulation and reduce innovation even when risk is low and unsafe behavior would be collectively optimal. Rewards can also promote safety and welfare, especially when highly cost-efficient, and do not suffer from the risk of over-regulation, but are generally less effective than punishment. The model provides explicit conditions for when each incentive is beneficial or harmful for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like innovation races is highly context-dependent. If the risk of disaster from unsafe behavior is high, punishment increases efficiency by promoting safe behavior. If the risk is low, strong punishment can reduce efficiency by suppressing innovation.
