The cost-effectiveness of punishment (punishment cost and impact) is critical: punishment is most beneficial when its cost to the punisher is low relative to its impact on the punished. Reward mechanisms can also increase efficiency, especially when highly cost-effective, and are less likely to reduce efficiency through over-regulation. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on the risk/benefit structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline efficiency of the control game. The findings are most directly applicable to innovation race or winner-take-all settings, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: Nakao, K. (2009). CREATION OF SOCIAL ORDER IN ETHNIC CONFLICT. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of punishment mechanisms (peer monitoring, collective peer punishment, or ethnic conflict) can sustain higher group efficiency (expected payoffs) compared to no punishment, but only if in-group monitoring is sufficiently efficient and not too costly. When monitoring is inefficient or costly, or when noise (misinterpretation of actions) is high, punishment can reduce efficiency due to frequent erroneous punishment or high monitoring costs. The model provides comparative statics showing that efficiency is maximized with efficient peer monitoring and high tolerance, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the quality of in-group policing, the length of conflict, and the density of inter-group interactions. The model predicts that groups with high-quality in-group policing achieve higher efficiency and more stable cooperation, while groups with low-quality policing suffer more frequent and longer conflicts, reducing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games depends critically on the efficiency and cost of monitoring and punishment. If the game design allows for efficient, low-cost peer monitoring (low punishment cost, high punishment tech), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control. If monitoring is costly or ineffective, punishment can reduce efficiency due to high costs or frequent erroneous punishment. The model also shows that the effect of punishment is moderated by noise (misinterpretation), group size, and the structure of interactions. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency only when monitoring is efficient and not too costly, and that the marginal effect of punishment is lower or negative when monitoring is costly or noise is high. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so findings should be transferred with caution.

- source: Matsushima, H. (2012). FINITELY REPEATED PRISONERS' DILEMMA WITH SMALL FINES: THE PENANCE CONTRACT. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that with a suitable contract that imposes a fine for deviation in the final period, full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) can be uniquely sustained in finitely repeated prisoners' dilemma games. The required fine can be small relative to total payoffs if the game is long enough. The penance contract is robust to renegotiation, unlike trigger contracts. The result generalizes to other two-player games with similar payoff structure. The findings are theoretical and show that the design of the punishment mechanism (timing, size, and contingency) is crucial for achieving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in two-player finitely repeated social dilemma games, the introduction of a well-designed punishment mechanism (specifically, a contract that imposes a fine for final-period deviation) can move the group from inefficient (all-defect) equilibria to fully efficient (all-cooperate) equilibria. The effect is robust to renegotiation if the punishment is contingent on final-period behavior and the fine is large enough to outweigh the gain from deviation. The result is directly relevant for predicting that enabling such a punishment mechanism will increase efficiency to the maximum possible, provided the contract is enforceable and the fine is sufficiently large. However, the result is limited to two-player, finitely repeated, contractible games and does not address larger groups or public goods games directly.

- source: Yaman, A., Leibo, J. Z., Iacca, G., & Wan Lee, S. (2023). The emergence of division of labour through decentralized social sanctioning. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that decentralized social sanctioning, implemented as a matrix of role-based rules for reward and punishment, enables groups of self-interested agents to learn and maintain efficient divisions of labour. This leads to substantially higher average group payoff compared to groups without sanctioning, or with only altruistic or selfish learning. The effect is robust across different environmental conditions and is close to the theoretical upper bound. Complexity regularization can promote the evolution of simpler, convergent social norms without sacrificing group payoff. The model shows that sanctioning transforms the incentive structure, making it individually rational to take on critical but otherwise unrewarding roles, thus solving the social dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that enabling decentralized social sanctioning (punishment and/or reward) in public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when critical roles are otherwise unrewarding. The effect is robust across a range of environmental parameters and is close to the optimal achievable by global optimization. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally adjacent, with payoff interdependence and social dilemmas. The findings support the prediction that, all else equal, enabling punishment (and/or reward) will increase efficiency relative to a control with sanctioning disabled, especially in settings where division of labour is required. The model also highlights the importance of norm complexity and the potential for simple sanctioning rules to be highly effective.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2020). Five rules for friendly rivalry in direct reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strategies combining partner and rival features (friendly rivals) exist in the repeated prisoner's dilemma, with the CAPRI strategy as a prime example. CAPRI achieves mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency) when both players use it, and punishes defection to prevent exploitation. Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI dominates other strategies in terms of abundance and average payoff across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios, error rates, and population sizes. The paper also shows that memory length (m >= 3) is crucial for such strategies. While the main results are for the two-player PD, the authors note that similar strategies exist for the three-person public goods game, suggesting potential generalizability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment strategies designed to be both efficient and defensible (like CAPRI) can substantially increase group efficiency and average payoff in repeated prisoner's dilemma settings, and by extension, in closely related public goods games. The findings suggest that, when punishment is implemented with sufficient memory and recovery mechanisms, it can robustly improve efficiency regardless of population size, error rate, or benefit-to-cost ratio. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that enabling well-designed punishment in repeated games will increase efficiency, especially when players can remember and respond to past actions. However, the results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated PDs, with only indirect evidence for multi-player PG games.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2011). What makes people cooperate? Individual differences in BAS/BIS predict strategic reciprocation in a public goods game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with super cooperators (who both punish defectors and reward cooperators), the ability of speculative defectors to identify and cooperate with super cooperators (i.e., higher information level) generally increases the prevalence of super cooperators and raises average social payoff (efficiency), provided the cost of punishment/reward is not too high. When the cost is high, increasing information can instead destroy super cooperators. The alliance between speculative defectors and super cooperators can insulate super cooperators from exploitation by unconditional cooperators, further supporting cooperation and efficiency. The model demonstrates that the introduction of punishment/reward mechanisms and information about others' strategies can substantially improve group efficiency compared to the baseline with only unconditional cooperators and defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatially structured prisoner's dilemma environments, enabling punishment (and reward) mechanisms—especially when paired with higher information about others' strategies—can increase group efficiency, as measured by average social payoff. The effect is strongest when the cost of punishment/reward is moderate or low. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games with similar structure, though the model is not a standard public goods game but a spatial PD with three strategies. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient information) should increase efficiency relative to the control. However, if the cost of punishment/reward is high, the effect can reverse. The model's focus on spatial structure, information, and cost of punishment/reward provides indirect but informative guidance for analogous PGG-like environments.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Third-party punishment as a costly signal of high continuation probabilities in repeated games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper develops a formal model in which third-party punishment (TPP) acts as a costly signal of trustworthiness in repeated games. The model shows that when individuals differ in their probability of future interaction (continuation probability), only those with high continuation probability (trustworthy types) find it worthwhile to engage in TPP, as they are more likely to receive future reciprocal benefits. The signaling value of TPP can make punishment equilibrium behavior in parameter regions where, absent signaling, punishment would not occur. The model provides explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions, and demonstrates via evolutionary simulations that the punishment signaling equilibrium is dynamically stable. The presence of TPP as a signal increases the set of parameter values for which cooperation and higher group payoffs are sustained, thus improving efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the availability of punishment as a costly signal can increase group efficiency in repeated games, especially when individuals differ in continuation probability and when signaling is possible. The model gives explicit conditions (in terms of cost/benefit ratios, continuation probabilities, and punishment costs) under which punishment increases efficiency. While the model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, its structure is closely related to repeated social dilemma games and provides mechanistic insight into when and why punishment will improve efficiency. The findings suggest that, in environments where signaling is possible and individuals can benefit from future interactions, enabling punishment can lead to higher efficiency than disabling it, provided the cost of punishment is not too high.

- source: Balmaceda, F., & Escobar, J. F. (2017). Trust in cohesive communities. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency and welfare effects of punishment in repeated trust games depend critically on the structure of the social network and the speed of information flow. When information about defections spreads only to direct neighbors (slow information), cohesive networks (complete components) are optimal for sustaining cooperation and maximizing welfare, as they allow for coordinated punishment. When information spreads rapidly to all direct and indirect connections (fast information), minimally connected networks (e.g., trees) are optimal, as they maximize the number of players who can punish a defection. The paper also shows that the convexity or concavity of the welfare function determines whether stars or equally sized components maximize welfare. The analysis provides explicit characterizations of equilibrium payoffs and welfare under different network and monitoring regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how network structure and information transmission technology moderate the efficiency gains from punishment in repeated trust-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not uniform but depends on whether the network allows for coordinated punishment and how quickly information about defections spreads. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that game design dimensions related to network structure, monitoring technology, and information flow are critical moderators of punishment's efficiency effects. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not provide empirical effect sizes, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution and only in settings structurally similar to the model.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2017). Asymmetric social norms. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games with heterogeneous players (random productivity), efficient outcomes may require some players to defect in certain matches. In such settings, standard public monitoring is insufficient to sustain efficiency, but contagious punishment strategies—where players switch to permanent defection after observing a deviation—can support high payoffs if the group is sufficiently patient, punishment is severe enough, and productivity differences are not too large. The paper provides explicit conditions under which these strategies are equilibrium and efficiency is achieved, and derives closed-form expressions for continuation payoffs. Thus, punishment (in the form of contagious, community-based sanctions) can enable high efficiency even in complex, asymmetric environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (specifically, contagious punishment) can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemma games, even when the efficient outcome involves some defections due to heterogeneity. The results are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random productivity, but the mechanisms and equilibrium conditions are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency in public-goods-like environments with heterogeneity. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to standard PGG parameters, but it does clarify that punishment can sustain high efficiency if players are patient, punishment is strong, and heterogeneity is moderate. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment in heterogeneous repeated games is likely to increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently severe and the group is not too heterogeneous.

- source: Stahl, D. O. (2011). Cooperation in the sporadically repeated prisoners' dilemma via reputation mechanisms. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in the SRPD, only a small subset of reputation mechanisms can sustain cooperation and achieve efficient outcomes. Specifically, mechanisms that distinguish between justified punishment and defection, and allow for penitence, can support equilibria where the long-run group payoff matches the fully cooperative outcome. The analysis shows that, with these mechanisms and high enough continuation probability, the population evolves toward full cooperation and efficiency. In contrast, most reputation mechanisms result in universal defection and zero efficiency. The paper also extends the analysis to clan-based (group) settings, showing that the efficiency result generalizes when within-group matching is sufficiently likely.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the structure of the punishment/reputation mechanism is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. If the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid penalizing justified punishers and allows for penitence, and if the continuation probability is high, then enabling punishment can move efficiency from zero (universal defection) to the full-cooperation benchmark. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the details of the punishment/reputation update rule and the effective 'delta' (continuation probability) are crucial for forecasting the efficiency impact of punishment. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, anonymous-matching games with public reputations, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like settings with similar reputation mechanisms.

- source: Corriveau, L. (2012). Game theory and the kula. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a repeated, circular n-player game (the 'game of kula'), the unique Nash equilibrium of the one-shot game is inefficient (all defect). However, when the game is repeated indefinitely and players are sufficiently patient, efficient (fully cooperative) equilibria can be sustained by social norms that attach punishment to deviations. The model formalizes three types of punishment-based norms: direct reciprocity (punished by the harmed party), indirect reciprocity (punished by a third party), and mixed reciprocity (punished by both). The mixed norm is shown to be the most robust, existing under the widest range of parameters. The analysis is entirely theoretical, but it provides formal conditions under which punishment enables efficient cooperation, and shows that the presence of punishment strategies can transform an inefficient game into an efficient one.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in repeated, multi-player social dilemma games (with circular or networked structure) can increase efficiency, provided players are sufficiently patient and punishment is credible. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the results generalize the repeated prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is possible and observable, and where players have high discount factors, the introduction of punishment mechanisms can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (defection) to efficient (cooperation), thus increasing group payoff. The paper also clarifies that the structure of punishment (direct, indirect, mixed) affects the robustness of cooperation, with mixed norms being most robust. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially as the number of players increases and as the possibility of both direct and indirect punishment exists.

- source: Tao, J., Zhong, X., & Lu, Y. F. (2011). Restrictive mechanism of flow control among non-cooperative Internet users. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated flow control games, the use of punishment strategies (including trigger, tit-for-tat, and finite punishment) can deter selfish deviations and sustain cooperation among users, leading to optimal or near-optimal system utility. In the absence of such mechanisms, the equilibrium is inefficient. The FCAR algorithm, which operationalizes finite punishment, is shown via simulation to effectively regularize user behavior and maintain high system utility, with only temporary utility losses during punishment. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the number of users and the length of punishment. In finitely repeated games, cooperation is harder to sustain, especially in the final stage, but rational punishment strategies can still maintain cooperation for most of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in repeated resource allocation games (adjacent to public goods games) increases efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of users, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong and the game is sufficiently long or indefinite. The findings support the prediction that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase average efficiency, especially when users are patient (high discount factor) and punishment is coordinated. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, continuous-action, non-anonymous settings with full information about others' actions and payoffs.

- source: Lancia, F., & Russo, A. (2019). Sustaining Cooperation Through Strategic Self-Interested Actions. *B E Journal of Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that organizations can sustain higher efficiency and cooperation by conditioning punishments (and rewards) not only on collective output but also on observable outside activities, even if those activities are not directly productive. Strategic distortion of privately beneficial effort (either increasing or decreasing it, depending on whether it is a complement or substitute to collective effort) reduces the short-run gain from defection, thus lowering the required severity and frequency of punishment. This mechanism increases the expected output and intertemporal utility of members compared to settings where only output is monitored. The result is robust to extensions with more generations and noisy monitoring of outside activities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated public-goods-like environments with imperfect monitoring, the ability to condition punishment on additional observable actions (beyond just output) increases efficiency by making cooperation easier to sustain. If a game design allows punishment to be based on richer or more informative signals (e.g., monitoring of side activities), the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be higher than in designs where only output is observable. The model also predicts that the efficiency gain from punishment is larger when monitoring is more informative and when organizations can strategically distort observable but privately beneficial actions. However, the model is not an empirical test and does not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Bendor, J., & Swistak, P. (2001). The evolution of norms. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated games of enforceable cooperation, only social norms (strategies that prescribe third-party punishment) can be evolutionarily stable. The presence of effective punishment is necessary for stability. Both efficient and inefficient social norms can be stable, but efficient (cooperative) norms are more robust to invasion and require a lower frequency to stabilize. The model predicts that, over time, groups are more likely to drift toward efficient, cooperative equilibria regulated by social norms, but inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria are also possible and robust. The findings are general for two-person repeated games and extend to sparse networks, provided third-party information is sufficiently available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the necessity of punishment (sanctions) for stable cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. It shows that the presence of punishment mechanisms is a necessary condition for the evolutionary stability of cooperative, efficient outcomes, but does not guarantee efficiency: inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria can also be stable. However, more efficient equilibria are more robust to invasion and require a lower threshold to stabilize. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in a repeated game with enforceable cooperation will generally increase the likelihood of stable cooperation and higher efficiency, but the actual efficiency achieved may depend on the specific norm that evolves. The robustness of efficient equilibria to invasion is higher, so in the long run, efficiency is more likely with punishment enabled, but inefficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to two-person repeated games, but the logic extends to larger groups and sparse networks if third-party information is available.

- source: Gioffré, A., & Tampieri, A. (2025). Community enforcement and the cost of cooperation. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement via grim punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with random matching, provided the cost of cooperation is not too low or too high. Specifically, if the cost-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold (less than 0.5), punishment is always credible and cooperation is an equilibrium for any population size and discount factor. For any environment, there exists a 'target cost of cooperation' that enables coordination on cooperation and makes punishment effective. When the cost of cooperation is endogenous, players can use it as a coordination device to achieve efficient outcomes. The results are entirely theoretical and provide explicit payoff-based conditions for when punishment increases group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with random matching and private monitoring, the introduction of credible punishment (grim trigger) can increase group efficiency to the cooperative level, provided the cost of cooperation is within a specific range. The key moderators are the cost-to-benefit ratio, population size, and discount factor. The results suggest that, for a wide range of parameter values, enabling punishment will move efficiency from the defection baseline to the cooperative optimum. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the cost of cooperation (analogous to MPCR in PGGs), population size, and the presence of punishment. However, the model is not a literal public goods game but an adjacent repeated PD, so some caution is warranted in direct quantitative transfer.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., Wang, X. J., Yang, J. B., Wu, K. B., & Dai, Z. L. (2019). Withhold-judgment and punishment promote cooperation in indirect reciprocity under incomplete information. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of punishment in indirect reciprocity models with incomplete information generally increases cooperation rates, but its effect on efficiency (average payoff) depends on the social norm and parameter settings. In some cases, especially when punishment is frequent and costly, the net effect on social welfare is negative, even as cooperation rises. Social norms that allow observers to withhold judgment (not update reputation) in ambiguous cases are more robust, especially under private reputation and noisy/incomplete information, and can maintain both high cooperation and high average payoff. The effect of punishment is thus context-dependent: it can either improve or harm efficiency depending on the interplay of punishment cost, observation probability, and the social norm in use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is not universally positive: while punishment reliably increases cooperation, it can reduce efficiency if the cost of punishment is high or if punishment is overused. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related (donation game with indirect reciprocity and group-level outcomes). The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one must consider not only the presence of punishment but also its cost, the effectiveness of punishment, the information structure (public vs private reputation), and the social norm governing reputation updates. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not always a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment), as punishment can sometimes lower group welfare despite increasing cooperation.

- source: dos Santos, M., Rankin, D. J., & Wedekind, C. (2011). The evolution of punishment through reputation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when reputationally visible, can evolve and be stable because it increases the likelihood of receiving help in the future, offsetting its immediate cost. Analytical and simulation results show that punishing discriminators (who punish defectors and are recognized for it) achieve higher payoffs and can dominate, leading to high cooperation and group efficiency. The effect is robust to a range of costs and benefits, but is sensitive to the accuracy of reputation information and the mutation rate. The findings suggest that reputation is a key moderator of punishment's efficiency-enhancing effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on whether punishment is reputationally visible and can influence future interactions. In environments where reputation for punishing is tracked and used by others to condition their cooperation, punishment can increase group efficiency and stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of costs and benefits, but efficiency gains are reduced if reputation information is noisy or mutation rates are low. For prediction, the presence of a reputation system that makes punishment visible is a key moderator for positive efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Bhaskar, V., & Thomas, C. (2019). Community Enforcement of Trust with Bounded Memory. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain efficient cooperation (trust/lending) in large societies with random matching and one-sided moral hazard depends critically on the information structure available to punishers (lenders). With perfect bounded memory, punishment unravels and efficiency collapses. With coarse information (binary flag for recent default), temporary exclusion can sustain high efficiency, with the exact level depending on the profitability of lending and the exclusion length. The model provides explicit formulas for borrower and lender payoffs under different regimes. When lending is very profitable, some inefficiency is unavoidable due to the need for randomization and endogenous adverse selection. The results generalize to other sequential-move games with one-sided moral hazard.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining efficiency depends on the information structure: coarse information (pooling recent defaulters) enables efficient punishment and high payoffs, while fine-grained information leads to unraveling and zero efficiency. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that punishment will only improve efficiency if the punishment mechanism is supported by an information structure that prevents cherry-picking of near-rehabilitated defectors. The model is not a PGG or direct PGG variant, but the logic applies to trust games and other one-sided moral hazard settings. The findings are most relevant for settings with random matching, temporary exclusion, and information constraints.

- source: Baker, S., & Choi, A. H. (2018). Reputation and Litigation: Why Costly Legal Sanctions Can Work Better than Reputational Sanctions. *Journal of Legal Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that legal sanctions (punishment) can be more efficient than reputational sanctions (boycotts) in deterring undesirable behavior, even when both are costly. This is because legal sanctions have an inframarginal effect: increasing damages not only deters more lawsuits at the margin but also increases the penalty for existing lawsuits, improving deterrence at lower cost. Reputational sanctions lack this inframarginal effect, so increasing their severity is less efficient. The efficiency advantage of legal sanctions is greatest when courts are accurate and when litigation outcomes are public. When court error is high or when consumers have independent, reliable information about quality, reputational sanctions become more important. The model predicts that the optimal regime combines both types of sanctions, but leans toward legal sanctions unless litigation is highly error-prone or costly. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide experimental or empirical estimates, but it offers clear comparative statics and policy implications for the efficiency of punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (legal sanctions) in repeated moral hazard environments can increase efficiency relative to relying solely on reputational sanctions, especially when punishment is not too costly or error-prone and when outcomes are public. The inframarginal effect of punishment means that its efficiency benefit can be greater than that of reputational sanctions, which only deter at the margin. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment should increase efficiency, particularly when the punishment mechanism is accurate and visible. However, if punishment is highly error-prone or costly, or if reputational information is highly reliable, the efficiency gain from punishment may be reduced or even reversed. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution, but the qualitative comparative statics are clear and relevant.

- source: Honjo, K., & Kubo, T. (2020). Social Dilemmas in Nature-Based Tourism Depend on Social Value Orientations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of outcomes in a two-player common-pool resource game depends critically on the social value orientations (SVOs) of the players. When both players are competitive, the Nash equilibrium is often Pareto-inefficient (i.e., group efficiency is low), and this inefficiency is robust to the introduction of fair punishment. In fact, punishment can increase the parameter space in which inefficiency occurs for competitive players. When at least one player is individualistic or prosocial, the Nash equilibrium is almost always Pareto efficient (i.e., high group efficiency). The model demonstrates that punishment does not always improve efficiency and can backfire in certain social contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the social preferences of the players. In environments where players are competitive (i.e., care about outperforming others), introducing fair punishment can worsen efficiency, increasing the likelihood of Pareto-inefficient outcomes. In contrast, with individualistic or prosocial players, efficiency is generally high even without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee improved efficiency and may even reduce it in competitive settings, especially in small, binary-choice, one-shot games. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, single-round, all-or-nothing, common-pool resource dilemmas, but the underlying logic may transfer to similar public goods environments.

- source: Murase, Y. (2025). Costly punishment sustains indirect reciprocity under low defection detectability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of costly punishment in sustaining cooperation depends critically on the detectability of defection. When defection is difficult to detect (i.e., high probability that defection is misperceived as cooperation), social norms that include costly punishment (CP norms) can be the most efficient ESS, achieving high cooperation and high average payoff. When defection is easy to detect, punishment is rarely efficient and often reduces payoffs. The model provides explicit boundaries for when punishment is efficient, and shows that punishment is most effective when it is rarely executed but strongly deters defection. Execution errors and high punishment costs shrink the parameter space where punishment is efficient. The results challenge previous theoretical work that found only a narrow efficiency margin for punishment, showing that under asymmetric detection errors, punishment can be broadly efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment on efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with pairwise donation structure. While not a standard public goods game, the model is adjacent and the efficiency results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in environments where defection is hard to detect. The explicit formulas and phase diagrams allow mapping from game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, error rates) and baseline efficiency to the expected efficiency with punishment enabled. The main moderator is the detectability of defection: when it is low, punishment can substantially increase efficiency; when high, punishment is unnecessary or even harmful. The results are most transferable to settings with pairwise interactions, reputation, and indirect reciprocity, but provide strong mechanistic insight for broader PGG-like environments.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2014). A tractable analysis of contagious equilibria. *Journal of Mathematical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that contagious punishment (grim trigger strategies) in repeated random-matching social dilemmas can sustain full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) if players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor), the cost of cooperation is not too high, and monitoring is sufficiently broad. The authors derive closed-form expressions for continuation payoffs and provide exact bounds on the discount factor and the cost of cooperation required to make cooperation self-enforcing. They show that increasing the breadth of monitoring (i.e., allowing players to observe more actions outside their own match) makes it easier to sustain cooperation, lowering the required discount factor and cost of cooperation. Conversely, if defection is not absorbing (i.e., if players can revert to cooperation), sustaining cooperation becomes harder. The results are general for random-matching repeated games with contagious punishment, and the framework is intended to inform both theory and laboratory design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the presence and structure of punishment (specifically, contagious, unforgiving punishment) can enable high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even with random matching and anonymity. The key moderators are the discount factor, the cost of cooperation, and the breadth of monitoring (how many others' actions are observed). The results suggest that, in environments with strong punishment and sufficient monitoring, enabling punishment should increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), provided the other parameters (patience, cost) are favorable. The paper also provides explicit formulas for the parameter thresholds needed to sustain cooperation, which could be used to predict when punishment will be effective in raising efficiency. However, the results are theoretical and apply to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-type games, not public goods games per se, though the logic is closely related.

- source: Jindani, S. (2020). Community enforcement using modal actions. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated random matching games where players are matched with multiple partners and can observe modal actions, any efficient (strictly individually rational) payoff can be supported as a strict, sequential equilibrium. This includes full cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma and similar games. The mechanism relies on community punishment: players who deviate (i.e., are non-modal) are sanctioned by others, and those who fail to sanction are also sanctioned. The equilibrium is robust to trembles and the presence of noisy players, and the efficiency loss due to noise is linear in the noise rate. However, the mechanism is not robust to observation errors unless these are rare relative to other noise. The informational burden is low: in the prisoner's dilemma, only one period of memory is needed to sustain cooperation. The results generalize to a wide class of games and show that community enforcement with punishment can achieve high efficiency under realistic informational constraints.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated random matching games with multiple partners per period and observable modal actions, enabling community punishment can raise efficiency to the full cooperative level, provided players are sufficiently patient and the population is large. The results are robust to some forms of noise, and the informational requirements are minimal. For prediction tasks, the presence of multiple matching, observable modal actions, and community punishment mechanisms should be expected to enable high efficiency, even in large groups. However, if observation errors are frequent or if players cannot observe modal actions, efficiency may not be sustained. The findings are most directly applicable to settings structurally similar to the model (random matching, multiple partners, observable actions, and community punishment), and less so to standard public goods games with fixed groups and no community enforcement.

- source: Quan, J., Pu, Z. J., & Wang, X. J. (2021). Comparison of social exclusion and punishment in promoting cooperation: Who should play the leading role?. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both keeping and reversing social norms can promote cooperation and increase social welfare (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games with punishment. The effect is moderated by the benefit-cost ratio: keeping rules are more effective at low benefit-cost ratios, while reversing rules are more effective at high benefit-cost ratios. Public reputation scenarios consistently support higher cooperation and welfare than private reputation scenarios. Punishment actions can help sustain cooperation, but their cost can reduce average welfare. The specific social norm in use (among 16 studied) determines whether the population reaches a cooperative or non-cooperative stable state, especially in private reputation settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of social norms and the structure of reputation (public vs. private) are critical moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency (average payoff) in indirect reciprocity games. The results suggest that, in environments similar to pairwise donation games with indirect reciprocity, enabling punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but the net effect on efficiency depends on the cost of punishment and the social norm in use. Public reputation and appropriate social norms (keeping or reversing, depending on benefit-cost ratio) are important for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to pairwise donation games, not standard public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for prediction.

- source: Ali, S. N., & Miller, D. A. (2016). Ostracism and Forgiveness. *American Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated partnership games with private monitoring and evidentiary communication, permanent ostracism (permanent exclusion of deviators) cannot achieve higher efficiency or group payoff than bilateral enforcement, because victims have incentives to conceal deviations rather than report them. This limits the effectiveness of the harshest forms of community punishment. However, if ostracism is temporary and includes forgiveness (eventual readmission of deviators), then higher levels of cooperation and group payoff can be sustained, as the prospect of future cooperation with forgiven players incentivizes truthful reporting. The paper provides formal bounds and equilibrium constructions for these results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment—specifically, whether it is permanent or temporary (with forgiveness)—is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated partnership games with private monitoring. If punishment is permanent ostracism, efficiency does not improve over bilateral enforcement. If punishment is temporary (forgiveness is possible), efficiency can be strictly higher, especially as patience or group size increases. For prediction tasks, the presence of forgiveness/temporary ostracism should be coded as a key moderator, and the effect of punishment on efficiency should be expected to be positive only when forgiveness is possible. The results are derived in a variable-stakes partnership game, which is adjacent to but not exactly a standard public goods game.

- source: Melkonyan, T., Zeitoun, H., & Chater, N. (2022). The cognitive foundations of tacit commitments: A virtual bargaining model of dynamic interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in dynamic games without external enforcement, the possibility of tacit commitment to punishment (as formalized by the virtual bargaining equilibrium) enables self-interested agents to achieve efficient, mutually beneficial outcomes that are not predicted by standard subgame perfect equilibrium. In games like the finitely repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, Centipede, and repeated Bertrand competition, VBE supports cooperation, collusion, or turn-taking, enforced by credible threats of punishment (even if costly). This leads to higher group payoffs and efficiency compared to the standard prediction. However, if players cannot commit to future punishment (SVBE), the threat is not credible and efficiency collapses to the Nash/subgame perfect level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling credible punishment (even if costly to the punisher) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated or sequential social dilemma games, relative to control conditions where punishment is not possible or not credible. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, but the logic applies to adjacent environments (e.g., repeated PD, Centipede, repeated Bertrand) that share key features with public goods games. The findings suggest that, when game design allows for credible commitment to punishment, efficiency is likely to be much higher than in control games without such commitment. The effect is robust to the absence of external enforcement or non-standard preferences. However, the model assumes agents reason as virtual bargainers; if they do not, or if commitment is not possible, efficiency gains disappear. The paper is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be credibly pre-committed.

- source: Saak, A. E. (2012). Collective Reputation, Social Norms, and Participation. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that collective reputation mechanisms with local peer punishment can sustain higher efficiency (average payoffs, welfare) than individual reputation, as long as group size is not too small or too large and peer monitoring is present. Peer punishment deters shirking and allows for softer public punishment, increasing the duration of efficient cooperation. The optimal group size increases with the discount factor, the informativeness of public signals, and the quality premium, but decreases with the relative gain from shirking. Without peer monitoring, collective reputation is less efficient than individual reputation. Perfect global peer monitoring allows for arbitrarily large efficient groups. Mandatory traceability (which enables individual punishment) can reduce welfare by undermining group-based peer punishment. The model provides clear comparative statics and welfare implications for the design of collective brands and group-based sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling peer punishment (local or global) in repeated group settings with imperfect public monitoring increases efficiency and group payoffs, compared to settings without such punishment. The model is not a literal public goods game, but is adjacent: it is a repeated moral hazard game with group-level payoffs, endogenous peer punishment, and collective reputation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms (especially peer punishment) on efficiency, conditional on group size, monitoring structure, and information. The paper also provides comparative statics on how efficiency gains from punishment depend on group size, monitoring, and information, which can inform predictions about moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Berger, U., & De Silva, H. (2021). Evolution of deterrence with costly reputation information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that in a repeated two-player deterrence game (with roles of challenger and defender), the possibility of costly punishment (retaliation) can lead to higher defender payoffs and social welfare, but only when reputation information is minimal (e.g., only the last action is observable and costly to obtain). When reputation information is rich (e.g., challengers can observe a defender's empirical fighting frequency over many rounds), deterrence breaks down, punishment loses its deterrent effect, and defender payoffs collapse. Agent-based simulations confirm that defender payoffs decline as the amount of reputation information increases, with a sharp drop when information becomes too rich. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: positive under minimal information, negative or null under rich information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the information structure: punishment increases efficiency (defender payoffs) when reputation information is minimal and costly, but not when information is rich and easily available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension 'information about others' past actions' (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' or 'show_punishment_id') is a key moderator: enabling rich, low-cost information can undermine the efficiency benefits of punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player deterrence games, but the mechanisms may generalize to PGG variants where punishment and reputation interact.

- source: Thomadsen, R., & Bhardwaj, P. (2011). Cooperation in Games with Forgetfulness. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of forgetfulness on efficiency in repeated games with punishment is fundamentally ambiguous: it can either reduce or increase the set of discount factors and game parameters under which full cooperation (and thus maximum efficiency) is sustainable. When the stage game is such that the cooperative action always yields nonnegative payoffs, forgetfulness weakens punishment and makes cooperation harder to sustain, reducing efficiency. However, when the stage game allows for the possibility that a forgetful player can be punished below the minimax, forgetfulness can make severe punishments credible and thus expand the range of parameters supporting cooperation, increasing efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas and graphical illustrations for these effects in the repeated prisoner's dilemma and a franchising effort game. The results are theoretical and depend on the structure of the stage game and the probability of forgetfulness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games is not monotonic and can be moderated by cognitive or institutional features like forgetfulness. In environments where punishment is only as severe as the stage minimax, introducing forgetfulness will generally reduce the efficiency gains from punishment. However, in environments where forgetfulness allows for below-minimax punishments, punishment can become more effective at sustaining cooperation, potentially increasing efficiency beyond what is possible with perfect recall. For prediction tasks, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined solely from standard game design dimensions; one must also consider whether the environment allows for forgetfulness and whether the stage game payoffs permit below-minimax punishments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates but gives clear theoretical boundaries and mechanisms.

- source: Evans, R., & Thomas, J. P. (2001). Cooperation and punishment. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated common interest games (with no discounting), the mere presence of cooperative (nondraconian) strategies in the set of possible strategies does not guarantee efficient (Pareto-optimal) outcomes in equilibrium. However, if the set of possible strategies includes at least one 'draconian' strategy (i.e., a strategy that is willing to punish noncooperation to the maximum extent, such as min-maxing the opponent forever after a deviation), then all Nash equilibria are efficient. The paper also shows that this result is robust to certain computability restrictions and discusses the limits of these results under discounting and other technical assumptions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated games with a structure similar to public goods or common interest games, the availability of sufficiently severe punishment (i.e., the ability to impose maximal punishment at low or no cost) is both necessary and sufficient for punishment to induce efficient outcomes in equilibrium. If the game design allows for draconian punishment strategies, then enabling punishment should lead to high efficiency, regardless of the control efficiency. If only mild or 'cooperative' punishment is possible, efficiency gains are not guaranteed. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, infinite-horizon, undiscounted games, but the logic is relevant for understanding the role of punishment severity and credibility in other settings.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). A remarkable effect of the combination of probabilistic peerpunishment and coevolutionary mechanism on the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences, together with a coevolutionary mechanism allowing players to break and form connections, leads to a substantial increase in the average payoff of all players (i.e., group efficiency) in evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on networks. This effect is especially pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate values of the punishment coefficient. The combination of these mechanisms expands the parameter range (punishment coefficient r) over which high cooperation and high average payoff are sustained, compared to models with only one or neither mechanism. The effect is less pronounced or absent in regular networks with high average degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in evolutionary networked prisoner's dilemma games, enabling a punishment mechanism (specifically, probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences) increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when combined with a coevolutionary mechanism for network rewiring. The effect is robust across a range of network topologies and average degrees, but is most pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate punishment cost/effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar networked games is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when players can also adjust their social ties. However, the results are from a simulation model, not a human experiment, and the game is a prisoner's dilemma on a network, not a standard public goods game.

- source: Okamoto, K., & Matsumura, S. (2000). The evolution of punishment and apology: an iterated prisoner's dilemma model. *Evolutionary Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that strategies incorporating both punishment and apology signals (especially the Pavlov-like pPAS strategy) can maintain high levels of cooperation and average payoffs in the iterated prisoner's dilemma, even in the presence of errors and deceptive strategies. The average payoff in a pPAS-dominated population is nearly as high as in a fully cooperative population, and much higher than in populations dominated by tit-for-tat (TFT) or defectors. The success of pPAS depends on the costs of punishment and apology: pPAS dominates when the cost of giving punishment is low, the cost of receiving punishment is high, or the cost of giving apology is high. The model also shows that pPAS can remain stable even when there is a dominance asymmetry in costs between players. The findings suggest that punishment and apology mechanisms can evolve to support efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemmas with error, the availability of punishment (and apology) mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency, provided the costs of punishment and apology are within certain bounds. The results are most directly applicable to iterated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent but not identical to public goods games. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a positive effect if the punishment cost is not too high, the punishment is effective, and the apology cost is not too low (to prevent deception). The paper also highlights the importance of considering the cost structure of punishment and apology when making such predictions. However, the results are based on theoretical models and evolutionary simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Han, T. A., Lenaerts, T. C., Santos, F. C., & Pereira, L. M. (2022). Voluntary safety commitments provide an escape from over-regulation in AI development. *Technology in Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated two-player (and generalizable to N-player) innovation race game, unconditional punishment of risk-taking (unsafe) behavior can reduce social welfare by over-regulating and suppressing beneficial innovation when risk is low. Introducing voluntary, binding commitments to safe behavior—where only those who commit and then defect are punished—enables the system to achieve high efficiency: safety compliance prevails when it is collectively optimal, and innovation is not stifled when risk-taking is preferred. Both peer and institutional punishment mechanisms are effective if they are targeted only at commitment violators. The model shows that the efficiency of punishment (cost to punisher vs. impact on punished) modulates these effects, but the voluntary commitment mechanism robustly improves outcomes across parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms—specifically, whether punishment is unconditional or conditional on voluntary commitment—has a major impact on group efficiency in public-goods-like innovation dilemmas. For prediction tasks, the presence of voluntary, binding commitments with targeted punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to unconditional punishment, especially in environments where the collective optimum varies with risk. The model also highlights the importance of punishment cost and effectiveness, and shows that allowing players to opt out of commitment (and thus avoid punishment) can prevent over-regulation and welfare loss. These insights are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in games with similar structure, even if the setting is not a classic PGG.

- source: Amor, D. R., & Fort, J. (2011). Effects of punishment in a mobile population playing the prisoner's dilemma game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing social punishment (where cooperators punish defectors with a small probability) in a mobile PD population can transform a system that would otherwise end in universal defection into one of universal cooperation, thereby maximizing group payoff. Even a 10% probability of social punishment is sufficient in many parameter regimes. The effect is robust to some degree of nonsocial punishment, but as nonsocial punishment increases, cooperation and mean payoff decline. The paper provides explicit analytical predictions and simulation results for mean payoff in the final state, showing that social punishment increases efficiency (mean payoff), while nonsocial punishment reduces it. The effect of punishment is moderated by mobility and temptation to defect: higher values of these parameters require higher rates of social punishment to sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in pairwise PD games with mobile agents, enabling social punishment (even at low rates) can dramatically increase group efficiency (mean payoff), often shifting the system from all-defection (zero payoff) to all-cooperation (maximum payoff). The effect is robust to moderate levels of nonsocial punishment, but high nonsocial punishment or harsh environments (high mobility, high temptation to defect) can negate the benefits. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (pairwise PD, mobile or spatially structured populations, punishment with cost:impact ratio of 1:3), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. However, the results are for pairwise PD, not n-person public goods games, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, though qualitative patterns are likely similar.

- source: Yan, F., Chen, X. J., Qiu, Z. P., & Szolnoki, A. (2021). Cooperator driven oscillation in a time-delayed feedback-evolving game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment (probabilistic detection and fine for defectors) can stabilize cooperation and maintain the common resource at sustainable levels, but only if the product of detection probability and fine (pβ) is sufficiently large relative to the cost of cooperation or the reinvestment rate. When punishment is strong, the system converges to a stable, efficient state with high cooperation and resource sustainability. When punishment is weak, defectors dominate and the resource is depleted. If cooperator reinvestment is proportional to endowment and there is a time delay in environmental feedback, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations in cooperation and resource levels, especially when punishment is moderate and resource growth is not too fast or too slow. The critical threshold for punishment effectiveness and the impact of time delay are analytically characterized. The model provides explicit conditions under which punishment improves or fails to improve group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how institutional punishment (with known cost and effectiveness) affects group efficiency in a common-pool resource game with feedback dynamics. The results are most relevant for adjacent PGG-like environments with renewable resources and environmental feedback. The model gives explicit formulas for the stationary group payoff/resource level as a function of punishment parameters, player count, resource growth, and reinvestment structure. For prediction, the key insight is that increasing punishment effectiveness (higher pβ) generally increases efficiency up to a threshold, above which full cooperation and sustainability are achieved. However, if punishment is too weak, efficiency remains low. Time delay in feedback can destabilize the system and induce oscillations, but does not always reduce average efficiency. The findings are most transferable to settings with institutional punishment, renewable resources, and feedback between group action and group payoff.

- source: Perry, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2020). Foresight in a Game of Leadership. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a two-player leadership/inspection game, standard rational or learning protocols do not support the emergence of punishment or cooperation, leading to inefficient outcomes (no production, no inspection). However, if leaders use foresight (weighting future payoffs and anticipating subordinates' best responses), then punishment and production can become stable equilibria, resulting in higher payoffs for both leader and group (i.e., higher efficiency). Selective imitation can also support this outcome if subordinates use best response. The effect depends on the weight placed on future payoffs (the foresight parameter) and the cost-benefit structure of punishment and production.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated or multi-round games where leaders (punishers) value future payoffs and anticipate others' responses, punishment can increase group efficiency by enabling cooperation/production that would not occur otherwise. The effect is sensitive to the cost of punishment, the benefit of production, and the degree of foresight. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in games with forward-looking or strategic agents (or mechanisms that mimic foresight/selective imitation) can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of punishment is not too high relative to the benefit. However, in the absence of such mechanisms, punishment may not emerge and efficiency remains low.

- source: Ezeigbo, I. C. (2017). How costly punishment, diversity, and density of connectivity influence cooperation in a biological network. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study uses agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks (18 players, 1000 rounds per game) to examine how costly punishment, diversity, and network connectivity affect cooperation and average payoff. Costly punishment (paying 1 unit to reduce another's payoff by 4 units) reduces defection but also significantly reduces average payoff compared to no-punishment conditions. Decreasing network connectivity (lower density of connections) increases average payoff when punishment is absent, but not when punishment is present. Diversity (randomizing connections and neighbors) can promote cooperation, but only in synergy with low connectivity. The main efficiency result is that costly punishment, while reducing defection, comes at a substantial cost to group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like network games reduces group efficiency (average payoff), even though it reduces defection. The effect is robust across different network densities and diversity conditions. If the control (no-punishment) game has higher efficiency, enabling punishment is likely to lower efficiency, unless other mechanisms (like diversity and low connectivity) are also present and interact synergistically. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in networked social dilemmas with similar payoff structures.

- source: Libois, F. (2022). Success and failure of communities managing natural resources: Static and dynamic inefficiencies. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
