  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that introducing sanctions (punishment) can increase efficiency in CPR games by enabling the enforcement of rules that prevent over-extraction (static inefficiency) and/or promote conservation (dynamic inefficiency). The effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the strength of the sanctioning mechanism relative to the incentives to deviate from cooperation. When sanctions are strong enough, the first-best (fully efficient) outcome can be achieved. With limited sanctions, only partial improvements are possible, and sometimes increasing the resource's value or growth rate can make enforcement harder, reducing efficiency. The model also shows that the number of players, extraction cost, and inequality among players all moderate the effect of sanctions on efficiency. In particular, more players or greater inequality can make conservation harder to enforce, while higher extraction costs can make it easier. The model provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in CPR-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment depends on the interplay between the sanctioning technology (maximum enforceable sanction), the number of players, the resource's growth rate, extraction cost, and initial value. If sanctions are strong enough relative to the incentives to deviate, enabling rules can be enforced and efficiency increases. If not, efficiency may not improve, or may even decrease if resource value/growth increases incentives to defect. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the logic of how punishment affects efficiency is highly transferable. The paper is especially informative for settings where the punishment mechanism is exogenous and bounded, and where resource dynamics (growth, conservation) are important. For prediction, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on whether the sanctioning parameters cross key thresholds relative to the game's other parameters.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2025). Cooperation in temporary partnerships. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random matching and temporary partnerships, combining direct punishment (within partnerships) and cyclical community punishment (across partnerships) can support full cooperation (efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, but only for sufficiently patient players (high discount factor). The milder cyclical punishment is less effective at deterring deviations in equilibrium (requires higher patience) but more effective at incentivizing punishment off-equilibrium (punishment is less costly, so more credible). In contrast, the classic grim punishment can sustain cooperation for less patient players when the temptation payoff is low, but fails when temptation is high. The length of partnerships (number of repeated rounds with the same partner) affects these thresholds: longer partnerships make cooperation harder to sustain for impatient players but make punishment more credible for patient players. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how the structure of repeated interactions (number of rounds per partnership, random rematching frequency), the type of punishment available (direct, community, grim), and player patience (discount factor) interact to determine whether punishment can sustain efficient (fully cooperative) outcomes. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling direct punishment (via temporary partnerships) can increase the set of conditions under which efficiency is sustainable, but only if players are sufficiently patient. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency depends on the interplay between the temptation payoff, the cost/structure of punishment, and the frequency of rematching. The results are most directly applicable to adjacent settings (repeated PD with random matching), but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or lab data, but its explicit equilibrium conditions and comparative statics are informative for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency.

- source: Leimar, O. (1997). Repeated games: A state space approach. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games like the IPD and dominance games, the ability to punish can support the evolutionary stability of cooperative strategies, leading to higher efficiency (i.e., higher expected payoffs, closer to full cooperation) when the probability of continuation is high and punishment is sufficiently severe and/or effective. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is parameter-dependent: if the probability of continuation is too low or punishment is too costly/ineffective, cooperation cannot be sustained and efficiency is low. The analysis also shows that there are many possible stable strategies, some of which are more efficient than others, and that the structure of punishment (e.g., how many rounds of mutual defection follow a defection) affects the range of stability and efficiency. In dominance games, punishment by a dominant can enforce resource control and higher efficiency for the dominant, but alternative equilibria exist where the subdominant prevails. In alternating PD, reciprocal strategies can be stable and efficient if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high enough. The findings are theoretical and based on formal analysis of payoffs and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games depends on game design dimensions such as the probability of continuation (analogous to number of rounds), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the structure of the game (simultaneous vs. alternating moves, role asymmetry). It shows that punishment can increase efficiency by supporting cooperation, but only when parameters are favorable (e.g., high continuation probability, effective punishment). The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, one should expect punishment to improve efficiency in repeated games when the game is long enough and punishment is not too costly or weak, but that the effect can be neutral or negative if these conditions are not met. The paper also highlights that many different punishment strategies can be stable, so the specific implementation of punishment matters. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in repeated, two-player, payoff-based games with punishment.

- source: Laclau, M., & Tomala, T. (2017). Repeated games with public deterministic monitoring. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper provides a general theoretical framework for understanding how punishment and reward mechanisms can be used to sustain efficient outcomes in repeated games with public monitoring. It shows that the set of sustainable payoffs is characterized by admissibility and joint rationality, and that explicit punishment phases (using approachability) can deter deviations. However, the efficiency achievable in equilibrium is limited by the monitoring structure: if deviations are not detectable or not attributable, efficiency may be strictly less than the social optimum. The paper gives explicit constructions of equilibrium strategies with main (cooperation), punishment, compensation, and reward phases, and proves that for sufficiently patient players, any payoff in the interior of the admissible and jointly rational set can be supported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the efficiency of repeated games with punishment, especially in settings with imperfect public monitoring. It provides a theoretical upper bound on efficiency as a function of the monitoring structure and the ability to implement punishment phases. The explicit construction of punishment and reward phases shows how punishment can be used to sustain cooperation, but also highlights that efficiency is limited by the possibility of undetectable or unattributable deviations. For the prediction task, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends critically on the monitoring structure: when monitoring is sufficiently informative, punishment can support high efficiency; when it is not, efficiency is bounded away from the optimum. The results are general and apply to a wide class of repeated games, including but not limited to public goods games.

- source: Mondal, S. S., Pathak, M., & Chakraborty, S. (2022). Reward versus punishment: averting the tragedy of the commons in eco-evolutionary dynamics. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can prevent the tragedy of the commons in a dynamic resource game, but their effects on efficiency differ. Sufficiently strong punishment leads to a stable equilibrium with all cooperators and a fully replete resource, achieving the social optimum (maximum possible group payoff). In contrast, even with strong reward, some defectors persist, so the group does not reach the maximum efficiency. The model also shows that the effect of punishment or reward depends on the initial state and the intensity of the intervention, and that bistability (multiple possible long-run outcomes) can occur. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis, and is not based on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in dynamic resource games with feedback (and by extension, in public-goods-like environments), enabling punishment (with sufficient intensity) can increase group efficiency and achieve the social optimum, while enabling reward alone cannot fully eliminate inefficiency due to persistent defectors. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is a prisoner's dilemma with resource feedback, and where punishment or reward is implemented as a direct modification of payoffs. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment should be expected to increase efficiency more than enabling reward, especially when the goal is to reach the maximum possible group payoff. However, the model is theoretical and does not address practical implementation details or empirical constraints.

- source: Aramendia, M. (2006). Asymmetric finite punishments in repeated games. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in repeated games, including social dilemmas and Cournot oligopoly, AFP strategies—where punishment is finite and asymmetric—can sustain any feasible and individually rational payoff profile when players are sufficiently patient. The punishment phase is designed so that nondeviators are always better off than the deviator, and after a finite number of periods, cooperation resumes. This approach is less restrictive than some standard equilibrium refinements and can support efficient outcomes (e.g., cartel profits) even in cases where other concepts (like WRP) cannot. The results are illustrated with examples including the prisoner's dilemma and Cournot oligopoly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that finite, asymmetric punishment strategies can sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes in repeated games, even when standard trigger or minimax punishments are not credible or too harsh. For the prediction task, it suggests that the design of the punishment phase (finite duration, asymmetry favoring nondeviators) can be critical for supporting high efficiency, especially as the number of players increases or when renegotiation-proofness is problematic. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide direct quantitative predictions for specific experimental public goods games, but they do indicate that efficiency can be maintained with appropriately designed punishment even in large groups or complex environments.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). Two ways to overcome the three social dilemmas of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with private reputation assessment, punishment can increase group efficiency and maintain stable cooperation if assessment norms are designed to (1) detect free riders, (2) justify punishment of bad actors, and (3) generously assess cooperation toward badly reputed recipients. Two main solutions are identified: one based on recipient-focused justified punishment (with generosity toward bad recipients), and another based on doer-focused justified punishment (with high cooperation efficiency required). The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c), with more generous norms required at lower efficiency. The presence of punishment, when justified and properly assessed, leads to higher average payoffs and stable cooperative regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the assessment norm and the efficiency of cooperation (b/c). If the punishment system is designed so that justified punishment is recognized and not misinterpreted (i.e., the intention is correctly communicated), and if generosity is applied in assessing cooperation toward bad recipients, then enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The benefit-to-cost ratio (mpcr analog) is a key moderator: at higher efficiency, more norms can sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence and design of punishment, the assessment norm, and the efficiency parameter are crucial for forecasting the efficiency gain from punishment.

- source: Voelkl, B. (2015). The evolution of generalized reciprocity in social interaction networks. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis demonstrates that introducing an effective penalty for unfaithfulness (selling outside the cooperative) leads to stable, efficient cooperative outcomes where all members sell to the cooperative. If the penalty is limited (not strong enough), full cooperation is unstable. When the penalty is strong (exceeds the extra profit from defection), the system converges to full cooperation, maximizing group payoff. Redistribution of penalties among faithful members further strengthens this effect, leading to a unique, stable Nash equilibrium with high efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which punishment stabilizes cooperation and maximizes group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like settings with continuous contribution and the possibility of punishment, increasing the effectiveness and/or strength of punishment increases group efficiency and stabilizes cooperation. The results are directly relevant for predicting that enabling or strengthening punishment will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is strong enough to outweigh the gains from defection. The model also highlights that redistributing penalties among cooperators can further enhance efficiency and stability. However, the results are derived from a stylized, theoretical model rather than empirical data, and the setting is a marketing cooperative with continuous contribution rather than a canonical PGG, though the structure is closely related.

- source: Isakov, A., & Rand, D. G. (2012). The Evolution of Coercive Institutional Punishment. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment can evolve and be sustained when leaders (kings) can punish subjects for low tribute, especially when subjects are intelligent (accurate learners) and update their strategies quickly relative to kings. Strong punishment leads to high tribute payments but reduces subject payoffs and overall efficiency, except in the idealized case where punishment is never actually used. The model predicts that confusion or slow learning among subjects can collectively benefit them by undermining the effectiveness of coercive punishment. Increased global awareness (subjects learning from other kingdoms) destabilizes coercion and can collapse institutional punishment. The model is robust to changes in population size and punishment strength bounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment, when used coercively in asymmetric settings, can increase compliance (tribute payment) but generally reduces group efficiency and aggregate payoffs compared to non-punishment or noncoercive equilibria. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that enabling punishment in similar asymmetric games will likely decrease efficiency unless compliance is perfect and punishment is never actually used. The effects are moderated by learning dynamics (intelligence, update speed) and the degree of information subjects have about other groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is imposed by a central authority and the 'public good' is tribute to that authority, rather than a symmetric PGG.

- source: Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A., & Sadedin, S. (2012). Evolving Righteousness in a Corrupt World. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a small benefit to punishers (such as status or a payment from cooperators) destabilizes corrupt equilibria and enables the evolution and stability of 'righteousness'—a state where cooperative punishers dominate and both corruption and defection are suppressed. Righteousness is associated with higher collective payoffs (efficiency/wellbeing) than either corruption or defection. The model demonstrates that egalitarian and harsh punishment (i.e., similar and sufficiently high penalties for defectors and corrupt punishers) are key to achieving righteousness. Power inequalities that favor corrupt punishers stabilize corruption, but removing or reducing these inequalities (and/or providing small benefits to punishers) can shift the population to righteousness. Once established, righteousness is highly stable and resistant to invasion by defectors or corrupt punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling punishment (especially when punishers receive even small additional benefits) can increase group efficiency/wellbeing compared to control conditions without punishment or with only corrupt punishment. The model suggests that the design dimensions most relevant for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment are: (1) ensuring punishers are not disadvantaged relative to cooperators (e.g., via small payments or status), (2) making punishment of defectors and corrupt punishers sufficiently harsh, and (3) minimizing power inequalities that allow corrupt punishers to avoid punishment. While the model is not an exact PGG, its structure and outcomes are highly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games, especially regarding the role of corruption, punishment severity, and benefits to punishers.

- source: Olcina, G., & Calabuig, V. (2015). COORDINATED PUNISHMENT AND THE EVOLUTION OF COOPERATION. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the long-run efficiency of a team trust game with coordinated punishment depends critically on the institutional parameters of punishment: the effectiveness of coordinated punishment (damage inflicted if enough punishers act), the cost of punishment, and the strength of peer punishment (peer pressure on non-punishers). If the effectiveness of coordinated punishment is high enough (damage parameter λ ≥ 0.5) and peer pressure is strong (peer punishment cost to defectors is high relative to cost of coordinated punishment), then the only stable long-run outcome is a fully cooperative, efficient culture with no observed punishment (because the threat is credible). If these conditions are not met, the system converges to a non-cooperative, inefficient culture. The model also shows that increasing the effectiveness of punishment, lowering its cost, or increasing peer pressure all make the efficient outcome more likely. The results are robust to the dynamics of preference transmission, and the model provides explicit boundaries for the basins of attraction of efficient vs inefficient cultures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling effective, coordinated punishment (with sufficient effectiveness and/or low enough cost) and strong peer punishment will increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, even when the baseline (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the mechanisms (threshold punishment, peer punishment, endogenous preference evolution) are highly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The explicit parameter boundaries (e.g., λ ≥ 0.5 for effectiveness, peer punishment cost vs coordinated punishment cost) can inform quantitative predictions about when punishment will be effective. The model also highlights that if punishment is not sufficiently effective or peer pressure is weak, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency. The results are most directly applicable to trust/investment games with team-based punishment, but the logic is transferable to PGG variants with threshold or coordinated punishment.

- source: Lim, I. S., & Capraro, V. (2022). A Synergy of Institutional Incentives and Networked Structures in Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems. *Ieee Transactions on Circuits and Systems Ii-express Briefs*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment and network structure can interact synergistically to promote prosocial behavior and increase group efficiency (mean payoff) in a trust game. Specifically, the combination of even modest punishment and network structure can achieve full cooperation and maximal efficiency in parameter regions where neither mechanism alone would suffice. The required punishment level to achieve full cooperation is lower in structured populations than in well-mixed populations. The mean payoff is maximized at the threshold punishment level that induces full cooperation; increasing punishment beyond this point does not further increase efficiency and may reduce it due to the cost of maintaining the incentive system. The paper provides explicit analytical boundaries for these effects as a function of the trust return (r), network degree (k), and punishment size (p).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a networked trust game (and by extension, in adjacent public-goods-like games) will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is not too costly and the network structure is not too sparse. The results suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is greater in structured populations than in well-mixed ones, and that the combination of network structure and punishment can achieve high efficiency at lower punishment levels than punishment alone. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the network structure (degree), the effectiveness and cost of punishment, and the underlying return to cooperation. The findings are most directly applicable to trust games but are likely to transfer to public goods games with similar strategic structure.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional division of labour on a tree network. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a division-of-labour game on a tree network, without sanctions, the only stable equilibrium is full defection (premierD), resulting in low efficiency. Introducing a defector sanction system (where defectors are punished with probability and severity) can make full cooperation or mixed equilibria stable, depending on the punishment parameters and cost/loss structure. As the effective punishment (probability times fine) increases, the system transitions from defection to mixed states to full cooperation, thus increasing efficiency. The premier sanction system (punishing the root node for any defection) is less effective at promoting cooperation. The model provides explicit stability conditions for each equilibrium, showing how efficiency depends on the sanction system and network structure. Adding more levels to the network (making it deeper) hinders cooperation and efficiency, while adding more parallel branches has an indeterminate effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like division-of-labour games on tree networks, enabling a defector-targeted punishment system increases group efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong (high probability and/or fine). The effect is mediated by the cost of cooperation, the loss from defection, and the network structure. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (no punishment), introducing punishment will increase efficiency if the punishment is strong enough, but the effect may be partial (mixed equilibria) if punishment is moderate. The model also predicts that deeper (more levels) networks make it harder for punishment to achieve full cooperation. These insights can inform predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings with hierarchical or supply-chain-like structure.

- source: McBride, M., Kendall, R., D'Orsogna, M. R., & Short, M. B. (2016). Crime, punishment, and evolution in an adversarial game. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, unlike standard social dilemma games (such as the public goods game), the adversarial game studied here can have multiple equilibria, including both efficient (no-crime, high-payoff) and inefficient (high-crime, low-payoff) outcomes. The presence of punishment (reporting and conviction) transforms the game from a pure social dilemma into a coordination game, where the effectiveness of punishment depends on the collective willingness to report. The Informant strategy (defectors who also report) is critical: under imitation dynamics, any presence of Informants is sufficient to reach efficiency, but under best response dynamics, Informants are necessary but not sufficient. The model shows that too severe punishment or high retaliation risk can undermine the emergence of efficient equilibria. Thus, the design of punishment institutions and the social context (e.g., support for reporting) are crucial moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like adversarial games is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment mechanism, the available strategy set (especially the presence of Informants), and the evolutionary dynamics. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate punishment severity, low retaliation risk, and the availability of strategies that combine defection and punishment. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will always improve efficiency; instead, the effect is conditional and can be negative or null if punishment is too harsh or if key strategies are unavailable. The results are most directly applicable to adversarial games with crime and reporting, but the mechanism insights may transfer to public goods games with similar punishment structures.

- source: Gámez, M., López, I., Rodríguez, C., Varga, Z., & Garay, J. (2018). Game-theoretical model for marketing cooperative in fisheries. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that effective punishment (penalty parameter β > 1) stabilizes full cooperation in a marketing cooperative, leading to the group achieving the maximum possible profit (i.e., efficiency). When punishment is limited (β = 1), cooperation is unstable and members tend to defect, reducing group profit. The model is dynamic, with payoffs and strategies evolving over time, but the key result is that sufficiently strong punishment ensures convergence to the efficient outcome. The findings are demonstrated both analytically and through simulation examples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling sufficiently strong punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, a marketing cooperative) will increase efficiency, moving the group toward the maximum possible collective payoff. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar: members choose how much to contribute to the group (by selling through the cooperative), and the group can punish defectors. The explicit analysis of payoff functions and the demonstration that efficiency is achieved only when punishment is strong enough (β > 1) are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where the punishment technology is effective and the cost/impact ratio is favorable. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a two-player donation game with peer punishment, prospect theory's cognitive distortions (overweighting small probabilities and losses) make individuals more sensitive to the threat of punishment. As a result, cooperation can become globally stable in a wide range of parameter values where, under standard expected utility theory, only defection is stable. The model shows that even when punishment is rare (due to errors), the perceived risk is high enough to deter defection and promote cooperation, thus increasing group efficiency. The cooperative non-punishing strategy (CN) becomes globally stable as punishment strength increases, while the cooperative punishing strategy (CP) is always dominated. The effect is robust to parameter changes in the prospect theory functions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the effectiveness of peer punishment in increasing efficiency depends not only on the objective game parameters (punishment cost, punishment strength, error rate) but also on the cognitive perception of risk and loss (as modeled by prospect theory). In environments where agents overweight the probability and severity of punishment, even infrequent or low-cost punishment can be sufficient to stabilize cooperation and increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that models assuming standard rationality may underestimate the efficiency gains from punishment, especially when cognitive biases are present. However, the model is limited to two-player donation games and does not directly address standard multi-player public goods games.

- source: Wang, X. L., Wu, J., Shu, G., & Li, Y. (2014). Punishment Based on Public Benefit Fund Significantly Promotes Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment funded by a public benefit fund (taxes on high earners) significantly increases both cooperation and average payoff (efficiency) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) is more effective than severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage) in raising average payoff. The effect of punishment is stronger when the temptation to defect is higher. The results are robust across square lattice and small world network topologies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with costs funded by a public benefit fund) increases group efficiency (average payoff) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, compared to no punishment. The effect is stronger for slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) than for severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage). The findings are most directly relevant to spatial PD games with local interactions, not standard PGGs, but the efficiency outcome is directly reported. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially with broad coverage and moderate intensity) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially as the temptation to defect increases.

- source: Vanderschraaf, P. (2016). In a Weakly Dominated Strategy Is Strength: Evolution of Optimality in Stag Hunt Augmented with a Punishment Option. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding a costly punishment option to a two-player Stag Hunt game (a coordination game with a payoff-dominant and a risk-dominant equilibrium) dramatically increases the basin of attraction for efficient, cooperative outcomes under evolutionary replicator dynamics. This means that, in evolutionary terms, populations are much more likely to end up in efficient, high-payoff states when punishment is possible, even though the punishment strategy is weakly dominated. The effect is strongest when the punishment inflicted on defectors is at least as large as the cost to the punisher. The results are robust across a range of parameterizations, and the paper provides both analytical and numerical (simulation) support. The findings are interpreted as theoretical support for the hypothesis that altruistic punishment can sustain cooperation and efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in two-player coordination games structurally similar to public goods games, the introduction of a costly punishment option can substantially increase the likelihood of efficient, cooperative outcomes, as measured by the basin of attraction under evolutionary dynamics. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a game with similar structure (even if punishment is costly and weakly dominated) should be expected to increase average efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the punishment inflicted is at least as large as the cost to the punisher. However, the results are derived for two-player games and evolutionary (not repeated-game) settings, so direct quantitative extrapolation to standard multi-player repeated public goods games should be done with caution. The qualitative direction of effect—punishment increases efficiency—is strongly supported.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2016). Evolution of cooperation by the introduction of the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism, where punishment is more likely and more severe when the payoff difference between punisher and target is larger, leads to a substantial increase in both cooperation and average payoff in spatial prisoner's dilemma simulations. This effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, and for different average degrees of connectivity. The model demonstrates that, contrary to some previous findings with standard peer-punishment, this form of punishment can increase group welfare (average payoff) rather than decrease it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, a punishment mechanism that is probabilistic and based on payoff differences can increase average payoff, in contrast to standard fixed-cost peer punishment which often reduces group payoff. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in adjacent game structures, especially where punishment is adaptive or conditional on payoff differences. The results suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (e.g., making it proportional to payoff differences and probabilistic) is a key moderator of its effect on efficiency. However, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution due to the difference in game structure.

- source: Nakamaru, M., Shimura, H., Kitakaji, Y., & Ohnuma, S. (2018). The effect of sanctions on the evolution of cooperation in linear division of labor. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing sanctions—especially the producer responsibility system—promotes the evolution of cooperation and increases group-level efficiency in a linear division of labor game. The actor responsibility system only promotes cooperation if monitoring and detection are sufficiently effective, which is often not the case in real-world settings. The producer responsibility system, which sanctions the generator if the manifest is not returned, is robust to low monitoring probabilities and is more effective at promoting cooperation and reducing illegal dumping. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable and shows that the three-role model captures more realistic dynamics than simpler two-role models. The findings are supported by both analytical results and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a well-designed punishment system (especially one that does not rely on costly monitoring, like the producer responsibility system) will increase group efficiency in public-goods-like environments with linear division of labor. The model is adjacent to standard PGGs but is directly relevant for settings with sequential roles and externalities. The results suggest that, for games with similar structure, enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and low monitoring requirements) should be predicted to increase efficiency relative to control. The paper also highlights the importance of the effectiveness of monitoring and the structure of the sanction system as moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical estimates of effect sizes.

- source: Liu, S. Y., Zhang, C. Y., Li, K., & Zhang, J. L. (2022). Exploring the inducement for social dilemma and cooperation promotion mechanisms in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in structured populations playing iterated prisoner's dilemma with memory-one strategies, increased randomness and heterogeneity in network topology (e.g., moving from regular lattice to scale-free networks) lead to a higher prevalence of defectors and lower collective average payoff (social welfare). The key behavioral parameter is p4, the probability of cooperating after mutual defection; low p4 is associated with 'dangerous' strategies that dominate in more random/heterogeneous networks and drive payoffs toward the minimum. The authors introduce two mechanisms: (1) enforcement, which forbids p4 < 0.3, and (2) punishment, which imposes a fine on agents with p4 < 0.3. Both mechanisms, especially with sufficient punishment intensity, eliminate dangerous strategies and raise the collective average payoff substantially, regardless of network structure. The effect is robust across random regular, ER random, and BA scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: Although the paper studies the iterated prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the structure is closely related, and the main outcome—collective average payoff as a fraction of the maximum possible—is directly analogous to efficiency. The findings provide strong evidence that introducing punishment (with sufficient intensity) in a repeated social dilemma robustly increases group efficiency, especially in environments where network structure would otherwise favor defection. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment targets low willingness to cooperate after mutual defection. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its strength: weak punishment may not eliminate low-cooperation strategies, but moderate to strong punishment does. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to network-induced defection.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). Characteristics of the evolution of cooperation by the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism—where the probability and severity of punishment are proportional to payoff differences—can increase both cooperation and the average payoff of all players in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and for varying average degrees of connectivity, though the parameter range for positive effects narrows as connectivity increases. The model avoids antisocial punishment by design. The results suggest that, with appropriate parameterization, punishment can enhance group efficiency, not just cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in environments structurally similar to spatial prisoner's dilemma games, a well-designed probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff), especially when the punishment is neither too weak nor too strong. The effect is sensitive to the network structure: as the average number of connections increases, the range of punishment parameters that yield efficiency gains becomes narrower. While the model is not a public goods game, the payoff-based outcome and the punishment mechanism are closely related, making the findings informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like settings, especially where network structure and punishment calibration are relevant.

- source: Luo, J. S., & Zhao, M. (2013). Proportional cost for punishment enhances spatial reciprocity in evolutionary games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that proportional punishment (where the cost is distributed across a proportion of the population and defectors are fined based on their payoff) enhances both cooperation and average payoff in spatial prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. As the proportion of the population bearing punishment cost (p) and the strength of punishment (beta) increase, the average payoff rises and more individuals achieve higher payoffs. The effect is robust across both game types. The paper provides detailed analysis of how payoff distributions shift with punishment parameters, showing that punishment reduces the number of individuals with very low or very high payoffs, concentrating payoffs in a higher, narrower range.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial pairwise games (PDG and SD), introducing a proportional punishment mechanism increases average payoff (efficiency) compared to the baseline without punishment. The effect is stronger as the proportion of the population involved in punishment and the punishment strength increase. While the setting is not a public goods game, the adjacent nature of the games and the explicit analysis of average payoff and its distribution make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (especially with higher coverage and strength) will increase efficiency relative to the control. However, the model is theoretical and does not address all PGG design dimensions (e.g., no chat, no reward, no explicit round structure), so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

- source: Yu, T. K., Chen, S. H., & Li, H. G. (2016). Social norms, costly punishment and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that allowing or encouraging costly punishment in a donor-recipient game increases both the size of the basin of attraction for the cooperative evolutionary stable state (CESS) and the speed at which a society converges to this high-payoff state. Under parameter settings where punishment is not too costly, the presence of punishment options increases the probability that a society will escape the non-cooperative trap and reach high efficiency. The effect is stronger when the social norm actively encourages punishment. However, in the long run, after full cooperation is achieved, continued costly punishment can reduce efficiency due to unnecessary punishment costs. The findings are based on mathematical modeling and numerical simulations, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemma environments with reputation and norm dynamics, enabling costly punishment increases the likelihood and speed of reaching high-efficiency cooperative states, especially when the social norm encourages punishment. The effect is most pronounced in 'developing' societies or initial conditions with many defectors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (especially with supportive norms) will generally increase efficiency relative to control, unless punishment is extremely costly. However, in fully cooperative steady states, punishment may reduce efficiency due to its cost. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar, so findings are likely transferable to PGG-like settings with reputation and norm evolution.

- source: Celik, G., & Peters, M. (2016). Reciprocal relationships and mechanism design. *Canadian Journal of Economics-revue Canadienne D Economique*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in environments where players cannot enforce punishments for non-cooperation, reciprocal contracting (where players can make commitments contingent on others' commitments) allows for a larger set of efficient outcomes than centralized mechanism design with the same punishment constraints. The key mechanism is that players can use contract offers to signal private information, which can lower the reservation utility of deviators and thus make more efficient outcomes individually rational and incentive compatible. The analysis is theoretical, with the Cournot duopoly serving as a worked example. The results show that, under certain conditions (private values, single crossing, transferable utility), the incentive compatibility constraints can be simplified, and efficient outcomes can be implemented with appropriate transfer schemes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that, even in the absence of enforceable punishment, mechanisms that allow for reciprocal commitments and information revelation can achieve higher efficiency than mechanisms that do not. For the downstream prediction task, the main implication is that the absence of punishment does not necessarily preclude efficient outcomes if the game design allows for reciprocal contracting and signaling. However, the results are derived in a general mechanism design context (e.g., Cournot duopoly, collusion, marriage, etc.) rather than in standard public goods games. The findings suggest that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games but with richer contracting and signaling possibilities, efficiency can be higher than predicted by models that only consider enforceable punishment or centralized mechanisms. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency changes when punishment is enabled or disabled in standard PGGs, but it does clarify the theoretical boundaries of what is possible in related environments.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Indirect reciprocity provides only a narrow margin of efficiency for costly punishment. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in models of indirect reciprocity, strategies that use costly punishment (punishing defectors at a cost to oneself) are almost always less efficient than strategies that simply withhold cooperation from defectors. Costly punishment can only be the most efficient equilibrium in a very small region of parameter space, requiring fine-tuned values of benefit, cost, punishment cost, punishment impact, and reputation accuracy. In most cases, the highest group payoff (efficiency) is achieved by not using costly punishment. The results are robust to errors in reputation assignment and action execution, as long as these errors are not too frequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity settings (which are adjacent to public goods games), enabling costly punishment will almost always reduce group efficiency compared to not enabling punishment, except in a very narrow set of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this suggests that unless the game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment impact, benefit/cost ratio, reputation accuracy) are in the narrow region identified, enabling punishment should be expected to lower efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to indirect reciprocity games with binary reputation and second-order norms, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar structure.

- source: Annen, K. (2011). Lies and slander: truth-telling in repeated matching games with private monitoring. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement mechanisms, where players can report on others' behavior and the group can punish defectors, can support efficient cooperation in repeated matching games with private monitoring. When experience-sharing (reporting) is exogenous and truthful, cooperation can be supported for any group size, and the minimal discount factor required does not depend on the number of players. When experience-sharing is endogenous (players choose what to report), personal punishments are needed to incentivize truth-telling, which reduces the severity of community punishment and increases the minimal discount factor required for cooperation. The ability to sustain efficient cooperation thus depends on the number of players and the matching rule in the endogenous case, but for large enough groups, community enforcement remains more effective than personal enforcement alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling community punishment (via experience-sharing/reporting) increases the efficiency of repeated matching games with private monitoring, especially in large groups. However, the efficiency gain is moderated by the need to incentivize truthful reporting, which introduces personal punishments that reduce the overall effectiveness of community enforcement. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is more positive in larger groups and when experience-sharing is exogenous or can be made truthful at low cost. When experience-sharing is endogenous, the efficiency gain from punishment is still present but smaller, and the minimal discount factor required for cooperation is higher. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent, especially on group size and the structure of information sharing.

- source: Uchida, S., Sasaki, T., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2024). Evolution of Pay-It-Forward in the Presence of the Temptation to Free-Ride. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a donation game with institutional punishment, the evolution of cooperation and pay-it-forward strategies is strongly affected by the cognitive model of the players. Under EUT, only strong punishment (high fine or high probability) can sustain cooperation, and pay-it-forward strategies are not stable. Under PT, even weak or infrequent punishment is sufficient to deter free-riding, because players overweight small probabilities of being punished. This leads to the emergence and stability of pay-it-forward strategies and higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population. The model shows bi-stability, with both cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria possible depending on parameters and initial conditions, but overall, punishment increases efficiency more under PT than under EUT.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games (here, a donation game) will increase group efficiency, especially when players are subject to prospect-theoretic biases. The effect is more pronounced when punishment is mild or infrequent, as real human players may overweight the risk of being punished. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the payoff-based outcomes are directly analyzed. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (even with low probability or low severity) should be expected to increase efficiency, particularly if the control game has low efficiency due to free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of psychological factors (probability weighting) in moderating the effect of punishment.

- source: Cai, G. S., & Kock, N. (2009). An evolutionary game theoretic perspective on e-collaboration: The collaboration effort and media relativeness. *European Journal of Operational Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player e-collaboration games, introducing or increasing social punishment for defection increases the likelihood of full cooperation, which is Pareto efficient and maximizes group payoff. When punishment is low, defection or asymmetric cooperation is stable; when punishment exceeds a threshold (typically half the cost of cooperation), full cooperation becomes the unique stable Nash equilibrium. This result holds in both discrete and continuous effort models. The paper also shows that the required punishment to induce cooperation decreases as collaboration efficiency increases. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium characterizations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling or increasing punishment in a two-player, public-goods-like game will increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong relative to the cost of cooperation. The analytic results specify the threshold at which punishment changes the equilibrium from defection to cooperation. While the model is for two players and not a standard PGG, the structure is closely related, and the findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in small-group cooperation games. The results are most applicable when the punishment mechanism is salient, effective, and the cost/benefit structure is similar to the model.

- source: Povey, R. (2014). Punishment and the potency of group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that embedding a punishment system within a group selection framework reduces the evolutionary success of altruism. While punishment can statically deter selfishness and improve immediate social welfare, it simultaneously weakens the group selection mechanism that would otherwise promote the evolution of intrinsic altruism. This leads to a lower equilibrium level of altruism and, in the long run, can reduce dynamic social welfare. The model shows that the requirements for group selection to sustain altruism become more stringent when punishment is available, and that the use of punishment may have negative dynamic welfare implications, even if it improves static outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment in a group environment may increase static efficiency (short-term group payoff) but can reduce dynamic efficiency (long-run welfare) by undermining the evolution of intrinsic cooperation. The model is not a standard public goods game but is adjacent, focusing on sequential punishment and group selection. The findings imply that, in environments where intrinsic motivation is important for long-run efficiency, introducing punishment may have ambiguous or even negative effects on overall efficiency, especially over evolutionary or cultural timescales. The paper is most informative for settings where the persistence of cooperation and welfare over time is of interest, rather than immediate, one-shot efficiency gains.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional linear division of labour of finite roles. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a linear division-of-labor game, the introduction of a defector sanction system (punishing defectors with a fine, with some probability of detection) can stabilize the all-cooperation equilibrium and thus maximize group efficiency, provided the expected punishment is sufficiently high relative to the cost of cooperation. The first role sanction system (punishing only the first role regardless of who defects) is less effective and only promotes cooperation when the cost of cooperation decreases downstream. The benefit from cooperation does not affect the stability of cooperation or efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which each equilibrium is stable, showing that punishment can transform a system from one where defection is stable (and efficiency is low) to one where cooperation and high efficiency are stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical support for predicting that, in public-goods-like environments with sequential or asymmetric roles, introducing a punishment mechanism that targets defectors (with sufficient expected punishment) will increase group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of roles (player count) and the cost structure, provided the expected punishment exceeds the maximum cooperation cost. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment system (who is punished, detection probability, and fine size) is critical for achieving efficiency gains. The results are most directly applicable to settings with sequential dependencies and asymmetric roles, but the logic may transfer to some PGG variants. The model does not address repeated finite rounds, communication, or stochastic group sizes, so predictions should be cautious if those features are present.

- source: Ito, H., & Yoshimura, J. (2015). Social penalty promotes cooperation in a cooperative society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a social penalty (punishment) for non-cooperative actions in already-cooperative societies universally increases cooperation and group welfare in theoretical models of the hawk-dove and prisoner's dilemma games. The penalty reduces the payoff of non-cooperators, shifting the equilibrium toward more cooperation. The effect is robust to the size of the penalty: larger penalties can lead to full cooperation. The results are generalized to non-cooperative games, with the claim that social penalties always promote cooperation and never reduce it. The paper also discusses the implications for real-world legal systems and public goods provision, but does not model or simulate public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing or increasing punishment for non-cooperation will increase group efficiency and average payoff in non-cooperative games, including those adjacent to public goods games. The results are derived analytically and show that the effect of punishment is always non-negative for cooperation and group welfare. However, the models are not public goods games per se, and do not address multi-player, repeated, or more complex institutional features. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where punishment is externally imposed and not subject to strategic choice by players. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, especially as punishment becomes more severe or certain.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2004). Altruism, altruistic punishment and social investment. *Acta Biotheoretica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to examine the effects of altruistic punishment on aggregate wealth (efficiency) in artificial societies. It finds that while punishment increases generosity and norm compliance, it reduces aggregate wealth compared to societies without punishment, unless the punished behaviors trigger synergistic benefits for society. The effect is observed in both structured (with division of labor) and unstructured societies, though structured societies are more susceptible to social control via punishment. The study concludes that punishment is a strong mechanism for norm enforcement but is not, by itself, efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides evidence that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, an agent-based resource-sharing simulation) increases norm compliance and generosity but reduces group efficiency (aggregate wealth), unless there are additional synergistic benefits from the enforced behaviors. The negative effect on efficiency is robust to variations in social structure and punishment intensity. Thus, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper suggests that, in the absence of explicit synergistic returns, punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where punishment is costly and does not directly generate group-level surplus.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2018). Evolution of global cooperation and ethnocentrism in group-structured populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops an analytical model of a group-structured population where individuals can adopt one of four strategies (global cooperator, ethnocentrist, traitor, egoist) and interact via a simplified prisoner's dilemma. Without any promoting mechanism, global cooperation is never favored by selection, regardless of migration pattern or group number; ethnocentrism is promoted, especially by global migration. When leader punishment is introduced (leaders punish defectors), global cooperation can be favored and can become more abundant than ethnocentrism if the punishment fine is sufficiently high. Efficiency-oriented leader punishment is more effective than equality-oriented punishment. The number of groups, migration rate, and mutation rate all moderate these effects. The main outcomes are derived as conditions for strategy abundance, which are directly linked to group payoff and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in group-structured prisoner's dilemma-like environments, punishment mechanisms (especially leader/third-party punishment with sufficient fines) can transform a setting where global cooperation is not favored into one where it is. The effect is more pronounced for efficiency-oriented punishment. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the payoff-based selection conditions are directly interpretable as efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment, the cost/fine of punishment, and the structure of the population (number of groups, migration/mutation rates) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. In the absence of punishment, efficiency is low (global cooperation not favored); with strong leader punishment, efficiency can be high (global cooperation favored).

- source: KANDORI, M. (1992). SOCIAL NORMS AND COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that informal sanctions (punishment by community members) can support efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, provided there is sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized labels/reputation). Under public observability, any efficient and individually rational outcome can be sustained as an equilibrium, regardless of population size or matching rule. With only private experience and no information processing, cooperation (and thus efficiency) can sometimes be sustained via a 'contagious equilibrium,' but this is fragile and breaks down in large populations. Introducing local information processing (labels/reputation) allows for robust, straightforward equilibria that sustain efficiency for any population size and matching rule. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and conditions for when these results hold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the presence of a punishment mechanism, when combined with sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized reputation/labels), enables the group to achieve efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, even with random matching and large populations. For the prediction task, this suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency to the maximum feasible level, provided the game design allows for sufficient information about past actions (e.g., public histories, visible labels, or reputation). If information is very limited (players only know their own history), punishment is less effective, and efficiency gains are limited, especially as group size increases. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes but gives clear qualitative predictions about the moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Brandt, U. S., & Svendsen, G. T. (2019). How robust is the welfare state when facing open borders? An evolutionary game-theoretic model. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly punishment (by Willing Punishers) is essential for maintaining high average welfare in a society vulnerable to free riding. If the proportion of punishers falls below a threshold, defectors proliferate and average welfare collapses. Timely recognition of the threat and sufficient support (redistributive rewards) for punishers can reverse this trend and restore high welfare. The model identifies threshold effects: there is a minimum level of support for punishment required to sustain cooperation and high efficiency. Delays in shifting support to punishers or insufficient rewards for them lead to welfare loss or collapse. The model also shows cyclical dynamics, with periods of high and low welfare depending on the political narrative and group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The model suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only if punishment is sufficiently incentivized and timely. The effect is not automatic: if support for punishers is too low or delayed, efficiency gains are not realized and may even be reversed. The findings support the prediction that punishment will increase efficiency when the game design ensures adequate support for punishers and rapid response to defection. The model also highlights the importance of migration/inflow of defectors and the political/institutional context (ingroup favouritism) as moderators. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct mapping to specific experimental game parameters.

- source: Ubeda, F., & Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A. (2011). POWER AND CORRUPTION. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Prisoner's Dilemma with costly punishment, allowing for corrupt punishers and power asymmetries enables the evolution of stable states with both cooperation and corruption. When the social cost of punishing a punisher (q+d) is less than the cost of punishing a civilian (p), and within a certain range, a mixed equilibrium emerges with both cooperating civilians and corrupt policers. In this equilibrium, the population mean payoff (efficiency) is higher than in the all-defect equilibrium, and can approach the fully cooperative payoff as power asymmetries increase. The model demonstrates that costly punishment can increase both cooperation and efficiency, especially when punishers are protected by power asymmetries. The likelihood of reaching the efficient equilibrium depends on the relative costs and effectiveness of punishment. The results challenge the view that costly punishment is always inefficient, showing that under the right conditions, it can benefit the group even when punishers are themselves corrupt.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the structure of punishment costs and power asymmetries. If punishment is costly but power asymmetries exist (punishers are less vulnerable to punishment or punishment is more effective against non-punishers), then enabling punishment can increase efficiency above the control (no-punishment) baseline.
