The model suggests that the efficiency gain is larger when the punishment of defectors is strong and the punishment of punishers is weak. The findings are most directly applicable to Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the logic is relevant for public goods games with similar punishment structures. The paper does not address all game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, communication), but provides direct, mechanistic insight into how punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and power asymmetries moderate the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Watve, M. G., Damle, A., Ganguly, B., Kale, A., & Dahanukar, N. (2011). Blackmailing: the keystone in the human mating system. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a hidden-strategy game modeling human mating, the combination of punishment (social sanctions) and policing (gossip/observation), when stabilized by the possibility of blackmailing, leads to the stable coexistence of monogamy and other strategies, with monogamy maintained at high frequency. This results in higher average fitness (efficiency) for the population. If punishment is present but blackmailing is not possible, policing collapses due to second-order free-riding, and monogamy cannot be maintained. Thus, the introduction of punishment (with blackmailing) increases efficiency relative to the baseline with no punishment or with punishment alone. The model is robust to variations in the opportunity for blackmailing and to gender differences in payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (with sufficient severity and the possibility of side-payments/blackmailing) in a public-goods-like environment increases group efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The model is not an exact PGG but is structurally analogous, with cooperation (monogamy) as the group-beneficial act and defection (EPM) as the selfish act. The results suggest that punishment alone may not be sufficient unless stabilized by additional incentives (e.g., blackmailing), but when these are present, efficiency is higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costly and subject to second-order free-riding, and where additional mechanisms (like blackmailing) can offset these costs.

- source: Barron, D., & Guo, Y. N. (2021). THE USE AND MISUSE OF COORDINATED PUNISHMENTS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in relational/trust games with coordinated punishment, the possibility of extortion (i.e., agents threatening to trigger punishment unless paid, regardless of their effort) can completely undermine cooperation and efficiency. In the baseline model with extortion, equilibrium effort and surplus are zero. However, if institutions can tie the ability to trigger punishment (leverage) to actual effort—such as through public signals of effort or strong dyadic relationships—then positive effort and efficiency can be restored, though often at the cost of rents to agents or the need for strong relationships. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore not universally positive: it depends critically on whether misuse/extortion is possible and whether institutional features can prevent it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games is highly sensitive to the possibility of extortion and the institutional context. If punishment can be misused (e.g., via action-contingent threats), enabling punishment may reduce efficiency to zero. However, if the game design includes mechanisms that tie punishment power to actual cooperative behavior (e.g., public effort signals, strong dyadic relationships), punishment can support high efficiency. For prediction, this means that the presence or absence of extortion opportunities, and the presence of institutional safeguards, are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. Game design dimensions that affect the ability to make credible threats, the observability of effort, and the structure of relationships are especially important.

- source: Liu, M. M., Ma, Y. H., Liu, Z. Y., & You, X. M. (2017). An IUR evolutionary game model on the patent cooperate of Shandong China. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that higher punishment for negative cooperation (defection) increases both the rate of positive cooperation and the average payoff (\bar{U}) in the network, especially when punishment exceeds the development cost. When punishment is low, negative cooperation is more attractive and average payoffs are lower. Knowledge spillover reduces cooperation and can increase payoffs for defectors, but if punishment is high, spillover has less effect. The most efficient and cooperative outcome is achieved when profits are split equally between the two sides. The findings are based on simulation of the theoretical model, not on lab or field experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that, in a networked, repeated, two-population cooperation game with punishment, increasing the punishment for negative cooperation increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when punishment exceeds the cost of cooperation. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related. The results suggest that, for similar networked cooperation games, enabling or increasing punishment should be predicted to increase efficiency, particularly when the punishment is substantial relative to the cost of cooperation. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with network structure, two-sided cooperation, and payoff sharing, but the qualitative direction is likely to generalize to PGG-like environments.

- source: Friedman, D., & Singh, N. (2004). Negative reciprocity - The coevolution of memes and genes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that negative reciprocity (punishment) can transform a standard social dilemma (Prisoner's Dilemma) from an inefficient all-defect equilibrium to a coordination game with a Pareto efficient all-cooperate equilibrium, provided the punishment motive is strong enough. However, individual willingness to punish is undermined by evolutionary free-rider problems, as the costs are borne individually but the benefits are shared. The key finding is that group-level enforcement of punishment norms (memes), using low-cost sanctions such as shunning or status reduction, can sustain the optimal level of negative reciprocity and thus maximize group fitness. The model predicts that the efficiency of cooperation increases with the strength and enforcement of punishment norms, and that these norms will coevolve with individual capacities and environmental hostility. The comparative statics suggest that efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly, more effective, and more strongly enforced by group norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when group-enforced) increases efficiency in social dilemmas, compared to control conditions without punishment. The model is based on a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, but the authors argue that the logic extends to n-person games like public goods games. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the rigor of group enforcement are key moderators of the efficiency gain from punishment. The model also predicts that efficiency gains from punishment are larger when punishment is less costly and more effective, and when group norms for enforcement are strong. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct evidence from public goods games.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2010). Honor among thieves: Cooperation as a strategic response to functional unpleasantness. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and high group efficiency can emerge and persist in populations where all individuals are fundamentally self-interested and prefer to cheat, as long as some are willing to punish cheaters and some are strategic enough to cooperate when the risk of being punished is high. The presence of 'Strategic Unpleasant' types—who punish cheaters and cooperate only to avoid punishment—can lead to equilibria where most or all players cooperate, resulting in high average payoffs (efficiency). The model provides explicit payoff matrices and equilibrium conditions showing that, under reasonable parameter values (punishment not too costly, punishment more aversive than being cheated, etc.), punishment enables efficient outcomes. The analysis also shows that if punishment is too costly or ineffective, cooperation and efficiency break down.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency, provided that punishment is not excessively costly and is sufficiently effective to deter cheating. The model clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost/impact structure of punishment and the strategic response of players. If the control (no-punishment) game is dominated by cheaters (low efficiency), introducing punishment—even by self-interested types—can shift the equilibrium to high efficiency. The paper's explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions can be used to inform quantitative predictions, especially regarding the importance of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the strategic incentives for cooperation.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2008). Functional unpleasantness: the evolutionary logic of righteous resentment. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can be sustained in populations where some individuals are willing to punish cheaters, even if those punishers are themselves not cooperative. The evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the relative costs of punishing, being punished, and being cheated. When the cost of punishing is less than the cost of being cheated, punishment can support a stable mix of cooperators and punishers, increasing overall efficiency compared to a world with only cheaters. If punishment is too costly, cheaters dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the existence of punishment mechanisms—regardless of the punishers' motives—can improve group welfare and efficiency in collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in collective action games (including public goods game variants) can increase efficiency, as measured by average group payoff, provided the cost of punishing is not too high. The findings suggest that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment. While the model is not an exact public goods game, its structure and payoff logic are closely related, and its results are directly relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency. The paper does not address empirical game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, or communication, but it does provide strong evidence that the cost and effectiveness of punishment are critical for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2016). The evolutionary advantage of limited network knowledge. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with gossip-based punishment, the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximize group efficiency/fitness) depends on both the structure of the social network and the precision of individuals' knowledge about that structure. When all individuals have precise knowledge of the network, it is harder to sustain full cooperation because some individuals can be identified as weakly connected and thus are tempting targets for defection. When individuals have only coarse knowledge (e.g., know only the average neighborhood size), it is easier to sustain full cooperation, as targeting is not possible. The minimum discount factor required to sustain cooperation is lower under coarse knowledge than under precise knowledge, making full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) more robust. The model thus predicts that limited network knowledge facilitates the enforcement of cooperation through punishment, leading to higher group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the information structure—specifically, the precision of network knowledge. In environments where individuals have only coarse knowledge of the network, punishment is more effective at sustaining full cooperation and thus maximizing efficiency. If a public goods game or similar environment allows only limited information about the network or about others' positions, the model predicts a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency. Conversely, if players have precise knowledge of the network, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency may be reduced. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers clear theoretical mechanisms and comparative statics relevant to prediction.

- source: Vincent, J. R. (2007). Spatial dynamics, social norms, and the opportunity of the commons. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that economic incentives—specifically, the use of sanctions (punishments for non-cooperation and rewards for cooperation)—are critical for achieving efficient and sustainable management of common pool resources. Theoretical models (e.g., repeated games, evolutionary game theory) and empirical case studies (e.g., Ostrom 1990) show that monitoring and enforcement mechanisms enable groups to avoid the tragedy of the commons, leading to higher group profits and resource stocks. The author is skeptical that social norms alone, without sanctions, can achieve similar efficiency, especially when rapid or large-scale behavioral change is needed. The paper also notes that public goods (as opposed to common pool resources) face similar free-rider problems and require external enforcement (e.g., taxes, quotas) to achieve efficient provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency, group payoff, and resource sustainability, especially when compared to environments without such mechanisms. The presence of monitoring and enforcement is highlighted as a key moderator: systems with sanctions are much more likely to achieve efficient outcomes than those relying on norms alone. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates but offers strong theoretical and qualitative support for the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment/sanctions should be coded as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially in settings analogous to common pool resources.

- source: Podobnik, B., Gabor, A. M., & Kirbis, I. S. (2019). Scale-free growth of human society based on cooperation and altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that the system's growth rate (interpreted as efficiency or collective payoff) increases monotonically with the level of cooperation, which itself increases with the level of punishment. There is a threshold: if punishment is too high, the system can collapse (negative growth), but within reasonable bounds, higher punishment leads to more cooperation and higher efficiency. Empirical data suggest that less corrupt (more cooperative) countries tend to have higher fines, supporting the model's prediction that higher punishment is associated with higher cooperation. Survey data indicate the perceived fine needed to induce compliance, but do not directly measure payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling or increasing punishment increases efficiency (system growth rate), up to a point. The model is not a direct PGG but is closely related, and the main mechanism—punishment increasing cooperation, which increases collective payoff—transfers to PGG settings. The paper does not provide empirical estimates of effect size, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment increases efficiency unless set excessively high. The model also highlights that the effect of punishment depends on the baseline level of cooperation and the distribution of punishment sensitivity in the population.

- source: Nasrallah, W. F., & Cheaib, K. A. (2016). An equilibrium model of how regulative and normative institutions influence micro-economic and organizational behavior. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The theta-pi model demonstrates that both punishment (regulative institutions) and empathy (normative institutions) can increase the efficiency of economic interactions by expanding the set of transactions that are mutually beneficial and deterring predation. The model provides explicit conditions under which markets are efficient (all cooperate), collapse (all defect or abstain), or are path-dependent/oscillatory. Punishment (pi) is especially effective in high-temptation, low-sucker-loss environments, while empathy (theta) is more effective when losses from being cheated are high. When some actors are exempt from punishment (elites), efficiency declines and can collapse if the exempt group is too large. Overlapping institutions and partial coverage can lead to complex dynamics, including cycles of cooperation and predation. The model quantifies the marginal value of increasing institutional strength and provides phase diagrams for different transaction types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (increasing pi) in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency, especially when the temptation to defect is high and the cost to victims is not overwhelming. The model clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its universality and the structure of payoffs, and that partial or selective enforcement can undermine efficiency. The findings suggest that, all else equal, increasing the probability or effectiveness of punishment will shift the equilibrium from market collapse or low efficiency to high efficiency, provided the institutional coverage is broad. The model also highlights that empathy-based mechanisms can substitute for punishment in some environments, but are less effective when temptation is high and losses are low. For prediction tasks, the model's phase diagrams and threshold conditions can be mapped to game design dimensions such as punishment cost, effectiveness, and universality, supporting the expectation that enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like games.

- source: Wang, S. X., Cao, M., & Chen, X. J. (2025). Optimally Combined Incentive for Cooperation Among Interacting Agents in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal protocol for combining reward and punishment to promote cooperation is time-invariant and depends on the cost-to-benefit ratio and the initial and target cooperation levels. When the initial cooperation level is high, pure punishment is most cost-effective; when it is low, pure reward is more efficient; and for intermediate cases, a specific mix of reward and punishment minimizes the total cost. The minimal cost protocol is analytically derived for both well-mixed (complete graph) and regular network populations. The results are robust across different network structures. The findings are directly relevant to predicting the efficiency of institutional punishment and/or reward interventions in repeated social dilemma settings, though the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of punishment (and reward) interventions in repeated social dilemma games, especially when the outcome of interest is the cost required to achieve a target level of cooperation. While the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game, the structure and logic of institutional incentives are closely related. The results suggest that the efficiency of punishment depends critically on the initial cooperation level, the cost-to-benefit ratio, and the network structure. The paper does not report group payoff or surplus in the standard PGG sense, but its focus on cumulative cost as a measure of efficiency is highly relevant for predicting the cost-effectiveness of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Ntemos, K., Pikramenos, G., & Kalouptsidis, N. (2022). Dynamic Information Sharing and Punishment Strategies. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic information sharing games with two agents, cooperation (information sharing) is not sustainable in finite-horizon settings, but can be sustained in infinite-horizon settings using punishment strategies (CGT). The equilibrium region where cooperation is optimal is characterized, and its size increases as the cost of sharing decreases or the discount factor increases. Theoretical results show that the maximal equilibrium region is unique and can be computed iteratively. Simulations confirm that lower costs and more informative observations lead to larger cooperation regions. Thus, enabling punishment strategies increases the efficiency of the system (total expected information gain minus cost) compared to the baseline where no sharing occurs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment strategies in repeated/dynamic information sharing games can increase efficiency (total payoff) compared to the baseline with no punishment, provided the game is sufficiently long (infinite horizon) and the cost of sharing is not too high. The effect is more positive when the cost of sharing is low and the discount factor is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games: the 'public good' is information, and the payoff is information gain minus cost. The results suggest that, in similar dynamic games, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially as the game becomes more repeated and the cost of cooperation decreases. However, the model is limited to two players, binary actions, and does not include monetary payoffs or group sizes greater than two.

- source: Wang, S. X., Chen, X. J., Xiao, Z. L., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2023). Optimization of institutional incentives for cooperation in structured populations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both institutional punishment and reward can be tuned to achieve near-full cooperation in structured populations, with the optimal protocol being time-invariant and identical for both types of incentives under a given update rule. The minimal cumulative cost to the institution depends on the initial fraction of cooperators: punishment is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is high, while reward is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is low. The results are supported by analytical derivations and simulations across regular, random, small-world, and scale-free networks. The main outcome is the cumulative cost to the institution, which is a proxy for efficiency from the perspective of implementing cooperation-promoting interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the cost-effectiveness of punishment (and reward) depends on game parameters such as network structure, update rule, and initial cooperation level. While it does not report group efficiency in the standard sense, its focus on the cumulative cost to achieve high cooperation is closely related and can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in similar structured games. The results suggest that, with optimally tuned punishment, high cooperation (and thus high group efficiency) can be achieved at lower institutional cost when initial cooperation is already substantial. The findings are most directly applicable to structured Prisoner's Dilemma settings with institutional (not peer) punishment or reward, and may be less directly transferable to standard public goods games.

- source: dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Explaining the evolution of parochial punishment in humans. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment can promote cooperation and increase group average payoff (efficiency) when it is targeted at cheats and used to enforce cooperation. However, the evolution of parochial punishment (harsher punishment of out-group cheats) depends on the ecological scenario. Parochial punishment evolves (and can support high between-group cooperation and group payoff) when groups interact collaboratively and conflict occurs with random groups, or when cooperation is difficult to enforce. In contrast, when conflict occurs with the same group as prior interactions, punishment becomes spiteful (targeting all out-groups, including cooperators), which undermines between-group cooperation and does not improve efficiency. The model shows that both collaborative intergroup relations and the structure of conflict are key moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent: it can be positive (by promoting cooperation) or negative (by reducing group payoff through excessive or misdirected punishment).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games, especially in settings with group structure, third-party punishment, and group-level selection. It shows that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether punishment is targeted at cheats (promoting cooperation and group payoff) or is spiteful (reducing group payoff by punishing all out-groups). The presence and structure of intergroup conflict, the difficulty of enforcing cooperation, and the ability to form collaborative intergroup relationships are key moderators. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when it is used to enforce cooperation (especially when cooperation is hard to sustain), but can reduce efficiency if it is misdirected or used spitefully. The findings are most relevant for games with group structure, repeated interactions, and third-party punishment, but less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without these features.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Guo, J. L. (2019). A synergy of punishment and extortion in cooperation dilemmas driven by the leader. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a networked donation game, a single high-degree leader node using a combined punishment and extortion strategy can induce full cooperation in the population with probability close to 1, regardless of initial conditions or the presence of other strategies. This combined strategy yields the highest group and leader payoffs, and is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the set of available strategies. Pure punishment or pure extortion by the leader is less effective. The results suggest a strong synergy between punishment and extortion in promoting cooperation and maximizing group welfare in networked social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in networked prisoner's dilemma/donation games, the presence of a punishment mechanism—especially when combined with extortion and wielded by a central leader—can dramatically increase group payoff and the probability of full cooperation. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with network structure and leader influence. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially in combination with other mechanisms) can shift efficiency from low to near-maximal, even when baseline cooperation is low. However, the effect depends on the presence of influential nodes and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-networked settings.

- source: Helbing, D., Yu, W. J., Opp, K. D., & Rauhut, H. (2014). Conditions for the Emergence of Shared Norms in Populations with Incompatible Preferences. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (sanctioning) is a powerful mechanism for establishing shared norms in heterogeneous populations, even when preferences are incompatible. Behavior-based punishment is more effective than preference-based punishment in creating conformity. However, the resulting norm is not always efficient: sometimes a minority can establish an unpopular or suboptimal norm, and average payoff may not be maximized. Adaptive group pressure (where local majorities punish minorities) can lead to spontaneous norm emergence, but again, the content of the norm may be arbitrary and not welfare-maximizing. The model highlights the importance of path dependence, local interaction, and randomness in norm formation. Payoff (welfare) is explicitly tracked, and the model demonstrates that sanctioning can both increase and decrease average payoff depending on the scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (payoff) in public-goods-like environments is highly context-dependent. Punishment can increase conformity and create shared norms, but does not guarantee higher efficiency: the emergent norm may be suboptimal. The model suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its cost, effectiveness, and whether it is behavior- or preference-based. Local interaction and adaptive group pressure can facilitate norm emergence, but do not ensure welfare maximization. For prediction, this paper suggests that enabling punishment will often increase conformity but may have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially if the norm that emerges is not aligned with group welfare. The model's adjacent structure to PGGs means its findings are suggestive but not directly quantitative for standard PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Boyd, R., & Mathew, S. (2015). Third-party monitoring and sanctions aid the evolution of language. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper develops a repeated signaling game (a variant of the Sir Philip Sidney game) to model the evolution of honest communication. It finds that when only direct reciprocity is possible and lies are hard to detect, honest signaling is not evolutionarily stable except in special cases. However, when third-party monitoring and punishment are introduced—so that multiple community members can observe, aggregate evidence, and sanction dishonesty—the range of parameters supporting honest signaling expands greatly. This is because third-party monitoring increases the probability of detecting lies and reduces false accusations, making it more likely that group-beneficial honest signaling (analogous to high efficiency) is maintained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that adding third-party monitoring and punishment mechanisms to repeated social dilemmas (even when not strictly public goods games) can substantially increase the likelihood of group-beneficial outcomes, such as honest signaling or cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of third-party punishment and monitoring should be expected to have a strongly positive effect on efficiency or group payoff, especially when direct detection of defection is difficult. The model is not a standard PGG, but the logic is closely related and the results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments where monitoring and punishment are possible.

- source: Ågren, J. A., Davies, N. G., & Foster, K. R. (2019). Enforcement is central to the evolution of cooperation. *Nature Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enforcement mechanisms—defined as actions that reduce selfishness within cooperative alliances—are predicted by evolutionary models to evolve across a wide range of biological systems, from genomes to human societies. The models show that, in the absence of enforcement, selfishness undermines cooperation and reduces group fitness. When enforcement is possible, it evolves under many parameter regimes and leads to higher cooperation and improved collective outcomes. Empirical examples from animal societies, mutualisms, and cellular systems support the theoretical predictions. The effectiveness of enforcement depends on its cost and the structure of the system, but its presence is generally associated with higher group efficiency or fitness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment or enforcement in public-goods-like environments will increase group efficiency or collective payoff, relative to control conditions without enforcement. The models are not direct PGGs but are structurally analogous, and the mechanisms (punishment, policing, sanctions) map closely to punishment in PGGs. The effect is generally more positive, but the magnitude depends on the cost of enforcement and system details. The paper also highlights that enforcement is most critical when relatedness is low and that high enforcement costs can limit its effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment will increase efficiency, especially when enforcement is not prohibitively costly.

- source: Mariano, P., & Correia, L. (2015). THE GIVE AND TAKE GAME: ANALYSIS OF A RESOURCE SHARING GAME. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in the Give and Take game, the existence of costly punishment (costs to both the punisher and the punished for the 'take' action) creates a strong incentive for cooperative, efficient sharing of the resource. When punishment costs are high enough, multiple Nash equilibria exist in which players alternate giving the resource, maximizing average payoffs and achieving Pareto optimality. If punishment is cheap or absent, exploitative strategies dominate and efficiency is lower. Simulations confirm that strategies which give the resource (cooperate) are more successful when punishment is costly, even if there is no bonus for giving. The game thus demonstrates that built-in, costly punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in resource sharing dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in two-player resource sharing games with built-in punishment, increasing the cost of punishment (to both punisher and punished) increases the efficiency of the game by supporting cooperative equilibria. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment with sufficiently high costs will increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment) condition. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, turn-taking, indivisible resource games, but the mechanism—costly punishment deters exploitation and supports efficient cooperation—may generalize to adjacent public goods or resource sharing environments.

- source: Zhang, Y., & van der Schaar, M. (2013). Robust Reputation Protocol Design for Online Communities: A Stochastic Stability Analysis. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in a repeated random matching game (asymmetric gift-giving, not a standard PGG), a reputation protocol with probabilistic punishment (parameterized by punishment probability beta) can induce full cooperation and achieve maximal efficiency (social welfare = b-c) in the long run, provided the discount factor and punishment strength are within certain bounds. If punishment is too weak or too strong, efficiency is lower. Theoretical analysis is supported by numerical simulations showing that the optimal protocol achieves much higher average social welfare than direct reciprocity (tit-for-tat) or less robust reputation schemes. The protocol's design is robust to stochastic perturbations and ensures convergence to the efficient equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated random matching environments with binary contribution (gift-giving), the introduction of a well-calibrated reputation-based punishment protocol can move the system from zero efficiency (no cooperation) to full efficiency (all cooperate, social welfare maximized). The effect is robust to noise and initial conditions, provided the punishment probability is neither too low nor too high. While the game is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure and explicit efficiency analysis make the results highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar environments. The findings suggest that, when punishment is enabled via a reputation protocol and properly tuned, efficiency can increase dramatically compared to the no-punishment baseline.

- source: Chassang, S., & Takahashi, S. (2011). Robustness to incomplete information in repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoners' dilemma games, the set of robustly sustainable equilibrium payoffs (i.e., efficiency) is sharply affected by concerns about incomplete information. Under complete information, grim-trigger strategies can sustain cooperation if the discounted value of future cooperation exceeds the temptation to defect. However, when robustness to incomplete information is required, the cost of being the 'sucker' (cooperating while the other defects) becomes critical, and the set of sustainable payoffs can shrink dramatically. Selective-punishment strategies, which punish only the deviator, are more robust than grim-trigger strategies, and can sustain cooperation under a wider range of parameters. The paper provides explicit characterizations of when efficient cooperation is robustly sustainable and when only inefficient outcomes (permanent defection) are robust. The main outcomes are theoretical characterizations of efficiency/welfare as a function of game parameters and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma–like games under incomplete information. It shows that the design of the punishment mechanism (grim-trigger vs. selective punishment), the cost of being punished, and the enforceability of asymmetric outcomes are critical moderators. If only symmetric cooperation (CC) is enforceable, robust efficiency collapses to permanent defection. If asymmetric outcomes (DC or CD) are enforceable, robust efficiency can be as high as in the complete information case. The results are directly about efficiency/welfare, not just contribution rates. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment scheme and the game's payoff structure, especially under incomplete information. However, the results are for repeated prisoners' dilemma, not public goods games per se, so transfer to PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Feinberg, Y., & Kets, W. (2014). Ranking friends. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of social interactions—specifically, the ranking profiles that determine the order in which players seek help—critically affects the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) in repeated helping games. When punishment is available (either community or bilateral), full cooperation can be sustained if and only if incentive constraints are satisfied. These constraints are least restrictive (i.e., efficiency is most easily sustained) when expected costs are equalized across players (Latin square ranking profiles). Under bilateral punishment, even stricter symmetry (BFF) is required. The paper provides explicit formulas for the minimum discount factor required to sustain efficiency under each regime. Thus, the design of the interaction structure and the punishment regime jointly determine whether efficiency is achievable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated helping games with sequential requests and costly helping. It shows that the ability of punishment to sustain efficiency depends on the structure of interactions (ranking profiles) and the punishment regime. When the structure equalizes expected costs (Latin square), punishment is most effective at sustaining efficiency. If the structure is unbalanced, punishment is less effective, and efficiency may not be sustainable. The results are theoretical but provide explicit conditions and formulas that can be mapped to efficiency predictions in similar repeated public-goods-like environments, especially where helping is sequential and punishment is either community-wide or bilateral. However, the model is not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for standard PGGs should be made with caution.

- source: Lie-Panis, J., Fitouchi, L., Baumard, N., & André, J. B. (2024). The social leverage effect: Institutions transform weak reputation effects into strong incentives for cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that institutions, by leveraging contributions from individuals (motivated by reputation), can create strong enough incentives (via punishment, monitoring, or rewards) to sustain cooperation in settings where reputation alone would fail. The efficiency of the institution (how much incentive is generated per unit contribution) is a key moderator: high efficiency greatly expands the parameter space where cooperation and high payoffs are possible. The model also shows that when the population is patient (values future reputation), more individuals contribute, further increasing institutional effectiveness. However, when cooperation is easy, the institution can be wasteful, as its costs are not offset by additional gains in cooperation or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling efficient institutional punishment or monitoring in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency and payoffs, especially when baseline (control) efficiency is low due to hard cooperation problems. The model clarifies that the effect of punishment depends critically on the efficiency of the institution (punishment technology), the cost of contributing to the institution, and the patience of the population. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that when institutional punishment is efficient and the population is sufficiently future-oriented, enabling punishment will lead to large increases in efficiency relative to control. However, if cooperation is already high in control, the marginal benefit of punishment may be negligible or even negative due to unnecessary costs.

- source: Röhl, T., Röhl, C., Schuster, H. G., & Traulsen, A. (2007). Impact of fraud on the mean-field dynamics of cooperative social systems. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a reputation-based cooperation system (image scoring), the introduction of fraud (scammers who fake cooperation) and punishment (penalties for detected fraud) leads to complex dynamics. If the probability of detecting fraud and the penalty for fraud are high enough, cooperation (discriminators) can dominate. For intermediate detection probabilities, a limited presence of scammers can actually increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, making it easier for cooperation to evolve. The number of rounds of interaction is a key moderator: with more rounds, the payoff advantage of cooperation increases. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of cost, benefit, penalty, detection probability, and rounds) for when each strategy is favored. The overall effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by suppressing fraud, but the presence of some fraud can also facilitate cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games depends on the detection probability, penalty size, cost-benefit ratio, and number of rounds. In games with more rounds and higher detection/penalty, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency by supporting cooperation. However, a limited amount of undetected fraud can sometimes increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, so the relationship is not strictly monotonic. The model is not a standard public goods game, but its structure and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the efficiency impact of punishment will be more positive when detection is high, penalties are large, and the game is repeated over more rounds. The presence of fraud and its detectability are important moderators.

- source: Hooper, P. L., Kaplan, H. S., & Jaeggi, A. V. (2021). Gains to cooperation drive the evolution of egalitarianism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that punishment of dominance (hawkishness) by withholding cooperation (defection in the PD game) can be evolutionarily stable and lead to egalitarian outcomes when the future benefits of cooperation outweigh the short-term gains from dominance. The efficiency of the group increases as leveller strategies proliferate, reducing the frequency of costly contests and increasing resource sharing. Simulations show that an ecological shift increasing the gains to cooperation leads to a transition from dominance/defection equilibria to egalitarian/cooperative equilibria with higher payoffs. Empirical analysis of forager societies finds that greater reliance on cooperative hunting predicts a higher probability of egalitarianism, consistent with the model's predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment (here, the ability to withhold cooperation from dominant individuals) increases group efficiency and resource equality, especially when the marginal benefit of cooperation is high and the probability of future interaction is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games but not an exact match; however, the logic and payoff structure are highly relevant. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that in repeated, high-mpcr, high-future-interaction environments, enabling punishment (even in the form of exclusion or shunning) will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the benefits of cooperation are high relative to the benefits of dominance or defection.

- source: Qian, X. H., Huang, M., Zhang, Q. Y., Yin, M. Q., & Wang, X. W. (2018). Mechanism design of incentive-based reverse auctions with loss-averse 3PLs under incomplete attributes. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that combining incentive-based contracts (cost-sharing) with contingent penalties (punishment for delivery failure) in reverse auctions increases the ex ante expected profit (efficiency) of the system and the principal, compared to standard reverse auctions without these mechanisms. The efficiency gains are moderated by the number of agents (more agents increase efficiency), the fraction of low-risk agents, and the degree of loss aversion (higher loss aversion reduces efficiency). The presence and magnitude of the penalty (punishment) is a key design variable, and its optimal value depends on delivery risk parameters. The model provides explicit formulas for optimal efficiency under different mechanism designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that introducing punishment (contingent penalty) in a competitive procurement game with cost uncertainty and risk can increase system efficiency, but the effect is moderated by agent risk aversion and the number of agents. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that, all else equal, adding a punishment mechanism to a reverse auction or similar environment will increase efficiency, especially when there are more agents and when agents are less loss-averse. However, the magnitude of the effect depends on the specific design of the punishment and incentive parameters. The findings are most directly applicable to procurement/auction settings but are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2004). How should we define goodness? Reputation dynamics in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that only a small subset of reputation assignment rules—those that treat defection against bad individuals as good (i.e., as a form of justified punishment or sanction)—can sustain high efficiency (group payoff close to the maximum possible) in indirect reciprocity games. These 'leading eight' rules enable stable cooperation by allowing individuals to refuse help to bad actors without losing their own good reputation. The model shows that, under these rules, the average payoff in the population approaches the maximum possible (b-c), minus a small term due to errors. The results are robust to error rates and to the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as b > c. The paper provides a formal evolutionary justification for the importance of sanctioning/punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in reputation-based social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms—specifically, the ability to sanction or refuse help to bad actors without reputational cost—are critical for sustaining high efficiency in indirect reciprocity environments. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related: it is a repeated, pairwise helping game with a social dilemma structure. The findings suggest that, in environments where individuals can sanction defectors (and where such sanctioning is not itself punished), group efficiency can approach the maximum possible. For prediction tasks, this implies that enabling punishment or justified sanctioning in a game design is likely to increase efficiency, provided the reputation system is designed so that justified punishment does not harm the punisher's reputation. The results are robust to errors and to variations in the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as cooperation is individually costly but socially beneficial.

- source: Choi, J. K. (2007). Trembles may support cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of trembles (errors) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games can support the evolutionary stability of conditional cooperation (punishment-based strategies), but only when the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation is not too high. Specifically, if the benefit of cooperation is less than or equal to twice the cost (b ≤ 2c), then the ALLT (all trigger) equilibrium is asymptotically stable for sufficiently high continuation probability and low error rate. If the benefit is too high (b > 2c), then the equilibrium becomes unstable, and unconditional cooperators can invade, undermining the effectiveness of punishment. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using explicit payoff calculations and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in supporting cooperation (and thus efficiency) in repeated prisoner's dilemma-like environments depends critically on the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation and the presence of errors. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment will only improve efficiency when the benefit-to-cost ratio is not too high and when errors are present but not too frequent. The results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated games with trigger strategies, but the logic may extend to public goods games with similar strategic structure. The findings highlight that increasing the benefit of cooperation does not always make punishment more effective; in fact, it can undermine stability if too large. The paper does not provide direct evidence for multi-player public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed are relevant for adjacent settings.

- source: Nakamura, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2014). Indirect reciprocity in three types of social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player simultaneous-move social dilemmas, the stability and efficiency of cooperation via indirect reciprocity depend on the structure of the social norm (reputation updating rule) and the underlying game. In Snowdrift and Prisoner's Dilemma, strong punishment of defectors (assigning bad reputations for defection against good players) is necessary to maintain high efficiency (i.e., stable cooperation with high group payoff). In Stag Hunt, such punishment is less critical, and norms that unfairly favor reciprocators (always assign good reputation to reciprocation) can enhance the robustness and efficiency of cooperation. The paper also shows that punishing unconditional cooperators (who help bad players) is important for maintaining efficient cooperation in all three games. The efficiency outcome (i.e., group payoff relative to full cooperation) is maximized under certain social norms, but the effect of punishment-like mechanisms is context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (via reputation loss) on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the underlying game structure and the details of the punishment mechanism (social norm). In games with strong temptation to defect (SG, PD), enabling punishment (bad reputation for defectors) increases efficiency by stabilizing cooperation. In coordination games (SH), such punishment is less necessary, and norms that favor reciprocators can be more effective. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency should be moderated by the type of social dilemma and the specific implementation of the punishment/reputation mechanism. The results are most directly relevant to two-player games, not standard multi-player PGGs, but the logic may transfer to small-group settings with reputation-based punishment.

- source: Dubey, S. P., Kedar, G. D., & Ghate, S. H. (2021). A COMMUNICATION NETWORK ROUTING PROBLEM: MODELING AND OPTIMIZATION USING NON-COOPERATIVE GAME THEORY. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a non-cooperative network routing game with a punishment mechanism (increased cost for exceeding link capacity), the efficiency of outcomes (minimized total cost) depends on the structure of the cost function and the distribution of user demands. When the cost function is linear in the number of packets, all Nash equilibria are efficient (Pareto optimal). When the cost function is convex, only some Nash equilibria (about 50% of preferred focal Nash equilibria) are efficient, and this depends on the relative size of user demands. The punishment mechanism (cost increase) can deter greedy strategies and promote more efficient, balanced transmission strategies, but does not guarantee efficiency in all cases. The model is adjacent to public goods games in that it involves individual versus collective use of a shared resource, but differs in the details of the payoff and punishment structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment mechanisms implemented as increased costs for overuse can improve efficiency in resource-sharing games, but the effect depends on the cost function and the distribution of demands. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that punishment (costly overuse) can align Nash equilibria with efficient outcomes under certain conditions (e.g., linear cost), but not always (e.g., convex cost). The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games where the 'punishment' is a proportional cost rather than a direct deduction or fine. The results support the idea that the design of the punishment mechanism (cost structure) and the symmetry of player demands are important moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2005). Indirect reciprocity, image scoring, and moral hazard. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in models of indirect reciprocity with binary reputation, cooperation can be stably maintained if individuals' knowledge of others' reputations increases over time (e.g., with age or experience). Costly punishment—refusing to help those with a Bad score, even at the risk of one's own reputation—can support a stable equilibrium of cooperation, provided the information environment is sufficiently rich. If information is too scarce, defectors dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the efficiency (total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is higher when these conditions for stable cooperation are met, and that the presence of costly punishment is essential in the minimal model for resisting defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency and payoff outcomes in social dilemmas with reputation and punishment-like mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment (in the form of costly refusal to help those with a Bad reputation) can increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, but only when the information environment allows for effective reputation tracking. The model is not a literal public goods game, but the mechanisms and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control only if players can reliably observe or infer others' reputations, and if the cost-to-benefit ratio of helping is not too high. The findings also highlight the importance of the number of rounds (or opportunities for information to accumulate) and the structure of information flow. However, the model does not address group size, communication, or some other public goods game design features directly.

- source: Li, J. M., & Jiang, S. S. (2023). How can governance strategies be developed for marine ecological environment pollution caused by sea-using enterprises?-A study based on evolutionary game theory. *Ocean & Coastal Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in the absence of strong governance mechanisms (punishment, compensation, or high ecological performance appraisal), all parties tend to adopt minimal effort strategies, resulting in low efficiency and poor environmental outcomes (prisoner's dilemma). Introducing punishment (penalties for non-compliance, ecological damage compensation), increasing the weight of ecological indicators in local government performance appraisal, and combining these tools can shift the system to a more efficient equilibrium, where local governments and enterprises fully implement and comply with environmental policies, even if central government supervision is weak. The effect of any single tool is limited, but their combination is necessary to achieve high efficiency in governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms (penalties, compensation for damage) and reward mechanisms (performance appraisal) are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from introducing punishment in public-goods-like governance settings. The results suggest that, in adjacent multi-agent settings, punishment alone may not be sufficient, but when combined with other governance tools, it can substantially increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment, reward, and their combination should be considered as key moderators of treatment efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to weak incentives.

- source: Yuan, Y., Wang, F. Y., & Zeng, D. (2016). Developing a cooperative bidding framework for sponsored search markets - An evolutionary perspective. *Information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated sponsored search auctions, if advertisers are limited to unconditional strategies (always cooperate or always compete), competitive strategies dominate, resulting in high bids and low efficiency (advertiser revenue is about 0.64 times the UBNE outcome, which is suboptimal). When contingent 'nice and retaliatory' (NR) strategies are available—where advertisers cooperate unless a rival deviates, in which case they punish by bidding competitively—stable cooperation emerges. This leads to lower bids (at the GEAE, the lowest-price equilibrium) and advertiser revenues at the socially optimal VCG level. The NR strategies are neutrally evolutionarily stable: they cannot be strongly invaded by competitive strategies, and cooperation persists. The effect is robust to incomplete information, as long as advertisers can monitor rivals' bids and retaliate. The findings are supported by both theoretical analysis and coevolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment-like contingent strategies (NR) in repeated, multi-player, auction-like public goods environments can substantially increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to a control condition where only unconditional strategies are available. The effect is robust across a range of parameterizations (number of players, rounds, etc.), and the mechanism is directly analogous to punishment in public goods games: punishment of defectors stabilizes cooperation and increases group welfare. For prediction tasks, the presence of contingent punishment strategies should be expected to increase efficiency from a low baseline (competitive equilibrium) to near the social optimum, provided players can observe and respond to others' actions. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, multi-player, auction-like games with observable actions and the possibility of contingent retaliation.

- source: Shinya, O., Yusuke, I., & Hiroki, T. (2016). The Condition for Generous Trust. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with uncertainty about partner intention and competence, neither pure punishment nor pure generosity alone can sustain high efficiency. Generous trust (forgiveness) becomes viable and can support efficient outcomes when uncertainty is moderate and is combined with moderate punishment. If uncertainty (incompetence) is too high, efficiency collapses. The agent-based simulation shows that a mix of matching generosity (forgiving in partner selection) and exit generosity (forgiving in relationship maintenance) strategies coevolve and support higher efficiency, but only above a threshold of partner competence. The results highlight the importance of balancing punishment and forgiveness to achieve efficient cooperation under uncertainty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the level of uncertainty (competence) and the balance between punishment and forgiveness. In environments with moderate uncertainty, introducing moderate punishment alongside generosity increases efficiency compared to either strategy alone. However, if uncertainty is too high, even punishment cannot sustain efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment will be highest when the environment allows for some, but not excessive, uncertainty, and when punishment is not too strict or too lenient. The findings are most directly relevant to trust games and adjacent repeated dyadic games, rather than standard public goods games, but the mechanisms may transfer to PGG variants with similar uncertainty and partner selection features.

- source: Rauwolf, P., & Bryson, J. J. (2018). Expectations of Fairness and Trust Co-Evolve in Environments of Partial Information. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of a one-shot trust game with partner choice and partial information, both trust (willingness to invest with unknown partners) and costly punishment (rejecting profitable but unfair offers) can co-evolve and are adaptive, maximizing average payoffs. The benefit of punishment for efficiency only arises when trust is also present, and only in environments where information about partners is partially but not fully available. When information is fully transparent or there is no partner choice, punishment does not increase payoffs. The effect is robust to changes in the number of partners, population size, investment multiplier, selection intensity, and mutation rates. The model predicts that as the value of investment increases, the advantage and prevalence of fairness norms and punishment also increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency benefit of punishment in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the presence of both partner choice and partial information. If a public-goods or trust game is designed with multiple potential partners and only partial information about others' behavior, enabling punishment (the ability to reject unfair offers) can increase group efficiency, but only if players are also willing to trust unknown partners. If either partner choice or trust is absent, punishment does not improve efficiency. The model also predicts that the efficiency benefit of punishment increases with the investment multiplier (analogous to MPCR in PGGs). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by information structure, partner choice, and the value of cooperation.

- source: Nuño, J. C., Herrero, M. A., & Primicerio, M. (2010). Fighting cheaters: How and how much to invest. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in the absence of anti-cheating expenditure, cheaters dominate and total wealth collapses. Introducing and increasing expenditure to fight cheating (via social or punitive means) reduces the cheater population and increases total wealth, but only up to an optimal point. Beyond this, further expenditure reduces wealth due to the cost of enforcement. The optimal strategy involves a balance between social promotion and deterrence, with the best compromise (for the parameter values used) being to spend about 5% of wealth on anti-cheating measures, allocating about 90% of that to social promotion. The model also shows that the effectiveness of punishment (police efficiency) is a critical moderator: if punishment is highly effective, more resources should be allocated to it; if not, social measures are preferable. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by sensitivity analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment or anti-cheating measures in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency (total wealth) up to an optimal point, after which further increases in punishment expenditure can reduce efficiency. The model highlights the importance of both the amount spent and the effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (or anti-cheating expenditure) will increase efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline, but the magnitude depends on the cost, effectiveness, and allocation of resources. The model also warns that excessive punishment spending can be counterproductive. While the model is not an experimental PGG, its structure and outcomes are closely analogous and informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Levine, D. K., & Pesendorfer, W. (2007). The evolution of cooperation through imitation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models where players can imitate successful strategies and occasionally innovate, the long-run outcome depends on the ability to identify others' strategies. With perfect identification, efficient cooperation enforced by punishment is stable and the group achieves high efficiency. With imperfect or noisy identification, punishment becomes less effective, and the long-run outcome is less efficient, sometimes even less efficient than the static Nash equilibrium. The model shows that punishment strategies that condition on signals about others' types can sustain cooperation, but only if those signals are sufficiently informative. The presence of strategies that can 'masquerade' as cooperators (i.e., avoid detection) can undermine cooperation and efficiency unless masquerading is costly.
