  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on the information structure—specifically, the ability to identify defectors. If the game design allows for perfect or highly informative identification of defectors, punishment can sustain efficient cooperation. If identification is noisy or easily circumvented, punishment is less effective and may not improve efficiency. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one should pay close attention to the information available to players about others' actions or types, and to the possibility of undetectable defection or masquerading. The model is not an empirical test, but it provides a mechanistic basis for expecting positive, null, or even negative effects of punishment on efficiency depending on these design features.

- source: Quiñones, A. E., van Doorn, G. S., Pen, I., Weissing, F. J., & Taborsky, M. (2016). Negotiation and appeasement can be more effective drivers of sociality than kin selection. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Negotiation and appeasement mechanisms, modeled as responsive strategies where dominants can threaten aggression (punishment) and subordinates can appease by helping, lead to higher group productivity than kin-selected (unconditional) helping. The model shows that when negotiation is possible, even unrelated individuals can achieve high levels of help and group output, with low realized aggression. High relatedness, however, reduces the effectiveness of punishment/enforcement, leading to lower help and lower group productivity. Thus, punishment-like negotiation mechanisms are more effective at driving efficient cooperation than kin selection alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence of responsive punishment or enforcement mechanisms (negotiation) increases group efficiency in public-goods-like environments, compared to settings where only kin selection operates. The model is not a literal PGG, but the structure is closely analogous: individuals can help (contribute) or not, dominants can punish (aggression), and group output (productivity) is tracked. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment/negotiation will increase efficiency, especially when relatedness is low. The effect is robust across a range of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment-enabled conditions on efficiency, and highlights that high relatedness may reduce the marginal benefit of punishment.

- source: Xu, J., Song, Y. B., & van der Schaar, M. (2014). Sharing in Networks of Strategic Agents. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that distributed rating-based sanctioning protocols can fully or nearly fully restore efficiency in repeated network sharing games with strategic agents, provided certain incentive compatibility conditions are met. When the network's connectivity is not too high and agents' discount factors are sufficient, the protocol achieves the social optimum (PoA=1). As network connectivity increases or agents become more impatient, efficiency declines but remains higher than with direct reciprocity mechanisms. The protocol is robust to imperfect monitoring and can be tuned for dynamic networks. Theoretical and simulation results show that the protocol outperforms Tit-for-Tat and similar direct punishment schemes, especially in heterogeneous or asymmetric networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that distributed, rating-based punishment mechanisms can substantially increase efficiency in repeated networked cooperation environments, often achieving the social optimum. The effect is most pronounced when the network is not too highly connected and agents are sufficiently patient. The protocol's performance is robust to imperfect monitoring and network dynamics. For prediction tasks, the presence of a distributed, indirect punishment mechanism (ratings affecting future access) is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially compared to direct, bilateral punishment. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with costly sharing, which are adjacent to but not identical to standard public goods games.

- source: Castro, L., Serrano, J. M., & Toro, M. A. (1998). Conceptual capacity to categorize and the evolution of altruism. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the Assessor strategy, which incorporates a punishment-like mechanism (reprobation) based on observed or reported selfish behavior, achieves higher fitness (i.e., greater group efficiency or payoff) than standard reciprocal strategies like Tit-for-Tat or Observer Tit-for-Tat when competing against always-defect strategies. This advantage is robust across both unstructured and family-structured populations, provided certain thresholds of cooperation frequency are met. The Assessor strategy is especially effective when individuals can observe or be informed about others' reprobation of selfish behavior, allowing them to withhold cooperation from defectors more efficiently. The model predicts that such punishment-like mechanisms can support higher levels of cooperation and group payoff than reciprocity alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms (here, reprobation) in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency/payoff relative to control conditions without such mechanisms. The findings are most directly relevant to iterated prisoner's dilemma settings, which are adjacent to public goods games, and suggest that the ability to observe and act on others' punishment signals is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, the results imply that, all else equal, introducing a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when players can observe or learn about others' punishment actions. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes or empirical validation.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The leading eight: Social norms that can maintain cooperation by indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations, only eight specific social norms (the 'leading eight') can maintain both evolutionary stability and a high level of cooperation/efficiency, even when errors in action and assessment occur. These norms require players to cooperate with good-reputation individuals, punish defectors, justify punishment, and allow for apology and forgiveness. Under these norms, the average payoff per interaction is close to the theoretical maximum (b-c), and the fraction of cooperative (good) players remains high, with only a small reduction due to errors. The paper provides analytical conditions for when these norms are stable and efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations and one-shot interactions, the structure of the social norm (reputation dynamics) is critical for achieving high efficiency. Specifically, norms that combine justified punishment (refusing to help bad actors and not being penalized for it) and forgiveness (allowing recovery from bad reputation) can maintain efficiency close to the maximum. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms in repeated or reputation-based social dilemmas. The results suggest that, when punishment is structured as justified refusal to cooperate (and not as costly peer punishment), and when forgiveness is possible, efficiency losses due to punishment can be minimized or avoided. The paper does not address group size, communication, or other PGG-specific dimensions directly, but it does show that the design of sanctioning and reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2008). Mutualism, market effects and partner control. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the threat of terminating an interaction (partner control) can suppress cheating and maintain cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the relative abundance of exploiters and victims (market effects). When victims are rare, they can enforce cooperation and achieve higher payoffs; when they are common, exploiters can cheat more, reducing the efficiency of the interaction. If exploiters gain more control over the duration of interaction, they can extract more benefit, potentially driving the system into parasitism (negative payoffs for the victim). The model provides explicit conditions for when mutualism (positive payoffs) or parasitism (negative payoffs) will occur, and shows that efficiency is maximized when partner control is strong and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like partner control on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the structure of partner choice and market conditions. If the 'punishment' (exit threat) is credible and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters, efficiency is increased. If exploiters can easily find new victims, or if they gain control over the interaction, efficiency can decrease or even become negative for the exploited party. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will depend on parameters analogous to partner abundance, search costs, and control over interaction duration. The model does not map directly onto standard PGGs, but its logic is relevant for settings where partner choice or exit is possible.

- source: O'Connor, C. (2016). The Evolution of Guilt: A Model-Based Approach. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that guilt, modeled as a behavioral trait leading to cooperation, apology, and reparative actions, can be individually adaptive in evolutionary games. In both stag hunt and prisoner's dilemma settings, guilt-proneness increases the likelihood of cooperation, which in turn raises individual and group payoffs. When punishment for defection is present, guilt-proneness further increases individual fitness by reducing the risk of being punished. The models show that costly or honest signals of apology (as a proxy for guilt) can stabilize cooperation and prevent the invasion of defectors or 'fakers.' The efficiency of the group (measured as average fitness or payoff) is higher in populations where guilt-proneness and apology are present, especially when punishment mechanisms exist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms, when combined with behavioral traits like guilt-proneness and apology, can increase efficiency (group payoff) in social dilemma games. While it does not directly model public goods games, the findings are adjacent and suggest that enabling punishment and supporting honest signals of intent (like apology) are likely to increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The models also highlight the importance of the cost and credibility of punishment and apology mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment (especially with some cost or honest signaling) will increase efficiency, provided that the game environment allows for reciprocity and repeated interaction.

- source: Handfield, T., Huang, P. H., & Simpson, R. M. (2016). Climate change, cooperation and moral bioenhancement. *Journal of Medical Ethics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that enhancing retributive (punishment) tendencies in social dilemma games (including public goods and prisoner's dilemma paradigms) is likely to lower group efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The authors review theoretical models and cite experimental evidence indicating that harsh punishment can make groups poorer, and that those who punish often gain less than those who do not. The effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio but relatively insensitive to punishment severity. The paper also notes that anti-social punishment (punishing cooperators) can be highly destructive to cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that enabling or increasing punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is likely to reduce group efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or misapplied. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, adding or intensifying punishment mechanisms may not increase and may even decrease efficiency, despite possible increases in cooperation rates. The effect is particularly negative when punishment is severe or when anti-social punishment is possible. The paper is less informative about the precise quantitative effect sizes but is clear about the direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: Gintis, H. (2003). The hitchhiker's guide to altruism: Gene-culture coevolution, and the internalization of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that internalized prosocial norms, including altruistic punishment, can become evolutionarily stable through gene-culture coevolution, especially when the fitness benefits of prosocial norms are high, extraparental socialization is strong, and assortative mating is present. Simulations show that groups with internalized altruistic punishment achieve high mean fitness and eliminate antisocial norms. Altruistic punishment is particularly effective at sustaining cooperation and group efficiency because its cost is lower and more concentrated than the cost of direct cooperation. The models predict that, in the presence of punishment, group efficiency and mean fitness are higher than in its absence, especially in large groups or when repeated interaction is infrequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments increases group efficiency and mean payoff, especially when direct cooperation is not stable due to high costs. The models suggest that punishment is a robust mechanism for sustaining cooperation and high group payoff, even in large groups or with high migration, as long as the cost of punishment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where internalized norms and cultural transmission play a role, and less so to short-run laboratory games, but the core logic supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like dilemmas.

- source: Lehmann, L., & Keller, L. (2006). The evolution of cooperation and altruism - a general framework and a classification of models. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and altruism can only evolve when at least one of four general conditions is met: (1) direct benefits to the actor, (2) repeated interactions with information about partner behavior, (3) preferential interactions with kin, or (4) genetic linkage between recognition and helping traits (greenbeard effect). Punishment, coercion, and policing are highlighted as mechanisms that can shift the cost-benefit ratio in favor of cooperation, making it more likely to evolve and persist. The paper reviews and classifies a large number of theoretical models, showing that all can be mapped onto these four categories. It emphasizes that punishment is effective in promoting cooperation when it increases the cost of defection relative to cooperation, but notes that the stability of punishment depends on its own cost and on whether it is linked to cooperation genetically or behaviorally.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase the efficiency (fitness, group payoff, or productivity) of cooperative systems by making defection less attractive. It does not provide quantitative predictions for specific game designs, but it clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its ability to alter the cost-benefit ratio and on the presence of at least one of the four general conditions for cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments if it is not prohibitively costly and if it is implemented in a way that aligns with one of the four evolutionary pathways. However, the paper does not address specific design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, chat) in the context of laboratory or field experiments, so its guidance is primarily mechanistic and qualitative.

- source: Ishida, J. (2009). Incentives in academics: Collaboration under weak complementarities. *Labour Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated game with voluntary team formation, optimal contracts may use high-powered team incentives and low-powered individual incentives to exploit peer monitoring and reputation effects. This can make collaboration sustainable and efficient even with weak complementarities, overcoming the free-rider problem present in static settings. However, when the opportunity to collaborate is frequent and agents are patient, the optimal contract may under-incentivize individual effort, leading to inefficiency when collaboration is not possible. Thus, repeated interaction and peer sanctions can both improve and harm efficiency, depending on parameter values (discount factor, frequency of collaboration opportunities, degree of complementarities).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling peer sanctions (punishment via reputation loss and exclusion from collaboration) in repeated public-goods-like environments depends on the structure of incentives and the frequency of collaboration opportunities. When peer sanctions are possible, efficiency can increase by overcoming free-rider problems, but if individual incentives are weakened too much to make peer sanctions more effective, efficiency can decrease when collaboration is not possible. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not unconditionally positive: it depends on whether the contract design balances team and individual incentives, the patience of agents, and the frequency of opportunities for collaboration. The model is adjacent to public goods games, with peer sanctions implemented via exclusion and reputation rather than explicit costly punishment.

- source: Glover, J., & Kim, E. (2021). Optimal Team Composition: Diversity to Foster Implicit Team Incentives. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated team production, the efficiency of implicit team incentives (mutual monitoring and punishment for free-riding) depends critically on team composition and the expected duration of the relationship (discount factor). Diverse teams (with productive complementarity) always have a monotonic relationship between tenure and incentive efficiency: as tenure increases, the cost of providing incentives decreases, and collusion is not a concern. Specialized teams (with productive substitutability) can initially benefit from longer tenure (lower incentive costs), but as tenure increases further, collusion becomes a problem, raising the cost of incentives and reducing efficiency. The model provides explicit formulas and parameter thresholds for when each team type is optimal. The main efficiency outcome is the principal's expected payoff, which is maximized by choosing the team composition that best balances productive efficiency and incentive efficiency, given the risk of collusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms (mutual monitoring, implicit threats) in repeated team production environments, especially as a function of team composition (diversity vs. specialization), the discount factor (tenure), and the production technology (complementarity vs. substitutability). While not a public goods game per se, the model is adjacent and highly relevant for settings where group payoff depends on repeated cooperation and the threat of future punishment. The results show that the efficiency effect of punishment depends on whether collusion is possible and on the structure of the team. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment (mutual monitoring) generally increases efficiency in diverse teams and in specialized teams with short tenure, but may reduce efficiency in specialized teams with long tenure due to collusion. The explicit formulas and parameter thresholds can inform quantitative predictions if the relevant parameters are known.

- source: Chalub, F. A. C. C., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2006). The evolution of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that, in a two-level selection model with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, simple and unambiguous social norms emerge that maximize group efficiency (average payoff). The dominant norm (10011001) enforces cooperation by rewarding help to good individuals and punishing refusal to help good individuals or helping bad individuals. This norm allows for forgiveness and rapid recovery of reputation, but also implacable punishment for bad actions. Under this norm, cooperative strategies (CO, OR) dominate, and islands reach maximal possible fitness. The model demonstrates that the evolution of such norms is robust across a wide range of benefit parameters (b), and that a small amount of migration between islands is essential for the evolution of efficient norms and strategies. The findings support the idea that reputation-based punishment and norm enforcement can sustain high efficiency in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that reputation-based punishment (loss of reputation for non-cooperation) in repeated social dilemma games can lead to the evolution of norms that maximize group efficiency. The model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a give-and-receive game with binary actions and reputation updating. However, the core mechanism—punishment via reputation loss, indirect reciprocity, and norm enforcement—closely parallels the logic of punishment in public goods games. The findings suggest that, when such mechanisms are present, enabling punishment (via reputation or direct sanctions) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The results are robust across a range of benefit-to-cost ratios and do not depend on fine-tuning of parameters. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that introducing effective punishment or norm enforcement in repeated social dilemma environments will increase efficiency, especially when group structure and reputation are salient.

- source: Abdulkadiroglu, A., & Bagwell, K. (2013). Trust, Reciprocity, and Favors in Cooperative Relationships. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with private information, efficiency (total group payoff) can be increased above the autarky (Nash) level through favor-exchange relationships (dynamic reciprocity) or through symmetric punishment (negative reciprocity). The optimal equilibrium structure depends on the parameters: when information asymmetries are low, symmetric punishment can support higher efficiency than favor-exchange. Hybrid equilibria (honeymoon period followed by favor-exchange or punishment) can further improve efficiency in some cases. The paper provides explicit formulas for the efficiency achieved under each regime. Immediate reciprocity (punishment for non-reciprocation) is not always necessary; dynamic reciprocity (future favor) is often sufficient. The model is not a PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, trust, and efficiency under repeated interaction and private information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions for how efficiency in repeated cooperation games is affected by the availability and structure of punishment and favor-exchange mechanisms. While not a PGG, the model is adjacent and the efficiency formulas and comparative statics are informative for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment (symmetric, infrequent) is possible versus when only favor-exchange is available. The results suggest that, in some parameter regions, introducing symmetric punishment can increase efficiency above favor-exchange alone, especially when information asymmetries are low. However, the model is limited to two players and does not include standard PGG features like group size or marginal per-capita return. The findings are most useful for understanding the theoretical potential of punishment to enhance efficiency in repeated cooperation with private information, but direct quantitative prediction for PGGs would require caution.

- source: Takahashi, S. (2010). Community enforcement when players observe partners' past play. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement—whereby third parties punish cheaters based on first-order information about partners' past play—can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with random matching. Under the independent and indifferent equilibrium concept, for sufficiently high discount factors, any symmetric group payoff between 0 (full defection) and 1 (full cooperation) can be achieved. The paper also shows that these results are robust to noise in actions or records, and that the equilibrium construction extends to finite populations. The threshold for sustaining cooperation does not depend on population size under this mechanism, in contrast to contagious equilibria. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and analyzes the stability of cooperation under shocks and noise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with random matching and first-order information, enabling community enforcement (punishment by third parties based on partners' past play) can increase group efficiency up to the full cooperation level, provided players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to population size and some forms of noise. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in analogous public-goods-game-like environments, enabling punishment mechanisms (even with limited information) can substantially increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the discount factor is high. The paper also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the structure of the stage game (supermodular vs. submodular) and the information available to players.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Santos, F. C., & Lenaerts, T. (2013). Good Agreements Make Good Friends. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that prior commitment strategies, where players agree in advance to cooperate and specify compensation for defection, can achieve higher levels of cooperation and average payoff than traditional costly punishment, provided the arrangement cost is not too high. Sharing the cost of commitment further increases the viability and efficiency of these strategies. The efficiency gains are robust across a range of compensation and arrangement cost parameters, and commitment-based cooperation is less susceptible to antisocial punishment. The findings are derived from analytical risk-dominance conditions and evolutionary simulations, focusing on the stationary distribution of strategies and average payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms analogous to prior commitment with compensation (distinct from standard punishment) can yield higher efficiency than costly punishment, especially when the cost of arranging the agreement is low or shared. While the model is based on the one-shot PD rather than a public goods game, the efficiency results are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with similar cost/impact structures. The findings imply that, for games with low punishment/arrangement costs and high punishment/compensation effectiveness, enabling commitment or cost-sharing mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to standard punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of cost-to-impact ratios and the potential for free-riding if arrangement costs are high and not shared.

- source: Inaba, M., Takahashi, N., & Ohtsuki, H. (2016). Robustness of linkage strategy that leads to large-scale cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that a linkage strategy—where defectors in a social dilemma are excluded from indirect reciprocity benefits—can be evolutionarily stable and maintain high group efficiency, but only when there are implementation errors in the social dilemma and no perception errors. In the absence of errors, linkage and non-linkage strategies are neutral, allowing non-linkage strategies to drift in and eventually enable defectors to invade, collapsing cooperation and efficiency. Perception errors (misjudging others' actions) undermine the stability and efficiency of linkage strategies, as they disrupt the ability to assortatively cooperate and exclude defectors. The results highlight that the effectiveness of exclusionary sanctions (linkage) for maintaining efficiency is highly sensitive to the type and prevalence of errors in the system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like exclusion mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that exclusionary sanctions (linkage) can increase efficiency, but only if players can reliably observe and agree on others' actions (i.e., low perception error) and if there is some noise in implementation (so that linkage strategies can distinguish themselves from non-linkage cooperators). If perception errors are common, the efficiency benefit of punishment/exclusion is undermined. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct costly punishment, and where the information environment is well-specified. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, but the mechanisms and moderators (error types, information) are highly relevant for prediction.

- source: Andersson, L. (2020). Cooperation between Emotional Players. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoners' dilemma games, the presence of emotional players—who value others' payoffs positively (friendly) or negatively (hostile) depending on the history of play—can alter the conditions under which cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) is sustainable. With Grim Trigger strategies, emotional states (both friendly and hostile) lower the required discount factor for cooperation, making efficient outcomes more attainable. With mutual minmax strategies, emotions can either facilitate or obstruct cooperation: friendly states increase the cost of deviating, but hostile states can make it harder to return to cooperation after punishment, sometimes requiring greater patience than in the standard model. The model also shows that a rational player can manipulate an emotional player into a friendly state to achieve higher payoffs. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with no empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how emotional dynamics—modeled as state-dependent preferences—can affect the efficiency of repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the repeated prisoners' dilemma is closely related. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, emotional transitions that make punishment more or less credible) on efficiency depends on the interplay between strategy, emotional responsiveness, and patience (discount factor). For prediction tasks, the paper implies that enabling mechanisms that make punishment more credible (e.g., emotional hostility after defection) can increase efficiency under some strategies, but may also backfire if they make return to cooperation difficult. The results are most directly relevant for settings where player preferences or states can change in response to observed actions, and less so for standard PGGs with fixed payoffs and explicit punishment stages.

- source: Chassang, S. (2010). Building Routines: Learning, Cooperation, and the Dynamics of Incomplete Relational Contracts. *American Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, in repeated relationships with incomplete information about how to cooperate, initial stages are characterized by inefficient punishment (exit) due to imperfect monitoring and the need to incentivize information revelation. As players learn and routines are established, the need for punishment disappears and efficiency increases. However, the cost of inducing further information revelation can outweigh the benefits, leading to path dependence and persistent inefficiency: partnerships may settle into suboptimal routines even when more efficient cooperation is possible. The extent of inefficiency depends on the magnitude of incentive conflicts and the cost of aligning incentives. The model provides qualitative predictions about the efficiency costs of punishment and the conditions under which punishment is required or can be avoided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (or inefficient exit) in repeated games with incomplete information can reduce efficiency, especially in the early stages of a relationship. The efficiency cost of punishment is highest when players are still learning about how to cooperate, and disappears once routines are established and monitoring becomes perfect. The model suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment depends on the information structure and the stage of the relationship: punishment is necessary to induce information revelation but is always inefficient compared to the complete information benchmark. The findings imply that, in environments similar to public goods games with incomplete information and learning, enabling punishment may initially reduce efficiency, but its necessity diminishes as learning occurs. The model also highlights the importance of path dependence and the possibility of persistent inefficiency due to early random events. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent and may be negative or mixed, especially when learning is costly and incomplete.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2021). Cooperation evolves by the payoff-difference-based probabilistic reward. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic reward mechanism, where the probability of rewarding a cooperator increases with the payoff difference between the player and the cooperator, leads to higher average payoffs and more cooperation in most cases. The effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, though the strength and range of the effect depend on the network structure and the reward parameter t. The paper does not directly study punishment in this model, but situates its findings in the context of prior work on punishment and reward in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a well-designed reward mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially under certain network structures and parameter ranges. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare punishment and reward within the same experimental or simulation framework. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by analogy and through its discussion of prior punishment studies.

- source: Frean, M. R., & Abraham, E. R. (2004). Adaptation and enslavement in endosymbiont-host associations - art. no. 051913. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The main finding is that in a two-species repeated cooperation game with differential adaptation rates, the slower-adapting species (host) evolves to provide just enough cooperation to keep the faster-adapting species (symbiont) maximally cooperative. This 'miser-slave' dynamic leads to highly asymmetric payoffs: hosts receive nearly all the benefit, while symbionts receive very little, even though they are highly cooperative. The total group payoff (efficiency) is much less than the maximum possible (full mutual cooperation), because the host withholds cooperation. The model provides a theoretical explanation for observed asymmetries in natural symbioses, where hosts benefit much more than symbionts. The findings are robust across a range of cost-benefit ratios and are supported by both analytic and simulation results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical mechanism showing that, in repeated cooperation games with contingent strategies and differential adaptation rates, the introduction of a contingent response (analogous to punishment or reward) by one party can lead to highly asymmetric efficiency outcomes. Specifically, if the 'punishing' party adapts more slowly, it can extract maximal cooperation from the other party while contributing minimally itself, resulting in low overall efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the structure of contingent responses and adaptation rates can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, the model is not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implemented as a contingent reward/withholding rather than explicit costly punishment. Thus, while the findings are informative about the potential for punishment/contingency to reduce efficiency via asymmetry, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs with explicit punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Pérez-Escudero, A., Friedman, J., & Gore, J. (2016). Preferential interactions promote blind cooperation and informed defection. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games where one player can gather information before cooperating or defecting, aversion to information gathering (i.e., a preference for 'blind' cooperation) can be sustained either by punishment (ending the game if the player looks) or by preferential interactions (continuing only with reliable partners). In homogeneous populations, punishment is required to enforce blind cooperation and thus higher payoffs. In heterogeneous populations, preferential interactions can achieve similar or greater efficiency without punishment. The model also predicts that if detecting information gathering is costly, punishment-based mechanisms break down in homogeneous populations, but preferential interactions remain effective in heterogeneous ones. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in homogeneous populations but redundant or unnecessary in heterogeneous ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games depends on population heterogeneity. In homogeneous populations, punishment (ending the game after information gathering or defection) is necessary to enforce cooperation and increase efficiency. In heterogeneous populations, preferential partner selection can achieve high efficiency without punishment. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should consider the degree of population heterogeneity and the possibility of preferential interactions. If the environment is homogeneous and punishment is strong, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If the environment is heterogeneous and allows for preferential interactions, punishment may have little or no additional effect on efficiency.

- source: Fishman, M. A. (2006). Involuntary defection and the evolutionary origins of empathy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated dyadic cooperation games with involuntary defection, unconditional punishment (retaliation for any defection) undermines cooperation and reduces efficiency, as it leads to cycles of mutual defection. In contrast, an empathic retaliator strategy—where punishment is withheld if the opponent's defection follows one's own involuntary defection—can be evolutionarily stable and support high efficiency, especially when the expected number of repeated interactions is large. The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the cost of helping, the probability of being able to help, and the expected number of interaction cycles. The paper provides explicit conditions and payoff matrices for these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms critically affects efficiency in repeated cooperation games. Specifically, unconditional punishment (retaliation) in the presence of involuntary defection leads to lower efficiency, while empathic (conditional) punishment can sustain high efficiency if repeated interactions are frequent. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on whether punishment is unconditional or conditional/empathic, the frequency of repeated interactions, and the possibility of involuntary defection. However, the model is not a public goods game, and punishment is not costly in the sense of standard PGG experiments; rather, it is refusal to help. Thus, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution, but the qualitative insight is that punishment can either harm or help efficiency depending on its design and the game context.

- source: Deb, J., & González-Díaz, J. (2019). Enforcing social norms: Trust-building and community enforcement. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that, in large enough populations and with sufficiently patient players, efficient (cooperative) outcomes can be sustained in equilibrium in repeated anonymous random matching games, even when players only observe their own transactions and have no information about others' actions or identities. The mechanism involves a trust-building phase followed by community enforcement (contagion-based punishment). The set of achievable equilibrium payoffs includes those arbitrarily close to the efficient frontier, as long as the stage game has a strict Nash equilibrium and a one-sided incentive profile. The paper provides detailed conditions and mathematical proofs for when and how this is possible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated games with random matching, minimal information, and community enforcement (contagion-based punishment), the introduction of a punishment mechanism (in the form of Nash reversion triggered by observed deviations) can enable the group to achieve efficiency levels arbitrarily close to full cooperation, provided the population is large enough and players are sufficiently patient. The results are not specific to the public goods game but apply to a broad class of two-player games with certain properties. The findings suggest that, in environments with similar structure (random matching, minimal information, repeated play, and the possibility of community-wide punishment), enabling punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, even when direct monitoring is not possible. However, the results are theoretical and depend on equilibrium reasoning, not empirical observation.

- source: Odouard, V. V., & Price, M. H. (2023). Tit for tattling: Cooperation, communication, and how each could stabilize the other. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with indirect reciprocity, if agents use an aligned norm (rewarding the behaviors it prescribes), can signal about both actions and the truthfulness of signals (meta-signaling), and make occasional errors, then a stable equilibrium emerges with near-maximal cooperation and efficiency. The model shows that any deviation from the prescribed strategy is costly, and the proportion of non-cooperation (and thus inefficiency) can be made arbitrarily small by reducing error rates. The equilibrium is robust to invasion by alternative strategies, and the system achieves high group payoff and efficiency. The findings are theoretical and based on mathematical analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma-like environments with indirect reciprocity, the introduction of mechanisms that allow for social enforcement (meta-signaling) and some error can stabilize highly efficient, cooperative equilibria. While the model is not a public goods game per se, the mapping to efficiency and group payoff is direct and informative for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms (here, social enforcement via meta-signaling) on efficiency. The results suggest that, under the right institutional and informational conditions, enabling social enforcement can substantially increase efficiency relative to baseline. However, the model is highly stylized and assumes infinite population, infinite rounds, and well-mixed interactions, so predictions should be adjusted for more realistic settings.

- source: Harbaugh, R., & To, T. (2014). Opportunistic discrimination. *European Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with two groups, the ability to sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes depends on the punishment strategy adopted by individuals. Solidarity-based punishment (where all individuals punish any opportunism) supports high efficiency and deters cheating. However, if individuals only punish opportunism against their own group (discriminatory trigger), the minority group is more likely to be cheated, leading to lower efficiency for the minority and lower overall surplus. The model also shows that selective legal enforcement (protecting only the majority) can reduce the effectiveness of reputational punishment, making the minority worse off. Only anti-discrimination enforcement (penalizing group-based opportunism) robustly increases efficiency and reduces discrimination. The results highlight that the structure of punishment and enforcement mechanisms critically shapes efficiency and the distribution of payoffs across groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency depends on whether punishment is coordinated across groups (solidarity) or limited to within-group (discriminatory). In environments where punishment is only triggered by harm to one's own group, efficiency gains from punishment are limited, especially for minorities. The model also warns that adding legal enforcement in a way that only protects the majority can reduce overall efficiency for minorities by undermining reputational incentives. For prediction tasks, this suggests that game designs enabling broad, group-independent punishment (solidarity) will yield higher efficiency gains from punishment, while designs that fragment punishment or allow selective enforcement may see limited or even negative effects on efficiency, especially for minority subgroups.

- source: Raub, W., Buskens, V., & Frey, V. (2019). Strategic tie formation for long-term exchange relations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when actors can invest in strategic tie formation (i.e., commit to repeated interaction at a cost), and when the continuation probability and payoff structure are favorable, they will rationally choose to form ties and cooperate, resulting in higher group efficiency (payoff) than in the absence of such ties, where defection prevails. The efficiency gain from tie formation is explicitly quantified as the difference in equilibrium payoffs between mutual cooperation and mutual defection, net of tie formation costs. The model also generalizes to other social dilemma games (e.g., Trust Game) and discusses the role of institutional design in facilitating or hindering efficient outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling mechanisms for repeated interaction and voluntary commitment (analogous to endogenous punishment or sanctioning institutions) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, compared to control conditions without such mechanisms. While the model is not a direct PGG or experimental study, its formal results on equilibrium payoffs, tie formation, and cooperation are highly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in repeated, two-player social dilemmas with commitment options. The explicit mapping of efficiency gains to game parameters (payoff structure, continuation probability, cost of commitment) can inform predictions about the impact of punishment-like or commitment-enabling design features in related experimental games.

- source: Robert, J. M., Otrok, H., Quttoum, A. N., & Boukhris, R. (2012). A distributed resource management model for Virtual Private Networks: Tit-for-Tat strategies. *Computer Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Simulation results show that classical Tit-for-Tat fails in this asynchronous, stochastic setting, leading to deadlocks and low total gain. Generous Tit-for-Tat avoids deadlocks and achieves high group gain, but is vulnerable to exploitation by selfish participants. Altruistic and reputation-based strategies can effectively punish selfish participants, forcing them to cooperate to receive benefits. The best strategies (reputation-based and altruistic-reputation) allow cooperative participants to achieve near-optimal total gain, while selfish participants are excluded unless they match cooperative behavior. The findings are robust across variations in the number of cooperative/selfish participants and the probability of offering resources.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (retaliation, exclusion, reputation-based refusal) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated resource-sharing games with asynchronous moves and stochastic cooperation. The results are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment or exclusion in public-goods-like environments, especially when cooperation is probabilistic and not always feasible. The specific game structure differs from standard PGGs (asynchronous, resource lending/borrowing, not simultaneous contribution), but the payoff logic and the effect of punishment on group efficiency are closely analogous. The paper supports the prediction that enabling effective punishment or exclusion will increase efficiency, especially when selfish behavior is otherwise possible.

- source: Gutnisky, D. A., & Zanutto, B. S. (2004). Cooperation in the iterated prisoner's dilemma is learned by operant conditioning mechanisms. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The operant conditioning model (ZL) can learn to cooperate in the IPD and achieves high average payoffs against a variety of strategies. In evolutionary simulations, ZL can dominate or survive in populations depending on the mix of strategies and the presence of noise (random moves). However, ZL is always slightly less efficient than non-learning strategies because it requires time to learn optimal responses. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on reinforcement learning from payoffs. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning mechanisms can support cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, but do not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide insight into how learning mechanisms (operant conditioning) can support cooperation and high payoffs in repeated dyadic dilemmas (IPD). The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means that the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that learning-based strategies can achieve high efficiency, but may be less efficient than fixed strategies in the short run due to learning costs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, contextual support.

- source: Murase, Y., & Hilbe, C. (2024). Computational evolution of social norms in well-mixed and group-structured populations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in well-mixed populations, even with complex (third-order) social norms, cooperation and high group payoff (efficiency) are difficult to sustain: defecting strategies (ALLD) are most abundant, and average payoffs are low. In group-structured populations, however, cooperation and high efficiency can evolve and persist, especially when third-order norms are available. The most successful norm (L1) treats cooperation as always good and defection as bad, except when defection is 'justified' (against bad recipients). This norm is robust to disagreements and errors in private assessment. The results highlight the importance of population structure and norm complexity for the evolution of efficient, cooperative outcomes. The model does not implement explicit costly punishment, but some norms functionally resemble punishment by allowing justified defection without reputational cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the population (well-mixed vs. group-structured) and the complexity of available social norms (second-order vs. third-order) are critical moderators of the effect of reputational 'punishment' (justified defection) on group efficiency. In well-mixed populations, enabling more complex norms or reputational punishment does not reliably increase efficiency; in group-structured populations, it can lead to high efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of group structure and the richness of the norm/strategy space are key predictors of whether enabling reputational punishment will improve efficiency. However, the results may not transfer directly to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, as the model is dyadic and punishment is reputational, not material.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2022). Evolution of trustfulness in the case where resources for cooperation are sometimes absent. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with stochastic absence of resources for cooperation, the evolutionarily stable outcome is often a coexistence of trustful cooperators and trustful defectors, provided the cost-to-benefit ratio, the probability of lacking resources, and the exogenous break-up rate are not too high. Trustful strategies (those that keep interacting with partners who defect due to lack of resources) are robustly favored by natural selection, and this leads to higher equilibrium frequencies of cooperation and higher group payoffs (efficiency). When parameters are unfavorable (high cost, high resource absence, high break-up), defectors dominate and efficiency is low. The model does not include explicit punishment or costly sanctioning, but the 'walk-away' (partner switching) mechanism is functionally adjacent to punishment in that it can enforce cooperation by breaking ties with defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not model a public goods game or explicit punishment, it analyzes a closely related repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with partner choice (walk-away) and stochastic resource constraints. The key design dimensions mapped are the cost-to-benefit ratio (analogous to MPCR), the probability of resource absence (a form of noise or cooperation constraint), and the exogenous break-up rate (analogous to the probability of game continuation or group stability). The main result is that mechanisms allowing players to break ties with defectors (even when defection is due to lack of resources) can support high efficiency, provided the game is not too harsh (low cost, low noise, stable groups). This suggests that in PGG-like environments, enabling partner choice or walk-away (an adjacent form of sanction) can increase efficiency, especially when the baseline efficiency is not too low. However, the absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the logic of positive assortment and the conditions for efficiency gains are highly relevant.

- source: Hilbe, C., Traulsen, A., & Sigmund, K. (2015). Partners or rivals? Strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper provides a comprehensive theoretical analysis of memory-one strategies in the IPD, showing which strategies can enforce mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency), competitive advantage, or linear payoff relationships (ZD strategies). It proves that partner strategies (including Grim and Tit-for-Tat under certain conditions) can enforce mutual cooperation, and that generous ZD strategies can promote cooperation by aligning payoffs. The existence and effectiveness of these strategies depend on the discount factor (patience) and the payoff matrix. The paper does not analyze the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature, but it shows that strategies implementing contingent punishment (e.g., Grim) are critical for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in repeated games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how strategy design in repeated games can enforce efficient outcomes, but it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling or disabling punishment as a separate game feature in public goods games. Its results are most relevant for two-player repeated games (IPD) and show that strategies implementing contingent punishment (like Grim or Tit-for-Tat) are necessary for sustaining cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, the paper suggests that the ability to implement credible punishment (via strategy) is essential for efficiency, but it does not address institutional punishment mechanisms or group settings directly. Thus, its value is mainly theoretical and mechanistic, not empirical or directly predictive for PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Rob, R., & Yang, H. X. (2010). Long-term relationships as safeguards. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous relationship formation and dissolution can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in a repeated prisoner's dilemma, but only if the fraction of always-defect (bad) types is neither too low nor too high. When the fraction of bad types is in an intermediate range, a 'good equilibrium' exists in which strategic players cooperate, and social welfare is maximized at the lower end of this range. If the fraction of bad types is too low, there is no credible punishment for defection, and cooperation unravels. If too high, the probability of forming a cooperative relationship is too low, and defection dominates. The model also characterizes mixed strategy equilibria and shows that welfare can drop discontinuously as parameters cross critical thresholds. The main outcome is social welfare (efficiency), which is explicitly calculated as a function of the type distribution and model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that endogenous social structure (relationship formation and dissolution) can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency, but only under certain conditions on the population's type distribution. The efficiency of the 'treatment' (i.e., the equilibrium with endogenous punishment via exclusion) is maximized when the fraction of bad types is just high enough to make the threat of exclusion credible, but not so high as to make cooperation infeasible. The model does not map directly onto standard public goods games with explicit punishment options, but it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner choice or ostracism mechanisms. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is non-monotonic and highly sensitive to the underlying population structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the presence and credibility of punishment (even if not costly or explicit) can have strong, but non-linear, effects on efficiency.

- source: Pei, H. (2024). Reputation Effects under Short Memories. *Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games with a patient player and a sequence of short-run players who observe only summary statistics of the last K actions (not the full sequence), the patient player can always secure at least his commitment payoff by building a reputation, regardless of K. However, the welfare of the short-run players (i.e., group efficiency) is maximized in all equilibria only if K is below a threshold (cutoff). For K above the cutoff, there exist equilibria where the short-run players' welfare is strictly below first-best, because longer memory makes it harder to motivate short-run players to punish deviations. Thus, increasing the memory window K can have a non-monotonic or even negative effect on group efficiency. The results are robust to various extensions, including imperfect monitoring and coarser information structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in repeated games with reputation and limited memory, the ability of short-run players to punish deviations (and thus sustain high efficiency) depends critically on the memory length K. If K is too large, the threat of punishment becomes less effective, and group efficiency can fall below first-best in some equilibria. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that in environments where punishment is implemented via reputation and memory is limited to summary statistics, increasing the memory window does not necessarily improve efficiency and may even reduce it. The results are most directly relevant to settings where efficiency is determined by the ability to sustain cooperation through reputation and punishment, rather than direct PGG lab experiments. The findings are less directly applicable to standard PGGs with explicit punishment stages, but the mechanism insights are informative for predicting efficiency in repeated or reputation-based games.

- source: Ho, T. H. (1996). Finite automata play repeated prisoner's dilemma with information processing costs. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with boundedly rational agents (finite automata), cooperation and high efficiency can evolve even from hostile initial conditions, as long as there are no costs for strategy complexity. (2) Imposing a cost on the size of the automaton (i.e., the 'storage' or monitoring cost) destroys cooperation and reduces efficiency, with populations converging to simple, defecting strategies. (3) Imposing a cost on the frequency of switching states (i.e., 'retrieval' or attention cost) does not harm, and may even promote, cooperation and efficiency, especially if the cost is quadratic. (4) The results are robust to a range of simulation parameters. (5) The findings suggest that the form of cognitive or implementation cost is a critical moderator of whether punishment-like strategies (contingent defection) can sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player games structurally similar to public goods games (but not group PGGs), the ability to implement contingent punishment (e.g., Tit-for-Tat or more sophisticated automata) is crucial for sustaining high efficiency. However, if the game design imposes costs on monitoring or strategy complexity (analogous to high punishment cost or high cognitive burden), efficiency collapses and simple, non-cooperative strategies dominate. In contrast, costs on the frequency of switching (analogous to costs for changing behavior or attention) do not undermine efficiency. For prediction, this suggests that the presence and form of punishment, and especially the cost structure for implementing contingent strategies, are key moderators of the efficiency effect. The results are most directly relevant to two-player, long-horizon, repeated games with deterministic feedback and no communication.

- source: Fujiwara-Greve, T., Okuno-Fujiwara, M., & Suzuki, N. (2015). Efficiency may improve when defectors exist. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the most efficient Nash equilibrium is not the symmetric trust-building equilibrium (where all players initially defect to build trust), but rather a bimorphic equilibrium where some players always cooperate and others always defect and leave. This equilibrium is locally stable and, under a 'small stake' condition (when the temptation to defect is not too large), is more efficient than any equilibrium with only trust-building strategies. The efficiency gain comes from allowing a higher share of cooperators, even though some are exploited by defectors. The paper provides explicit conditions for when this efficiency dominance holds and characterizes the parameter regions where the presence of defectors improves efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with endogenous partner choice and no information flow, the introduction of unconditional defectors can increase equilibrium efficiency compared to trust-building (punishment-based) equilibria, especially when the temptation to defect is not too large (small stake condition) and the effective discount factor (survival rate) is high. The efficiency of the equilibrium is explicitly characterized as a function of the payoff parameters and the share of cooperators/defectors. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not feature explicit costly punishment, it is highly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner selection and limited punishment options. The results suggest that, under certain conditions, the presence of defectors can be efficiency-improving, and that efficiency does not always monotonically increase with the strength or prevalence of punishment strategies.

- source: Lipman, B. L., & Wang, R. Q. (2000). Switching costs in frequently repeated games. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that adding small switching costs to frequently repeated games can overturn standard results from finitely repeated game theory. In the Prisoners' Dilemma, switching costs can enable cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) if the incentive to punish is greater than the incentive to cheat, but can also make cooperation impossible if the reverse is true. In coordination games, switching costs can make commitment to efficient outcomes credible, sometimes leading to unique, efficient equilibria. In some games, switching costs allow for more complex credible threats, which can result in equilibrium payoffs above Stackelberg levels for some players. The overall effect of switching costs on efficiency is thus mixed and highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, switching costs that make threats and commitments credible) on efficiency in repeated games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure and the relative size of incentives to cooperate, cheat, or punish. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment (or switching costs) on efficiency cannot be determined solely from the presence of punishment, but must also account for the specific incentive structure of the game.
