In some cases, enabling punishment (via switching costs) increases efficiency by sustaining cooperation; in others, it has no effect or even reduces efficiency by making punishment non-credible. The results are not directly in public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for settings where punishment credibility and switching costs are relevant.

- source: Bicchieri, C., Duffy, J., & Tolle, G. (2004). Trust among strangers. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot trust games, strategies that do not trust or reciprocate dominate, resulting in low average payoffs. When the game is repeated for more rounds (increasing the shadow of the future), conditional strategies that punish defection (e.g., grim trigger, hopeful) become viable and eventually dominate the population, leading to the emergence of a norm of trust and reciprocation and higher average payoffs. The transition from non-cooperation to cooperation occurs as the number of rounds increases beyond a threshold (typically r > 2 or 3). The stable outcome is a polymorphic population of conditional strategies supporting trust/reciprocation. The results are robust to initial conditions and to the inclusion of stochasticity in the evolutionary process.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments, the length of the repeated interaction (number of rounds) is a critical moderator of the emergence of cooperation and higher efficiency. When the game is one-shot or very short, efficiency remains low due to dominance of non-cooperative strategies. As the number of rounds increases, conditional strategies that implement punishment by withholding trust/reciprocation after defection can support the emergence of trust and higher efficiency. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, repeated interaction alone can enable the emergence of efficient, cooperative outcomes if conditional strategies are available. The model does not directly manipulate or analyze explicit punishment cost or technology, but the logic of conditional strategies is analogous to endogenous punishment via future play. The results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated trust games as a function of the number of rounds and the available strategy set.

- source: Ghachem, M. (2016). The institution as a blunt instrument: Cooperation through imperfect observability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that institutional tagging, even when imperfect, can substantially increase the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in social dilemmas like the Prisoner's Dilemma. The key mechanism is that tagged cooperators are more likely to be chosen for interaction, leading to non-uniform interaction rates that favor cooperators. The model provides analytical conditions under which cooperation is favored by selection, is risk dominant, or is more abundant in the mutation-selection equilibrium. The positive effect on efficiency is robust to moderate costs of tag production and search. The effect is highly sensitive to the institution's error rates, especially the rate at which defectors are mistakenly tagged as cooperators. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but institutional tagging serves a similar function by enabling partner selection based on observed tags.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional mechanisms which increase the observability of cooperation (even imperfectly) can increase group efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. While not a public goods game per se, the findings are adjacent and likely transferable to PGG-like settings where partner choice or reputation is possible. The model suggests that, in environments where institutions can credibly tag cooperators and individuals can use these tags to select partners, enabling such mechanisms will increase efficiency relative to a control with no tagging. The effect is stronger when the institution is more accurate (especially in avoiding false positives for defectors), when more of the population is screened, and when individuals can search more for partners. The results are less directly applicable to settings where punishment is the main intervention, but the underlying logic (institutional information enabling discrimination) is relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like or reputation-based interventions.

- source: Ochea, M. I. (2013). Evolution of repeated prisoner's dilemma play under logit dynamics. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with a finite set of memory-one strategies, the long-run average payoff (efficiency) and the prevalence of cooperation depend critically on the mix of strategies present and the game parameters. In some ecologies, strategies like Pavlov (WSLS), which can be interpreted as implementing endogenous punishment (by defecting after being defected upon), can dominate and sustain high payoffs, especially when unconditional cooperators are absent. However, the introduction of unconditional cooperators (AllC) can destabilize cooperation and lead to the dominance of defectors (AllD), reducing efficiency. The dynamics can be complex, with cycles and chaos emerging for intermediate values of the intensity of choice parameter. The paper provides explicit formulas for average payoffs and analyzes bifurcations in the system as parameters change. Overall, the effect of 'punishment-like' strategies on efficiency is mixed and highly sensitive to the strategic environment and parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like strategies (e.g., Pavlov/WSLS) on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games is highly context-dependent. In environments without unconditional cooperators, such strategies can sustain cooperation and high payoffs, but their effectiveness is undermined when unconditional cooperators are present. The results suggest that the presence or absence of certain strategy types (especially unconditional cooperators) and the values of key parameters (benefit of cooperation, error rates, intensity of choice) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment (or punishment-like strategies) on efficiency cannot be determined solely by the presence of punishment, but must account for the broader strategic ecology and parameter settings. The findings are most directly relevant to repeated PD-like environments, but may inform predictions in PGG-like settings by analogy.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2024). The universal probabilistic reward based on the difference of payoff realizes the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a universal probabilistic reward mechanism, where players with higher payoffs reward their neighbors (cooperators or defectors) with a probability proportional to the payoff difference, leads to a significant increase in both cooperation and average group payoff (efficiency) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and a range of reward coefficients (t), except when t=0 or t=1, where the mechanism is inactive. The study provides detailed analysis of how the speed and stability of cooperation and efficiency depend on the reward parameter and network structure. No punishment mechanism is implemented in the model, but the paper situates its contribution in the context of prior work on both reward and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a universal, payoff-difference-based reward mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when rewards are given to both cooperators and defectors. While the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, it is highly informative for predicting the effects of reward-based interventions on efficiency, especially in networked or spatially structured games. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, well-designed reward mechanisms can achieve high efficiency. However, the results may not directly transfer to public goods games with explicit punishment options, as the model does not include a punishment treatment or control. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that reward mechanisms (especially those not limited to cooperators) can be highly effective at increasing efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of adding punishment to a baseline.

- source: Kraines, D. P., & Kraines, V. Y. (2000). Natural selection of memory-one strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with memory-one strategies and evolutionary dynamics, the population tends to evolve toward a small set of stable strategies. The Pavlov (Win-Stay Lose-Switch) strategy is particularly robust, achieving high average payoffs and being resistant to invasion by other strategies, especially in the presence of noise. The analysis partitions the space of strategies into basins of attraction: cooperative (high payoff), defecting (low payoff), alternating (zero payoff), and a small equilibrium plane. The size of these basins depends on the initial conditions and the payoff matrix. The paper also shows that strategies that combine trust with readiness to retaliate (but not excessive forgiveness) are most successful. While explicit punishment mechanisms are not modeled, the evolutionary success of strategies that retaliate after being exploited is highlighted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or explicit punishment institutions, it analyzes the efficiency (average payoff) of strategies in a closely related repeated social dilemma (the IPD) under evolutionary dynamics. The findings suggest that strategies incorporating contingent retaliation (analogous to punishment) can sustain high efficiency, but only if they avoid excessive forgiveness or spite. The mapping of strategy space to efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the likely efficiency of repeated games with different behavioral rules, especially regarding the role of contingent sanctions. However, the lack of explicit group structure, multi-player interactions, and institutional punishment limits direct transfer to public goods games.

- source: Shibayama, S. (2015). Academic commercialization and changing nature of academic cooperation. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that increasing the prevalence of commercial actors (who do not cooperate gratis) in an academic community undermines the reputation mechanism that sustains indirect reciprocity. This leads to a decline in unconditional cooperation, a rise in demand for direct rewards (e.g., coauthorship, barter), and an increase in abstention from cooperation. The overall effect is a reduction in group efficiency and mean payoff, as the system moves away from the collectively optimal state. Centralized rewarding can partially restore cooperation and efficiency, but only if the reward is carefully calibrated; excessive rewards can backfire by incentivizing defectors. The model provides formal predictions and phase diagrams showing how the basin of attraction for efficient cooperation shrinks as commercialization increases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and design of punishment, reward, and reputation mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While not a direct PGG or lab experiment, the model is closely related and analyzes efficiency outcomes as a function of game design parameters (e.g., cost/benefit of cooperation, reward size, reputation availability, prevalence of defectors). For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling effective punishment or reward (especially centralized, carefully sized rewards) can increase efficiency, but that the presence of many defectors or commercial actors will reduce the effectiveness of reputation-based cooperation and lower efficiency. The model also highlights the importance of the matching rate for rewards and the availability of reputation information. However, the absence of empirical or experimental data means that quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Amegashie, J. A., & Runkel, M. (2012). The Paradox of Revenge in Conflicts. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to conventional wisdom, the desire for revenge does not always escalate conflict. In a two-period contest model, the anticipation of revenge (self-deterrence effect) can lead factions to reduce their initial effort, resulting in lower total conflict costs (higher efficiency) than in a setting without revenge motives. The paradox is most likely when the benefit of revenge is highly elastic to past destruction or when there are asymmetries in the value of revenge or resource valuation between factions. The model provides explicit comparative statics and closed-form solutions for aggregate effort with and without revenge, showing conditions under which efficiency is improved by the possibility of revenge.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the introduction of a punishment/revenge mechanism can, under certain conditions, increase efficiency (reduce total cost) in conflict-like games by inducing self-deterrence. While the setting is a two-player, two-period conflict game (not a standard public goods game), the mechanism is closely related to punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, when the threat of punishment is credible and especially when the benefit of punishment is highly sensitive to prior harm, the net effect on efficiency can be positive. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the possibility that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially in asymmetric or highly elastic settings, but the result is context-dependent and may not generalize to all PGG-like environments.

- source: Mani, A., Rahwan, I., & Pentland, A. (2013). Inducing Peer Pressure to Promote Cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing peer pressure mechanisms—where peers are incentivized to exert costly pressure on each other to reduce negative externalities—can lead to higher social surplus (efficiency) than traditional Pigouvian mechanisms, especially in large, sparse networks. Theoretical results show that peer pressure reduces equilibrium action levels (i.e., reduces over-consumption or under-contribution), but not all the way to the social optimum unless subsidized. Social mechanisms that reward peers for their neighbors' good behavior are more cost-effective than direct rewards to individuals. The model predicts that the efficiency gain from peer pressure increases as the cost of exerting pressure decreases and as social ties strengthen. An illustrative experiment on physical activity supports these predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling peer-based sanctions or rewards (peer pressure) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of peer pressure is moderate and the network is sparse. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing peer-based punishment or reward mechanisms should increase efficiency relative to control, and that the effect is stronger when the cost of peer pressure is lower and when social ties are stronger. The model is not an exact PGG, but is closely related and the efficiency results are directly relevant. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates for standard PGG parameters, but the qualitative direction is clear: peer pressure mechanisms are more efficient than no intervention, and often more efficient than centralized punishment/reward, especially at low budgets.

- source: Al-Dhanhani, A., Mizouni, R., Otrok, H., & Al-Rubaie, A. (2014). A game theoretical model for collaborative groups in social applications. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of reciprocity and punishment strategies in repeated social dilemmas (modeled as asynchronous iterated prisoner's dilemma) has a strong effect on group efficiency and the distribution of gains between cooperative and selfish (free riding) users. Classical Tit for Tat leads to deadlock and declining payoffs. Generous Tit for Tat avoids deadlock and increases group gain, but allows free riders to benefit disproportionately. Reputation-based Tit for Tat reduces free rider gain but can also reduce cooperative user gain, especially if the reputation threshold is set high or the initialization phase is short. Group reputation-based Tit for Tat (indirect reciprocity) achieves the highest cooperative user gain and lowest free rider gain, thus maximizing group efficiency and survivability. The presence of free riders always reduces group efficiency, but the right punishment/reciprocity mechanism can mitigate this loss.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment/reciprocity mechanisms (especially those based on group reputation and indirect reciprocity) can significantly improve group efficiency in repeated social dilemma environments, even in the presence of free riders. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related and the efficiency outcomes are directly comparable. The findings suggest that enabling punishment or exclusion based on group-level reputation (rather than only direct reciprocity) is likely to increase efficiency relative to no punishment or to simple direct reciprocity. The results are most relevant for settings with repeated interactions, observable histories, and the ability to track and act on group-level reputation. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to all design dimensions, but the qualitative direction is clear: more sophisticated, group-based punishment/reciprocity mechanisms are more effective at sustaining efficiency.

- source: Lütz, A. F., Amaral, M. A., Braga, I., & Wardil, L. (2023). Invasion of Optimal Social Contracts. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population playing a stag-hunt game (representing a social contract), the introduction of a moderate diversity incentive (modeled as a snowdrift game in a focal group) allows the population to overcome the invasion barrier and reach the globally optimal equilibrium (all adopting the efficient norm A). This transition increases group efficiency (total payoff). If the diversity incentive is too strong, the system may stabilize at a mixed equilibrium, but turning off the incentive after the transition can still result in full adoption of the optimal norm. The effect is strongest when the number of group connections is moderate; if individuals are too highly connected, the social pressure of the stag-hunt dominates and the incentive is less effective. The findings are supported by analytical and simulation results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that introducing a moderate incentive for diversity (analogous to a reward or tolerance for deviance) can increase group efficiency in coordination games with multiple equilibria, by enabling the population to escape suboptimal equilibria. While the model does not directly study public goods games or punishment, it is adjacent in that it addresses how incentive structures and group connectivity affect the ability to reach efficient outcomes. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that mechanisms which lower the cost or risk of deviating from the majority (even temporarily) can facilitate transitions to higher-efficiency equilibria, especially in moderately connected groups. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so direct quantitative predictions for punishment-enabled PGGs are not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). The shift between the Red Queen and the Red King effects in mutualisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in multiplayer snowdrift games modeling mutualisms, the introduction and increase of a collective reward mechanism can shift the allocation of mutualistic benefits from favoring the faster-evolving species (Red Queen effect) to favoring the slower-evolving species (Red King effect). Asymmetries in the number of interacting partners or in payoffs can also shift the balance of surplus allocation. The model predicts that, depending on the reward intensity and asymmetries, the efficiency (total surplus generated and its division) can be increased or made more unequal. The model is theoretical and does not provide empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the presence and intensity of a reward mechanism (analogous to a positive incentive or bonus for cooperation) can substantially alter the efficiency and allocation of surplus in multiplayer social dilemmas. While it does not directly model punishment or public goods games, its results suggest that reward mechanisms can increase group efficiency and shift the distribution of payoffs, especially in the presence of asymmetries. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient intensity) can increase efficiency and alter the distribution of payoffs, but the effect depends on the details of the reward and the structure of the game (e.g., group size, asymmetries). The findings are most relevant for settings with reward rather than punishment, and for snowdrift-like rather than public-goods-like games.

- source: Masuda, N. (2012). Ingroup favoritism and intergroup cooperation under indirect reciprocity based on group reputation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of cooperation in group-structured indirect reciprocity games depends critically on the social norm used for reputation assignment and the frequency of intragroup versus intergroup interactions. Full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency, with payoff b-c per round) is stable only when all observers use a 'serious' social norm (standing or judging) for all types of interactions. If observers use double standards (different norms for ingroup and outgroup interactions), then ingroup favoritism emerges, leading to lower efficiency (payoff reduced by the fraction of intergroup interactions). The key condition for any cooperation to be stable is b*r_in > c, where r_in is the probability of intragroup interaction. The model shows that changing the social norm (e.g., forcing consistency across interaction types) can shift the equilibrium from inefficient ingroup favoritism to efficient full cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but reputation loss/gain serves a similar function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with group structure. It shows that the efficiency outcome (full cooperation, partial, or ingroup favoritism) is determined by the social norm for reputation assignment and the structure of interactions (r_in). In the absence of explicit punishment or reward, but with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, efficiency is maximized only if the social norm is consistent and 'serious' (standing/judging) across all interaction types. If the game design allows or encourages double standards (different norms for ingroup/outgroup), expect lower efficiency due to ingroup favoritism. The model's efficiency results are directly parameterized by the benefit-to-cost ratio and the frequency of intragroup interaction, which can be mapped to MPCR and group structure in PGG-like games. However, the absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most applicable to games where reputation is the main enforcement mechanism.

- source: KRAINES, D., & KRAINES, V. (1993). LEARNING TO COOPERATE WITH PAVLOV - AN ADAPTIVE STRATEGY FOR THE ITERATED PRISONERS-DILEMMA WITH NOISE. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that Pavlovian strategies, which adapt their probability of cooperation based on positive and negative payoffs (interpreted as rewards and punishments), can achieve high average payoffs and are robust to noise in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma. Pavlov can exploit overly cooperative opponents (like All-C), is not easily exploited except by always-defect strategies, and can recover mutual cooperation after errors more quickly than Tit-for-Tat (TFT). The optimal learning rate (parameter n) balances exploitability and speed of learning, with n=3 or 4 performing best in simulations. The paper provides detailed payoff tables and Markov chain analyses for various strategy matchups, but does not study group interactions, explicit peer punishment, or public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in public goods games, as it does not study group games, peer punishment, or any of the relevant game design dimensions. However, it is informative about how strategies that adapt to positive and negative payoffs (interpreted as endogenous rewards and punishments) can lead to high efficiency and mutual cooperation in repeated dyadic dilemmas. The findings suggest that adaptive learning can be robust to noise and can outperform simple reciprocity (TFT) in some environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address the effect of enabling punishment mechanisms or any of the design dimensions in public goods games.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2023). Grouping promotes both partnership and rivalry with long memory in direct reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that group structure, when combined with the availability of longer-memory strategies (which allow for friendly rival strategies), leads to the evolution of nearly full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the repeated donation game. In well-mixed populations, longer memory does not have this effect. The results are robust to the benefit-to-cost ratio and to details of the evolutionary process. The paper does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, but the repeated donation game is adjacent to PGGs, and friendly rival strategies function as a form of endogenous sanctioning (never allowing the co-player to earn more). Efficiency is measured as the fraction of time mutual cooperation is achieved, which is equivalent to group payoff relative to the maximum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated donation games (adjacent to PGGs), the combination of group structure and the availability of longer-memory strategies (enabling friendly rival strategies) can dramatically increase efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise be difficult. While there is no explicit punishment mechanism, friendly rival strategies act as endogenous enforcers of cooperation by never allowing the co-player to earn more. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in environments with group structure and sufficient strategic complexity, efficiency can be much higher than in well-mixed or memory-limited settings, even without explicit punishment. However, the results may not transfer directly to multi-player PGGs or to settings with explicit costly punishment.

- source: Zimmermann, M. G., & Eguíluz, V. M. (2005). Cooperation, social networks, and the emergence of leadership in a prisoner's dilemma with adaptive local interactions - art. no. 056118. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that network adaptation (agents breaking unsatisfactory links and seeking new partners) enables the emergence of highly cooperative, high-efficiency steady states in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma. In these states, most agents cooperate, and the group achieves high average payoffs, even though defectors individually do better than cooperators. The system is robust to a range of parameters, but can be destabilized by noise or by the loss of key cooperative leaders, leading to cascades of defection and efficiency collapse. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms, but the ability to break links with defectors functions as a form of social sanction/ostracism. The results show that such adaptive partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that adaptive partner selection (ostracism, link breaking) in networked social dilemmas can dramatically increase group efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment or reward. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that enabling mechanisms for social exclusion or network adaptation (even if not labeled as 'punishment') can have similar or greater positive effects on efficiency as explicit punishment in public goods games. However, the model is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and the 'punishment' is indirect (ostracism, not costly reduction of others' payoffs). The results are most relevant for predicting the effects of partner choice, ostracism, or network adaptation, and less directly for classic costly punishment. The paper is highly informative for adjacent mechanisms but should be used with caution for direct predictions about explicit punishment in PGGs.

- source: 
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when multilateral punishment is ineffective due to group size and heterogeneity, agents can still achieve efficient, self-enforcing exchange by using costly, observable signals that reduce social distance (e.g., adopting local customs, gift giving, religious conversion). These signals are more costly for cheaters (impatient agents) than for cooperators (patient agents), allowing communities to screen out cheaters and admit only those likely to cooperate. In equilibrium, this mechanism exhausts the gains from trade and achieves social efficiency, even without formal enforcement or effective punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that, in environments where punishment is not feasible or effective (e.g., large, heterogeneous groups), alternative mechanisms such as costly signaling can substitute for punishment to achieve efficient outcomes. For the prediction task, this suggests that the absence of punishment does not necessarily preclude high efficiency if credible signaling mechanisms are available and used. However, the model is not a direct public goods game and does not provide quantitative estimates or experimental evidence, so its predictive value is mainly conceptual and applies to adjacent settings where punishment is infeasible.

- source: Kayser, K., & Lampert, A. (2021). Survivor's dilemma: The evolution of cooperation in volatile environments. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in the survivor's dilemma, cooperation can be evolutionarily stable if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and/or environmental volatility is low. In these cases, mutual cooperation leads to higher expected lifespan (efficiency) for all. However, if the benefit-to-cost ratio is low or volatility is high, defection dominates and efficiency is lower. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, indirect punishment occurs because defectors lose partners and thus reduce their own survival prospects. The findings are presented for both 2- and 3-player games, with detailed analysis of parameter regions where cooperation or defection is stable. The results are directly about efficiency (expected lifespan) and its dependence on game parameters, but do not address the effect of enabling or disabling explicit punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence about how efficiency (expected lifespan) in repeated social dilemmas depends on group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and environmental volatility. It does not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment, as no explicit punishment mechanism is present. However, it does show that indirect forms of 'punishment' (loss of cooperative partners) can sustain cooperation and efficiency under certain conditions. For prediction tasks involving explicit punishment in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation depend strongly on the benefit-to-cost ratio and the likelihood of future interaction (volatility), and that even without explicit punishment, cooperation can sometimes be stable. The model is adjacent to PGGs and does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment.

- source: Trejo, K. K., Juarez, R., Clempner, J. B., & Poznyak, A. S. (2023). Non-Cooperative Bargaining with Unsophisticated Agents. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing penalty mechanisms for deviation and time in bargaining games with myopic agents ensures convergence to efficient (Pareto optimal or Nash) outcomes, but at a cost: the process is slower and less efficient than with fully rational, forward-looking agents. The efficiency loss is quantifiable as the 'price of unsophistication,' and depends on the penalty parameters and the agents' behavior. The results are robust across a wide class of utility functions and game structures, including Markov processes. The paper does not study public goods games or peer punishment, but the penalty mechanism is analogous to centralized sanctions rather than decentralized peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of peer punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies bargaining games with centralized penalty mechanisms and myopic agents. However, it provides indirect evidence that introducing penalty costs can improve convergence to efficient outcomes even with unsophisticated agents, though always with some efficiency loss relative to the ideal. The results suggest that the design and magnitude of penalties (punishment costs) are critical for achieving near-efficient outcomes, but the context is bargaining, not public goods provision. The findings may be useful for understanding the general role of sanctions in promoting efficiency, but should be transferred to PGG-like settings with caution.

- source: Forges, F., & Horst, U. (2018). Sender-receiver games with cooperation. *Journal of Mathematical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in sender-receiver games with the possibility of credible punishment (a uniform punishment decision), there always exists an equilibrium (CTE) where both players cooperate and communicate, leading to expected payoffs for both that are at least as high as in the baseline game without communication, and often strictly higher. The existence of credible punishment is key: without it, such efficient equilibria may not exist. The paper provides examples where the introduction of a UPD enables partially revealing communication and joint decisions that strictly improve welfare for both players. The findings are theoretical and general, but the examples show that the efficiency gain from enabling credible punishment can be substantial.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling credible punishment (via a uniform punishment decision) in games with communication and cooperation can increase group efficiency/welfare relative to a no-punishment baseline. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the logic is adjacent: credible punishment for non-cooperation enables more efficient equilibria. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that, in environments where a credible punishment mechanism is available and can be used as a threat, enabling punishment should not decrease and may increase efficiency, sometimes strictly. The magnitude and details depend on the structure of the game and the nature of the punishment, but the direction is clear. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). Persistent parochialism: trust and exclusion in ethnic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that parochial networks can achieve higher efficiency (average payoff) than anonymous markets in social dilemma environments, due to better information and informal enforcement (via monitoring and exclusion). However, the relationship between parochialism and efficiency is non-linear: too little parochialism leads to poor information and low trust, while too much parochialism reduces trading opportunities and group size, also lowering efficiency. The model includes a monitoring/inspection strategy (analogous to punishment), which, when information is sufficiently accurate, supports a stable trust equilibrium with higher payoffs. The optimal efficiency is achieved at an intermediate level of parochialism, balancing enforcement and trading opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (monitoring, exclusion) on efficiency in social dilemma games depends on the information structure and group composition. Efficiency is maximized at an intermediate level of parochialism, where information is good enough to support trust and informal enforcement, but group size and trading opportunities are not too restricted. The model suggests that enabling punishment/monitoring can increase efficiency relative to a control (anonymous market) when information is sufficiently accurate and group size is within a viable range. However, excessive exclusion (high parochialism) can reduce efficiency by limiting opportunities. The findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment is implemented via monitoring and exclusion, rather than costly direct punishment as in standard PGGs.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2017). The evolution of conditional moral assessment in indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the 'Staying' norm, which prescribes abstaining from moral assessment when a donor interacts with a recipient of bad reputation, is more effective at establishing and maintaining cooperation than the four most studied social norms in indirect reciprocity. Under Staying, discriminators (conditional cooperators) are more likely to invade populations of defectors, and the evolutionary dynamics favor higher average payoffs and more efficient group outcomes. The model demonstrates that the range of initial conditions leading to efficient, cooperative equilibria is wider under Staying, and that the strict application of moral judgment (as in other norms) is less effective for promoting efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games (a close relative of public goods games), the design of the assessment/punishment norm can have a large effect on group efficiency. Specifically, conditional assessment (Staying) leads to higher efficiency than unconditional assessment (Scoring, etc.), especially when assessment errors are low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the details of the punishment/assessment mechanism (e.g., whether punishment is conditional or unconditional, and how errors are handled) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. The findings support the idea that more nuanced, context-sensitive punishment/assessment rules can yield higher efficiency than strict, always-on punishment rules.

- source: Iwamura, Y., Nagashima, K., & Tanimoto, J. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics of a 3-strategy game: Cooperator, defector and costly cooperative loner strategic types. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a costly cooperative loner (CL) as a third strategy in a prisoner's dilemma game does not prevent the collapse to all-defection, nor does it improve social average payoff (efficiency) compared to the standard two-strategy game. The CL strategy is always outcompeted and goes extinct, after which defectors dominate. This is in contrast to models with costly punishment, where punishment can sometimes sustain cooperation and higher efficiency. The paper provides a detailed analysis of the payoff matrix and evolutionary trajectories, showing that the addition of CL does not alter the efficiency outcome: the system always ends in the least efficient (all-defector) state.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a negative case: simply adding a third strategy that is not an active punisher (but pays a cost to avoid defectors) does not improve efficiency in a prisoner's dilemma environment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'costly loner' or similar non-punishing third strategy is not sufficient to increase efficiency. The results reinforce that only certain types of third strategies (e.g., active costly punishment or rewarding) can shift efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and may not generalize to finite or structured populations. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment per se, but its comparison to punishment models supports the view that punishment (with sufficient impact and cost structure) is necessary for efficiency gains in these settings.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2007). Indirect reciprocity in asymmetric interactions: when apparent altruism facilitates profitable exploitation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric games where only one party (the actor) can help or exploit, and the other (the recipient) can only choose whether to interact, a form of indirect reciprocity can evolve. Actors help when the gains from exploitation are low to maintain a positive image, which encourages recipients to approach them in the future, allowing profitable exploitation when the gains are high. This strategy is stable and increases the long-term average payoff for both actors and recipients, provided certain conditions on observability, state-switching, and payoff parameters are met. The model shows that efficiency (average group payoff) is higher under these conditions than if actors always exploit or if recipients always avoid interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in asymmetric games with image scoring and conditional approach, mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, avoidance of exploiters) can increase efficiency by supporting conditional cooperation. The findings suggest that when harmful behavior is observable and actors' states vary, efficiency is maximized for intermediate values of key parameters (cost of harm, switching probability, observability). While not a PGG or direct punishment setting, the model is adjacent and supports the idea that social mechanisms that deter exploitation (even without direct punishment) can improve group efficiency. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling mechanisms for reputation-based avoidance or indirect sanctions can increase efficiency, especially when exploitation is not always profitable and can be detected.

- source:
