  findings: The paper finds that in iterated two-person games with group-level mixed strategies and no individual recognition, evolutionary dynamics lead to the extinction of one group as mistrust parameters evolve. This occurs even in games (like the Prisoner's Dilemma) where initial conditions could support stable coexistence. The absence of individual recognition prevents the emergence of reputation, reciprocation, and retribution (punishment), which are necessary for stable cooperation and high group efficiency. As a result, group-level efficiency is undermined, and one group is always driven to extinction. The model is theoretical and does not directly manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, but frames punishment as a key missing ingredient for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but strong theoretical evidence that the absence of individual recognition (and thus the inability to implement punishment or reputation-based strategies) leads to the collapse of group-level efficiency and the extinction of one group in iterated two-person games. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that in environments where punishment is not possible (due to lack of individual identification), efficiency will be low and unstable. Conversely, the presence of punishment (enabled by individual recognition) is implied to be necessary for stable, high-efficiency outcomes. However, the model does not directly analyze public goods games or explicit punishment treatments, so its relevance is more mechanistic and cautionary than directly predictive for PGG-like lab experiments.

- source: Salahshour, M., & Couzin, I. D. (2025). Evolution of altruistic rationality provides a solution to social dilemmas via rational reciprocity. *Physical Review Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models where agents can evolve their degree of other-regarding preferences (altruism), populations transition from mutual defection (low efficiency) to high cooperation (high efficiency) in social dilemmas like the Prisoner's Dilemma, provided the benefit of cooperation is sufficiently high. This transition is robust to population structure and does not require explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. Instead, 'punishment' of selfishness occurs via the rational withholding of cooperation by conditional cooperators. The resulting group efficiency (average payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) increases substantially compared to the Nash equilibrium of selfish agents. The findings generalize across different two-person two-strategy games, with the mechanism being most pronounced in the Prisoner's Dilemma and also effective in the Snowdrift and Stag Hunt games.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that allowing for the evolution of other-regarding preferences (subjective rationality) can lead to high efficiency in social dilemmas, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. In environments structurally similar to two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, Snowdrift, or Stag Hunt games, if agents can recognize and respond to others' types, group efficiency can approach the cooperative optimum. However, the model does not include explicit punishment costs, technologies, or public goods game features such as multi-player interactions, continuous contributions, or explicit sanctioning. Thus, while the findings are highly informative about the potential for endogenous cooperation and efficiency gains, they are only indirectly applicable to standard public goods games with explicit punishment treatments. The main implication is that mechanisms enabling recognition and conditional cooperation can substitute for explicit punishment in promoting efficiency.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2002). From parasitism to mutualism: partner control in asymmetric interactions. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The model demonstrates that in asymmetric interactions, the ability of the exploited party to terminate the interaction acts as a deterrent to exploitation, increasing the efficiency (joint payoff) of the system. As the exploiter's power to prolong the interaction increases, exploitation rises, the victim's payoff falls (eventually becoming negative), and the system shifts from mutualism to parasitism. The temptation to cheat (benefit/cost ratio) and the rate at which mutualistic benefits diminish also modulate these outcomes. The model is supported qualitatively by empirical data from cleaner-fish systems, where larger clients (with more to gain from cleaning) experience less exploitation and longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing the exploited party to control the duration of interaction (analogous to exit, partner choice, or punishment in PGG-like games) can increase efficiency by deterring exploitation. While not a PGG or direct punishment experiment, the model is relevant for predicting that increasing the ability of group members to sanction or exit (or otherwise control exploiters) will generally increase group efficiency, especially when the temptation to cheat is not overwhelming. The model also suggests that efficiency gains from punishment or partner control are greatest when the benefits of cooperation are high and the costs of cheating are high relative to the exploiter's gain. However, the model does not address repeated group interactions, explicit punishment costs, or the full range of PGG design dimensions, so its predictive value is indirect.

- source: Peña, J., Heifetz, A., & Nöldeke, G. (2024). The shirker's dilemma and the prospect of cooperation in large groups. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the shirker's dilemma, as group size increases, the proportion of volunteers at the stable equilibrium increases (for low enough costs), but the expected payoff (efficiency) decreases. The probability that the public good is provided can either increase or decrease with group size, depending on the cost of cooperation. In the limit of very large groups and low costs, the proportion of volunteers approaches one, and the expected payoff and success probability converge to positive values. However, the basin of attraction for the cooperative equilibrium shrinks with group size, making cooperation less likely to be reached from random initial conditions. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in a binary-action threshold collective action game (shirker's dilemma), increasing group size generally reduces efficiency (expected payoff at equilibrium), even though the proportion of volunteers may increase. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, larger groups are less efficient, and the probability of successful collective action may not improve with group size. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, in similar threshold public goods environments without punishment, efficiency will decrease as group size increases. However, because the model does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it does provide a theoretical baseline for control (no-punishment) conditions in threshold games.

- source: Auerswald, H., Schmidt, C., Thum, M., & Torsvik, G. (2018). Teams in a public goods experiment with punishment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The evolutionary IPD with choice and refusal of partners (IPD/CR) produces higher average fitness (group efficiency) than random partner matching, with most runs evolving to near full cooperation. The average fitness clusters around a few regions, with the highest corresponding to mutual cooperation. However, if the refusal payoff is high or players are highly intolerant of defection, populations can become trapped in low-efficiency wallflower states. Sensitivity analyses show that the refusal payoff, minimum tolerance level, and memory weight are key moderators: high refusal payoffs or high intolerance increase the risk of social isolation and lower efficiency. Increasing population size reduces the risk of wallflower ecologies. The findings are robust across different strategy representations and parameter sweeps.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling partner selection (choice and refusal) in repeated social dilemmas can increase group efficiency compared to random matching, especially when refusal payoffs and intolerance are not too high. For prediction tasks, the presence of a partner selection mechanism (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be expected to increase efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit structure of refusal does not incentivize social isolation. The results are most directly relevant to settings where agents can avoid defectors, but less so to standard public goods games with centralized punishment. The findings suggest that game design dimensions such as the cost of refusal, tolerance thresholds, and population size are critical moderators of the efficiency effect.

- source: Kroumi, D. (2025). The role of selective extinction in promoting cooperation in a many demes model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that structuring a population into demes and introducing selective extinction—whereby groups with more defectors are more likely to go extinct—can dramatically increase the evolutionary success (fixation probability) of cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma. Without selective extinction (\alpha=0), defection is always favored, and cooperation cannot persist. As the selective extinction parameter \alpha increases, the fixation probability of cooperation increases, and that of defection decreases. There are explicit threshold values of \alpha (functions of game payoffs and demographic parameters) above which cooperation is favored by selection, and above which it is more likely to fix than defection. The model also shows how the baseline extinction rate (v) and the structure of payoffs (T-R, P-S) modulate these thresholds. The results are robust to several alternative life-cycle assumptions. The main outcome is that group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction) can overcome the individual-level advantage of defection and promote group-level efficiency (cooperation).
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in structured populations with group-level demographic feedback (selective extinction of defect-dominated groups), the efficiency of the system (measured by the fixation probability of cooperation and thus long-run group payoff) can be substantially increased by increasing the strength of selective extinction (\alpha). In the absence of such feedback, efficiency remains low (defection dominates). The model gives explicit formulas for the critical value of \alpha needed to shift the system from defection-dominated to cooperation-dominated, as a function of game payoffs and demographic parameters (deme size, number of demes, baseline extinction rate). While the model is not a direct public goods game and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, the selective extinction process is functionally analogous to a group-level sanction. Thus, the findings support the prediction that introducing or strengthening group-level sanctions (even if not individual punishment) can increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when the control (no-sanction) efficiency is low.

- source: Bird, R. B., & Power, E. A. (2015). Prosocial signaling and cooperation among Martu hunters. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In populations of SLA agents without memory, those with low penalty sensitivity dominate, leading to high defection rates and low average payoffs. (2) SLA strategies are not stable against invasion by TFT or ALLD; TFT can dominate and lead to higher population payoffs. (3) Adding memory to SLA agents enables them to learn to cooperate with TFT, increasing cooperation and average payoffs, but this effect is limited to interactions with TFT; among themselves, SLA agents still defect. (4) The penalty parameter (analogous to punishment sensitivity) is a key moderator: higher penalty sensitivity leads to more cooperation and higher payoffs, but is outcompeted by less sensitive types in evolutionary dynamics. (5) The model predicts that, absent memory or external cooperative strategies, populations of simple learning agents will evolve toward defection and low efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments without explicit punishment mechanisms, agents with low sensitivity to penalties (i.e., low punishment cost or low punishment effectiveness) evolve toward defection and low efficiency. The findings suggest that, in analogous public goods games, the presence and effectiveness of punishment (or penalty sensitivity) are critical for sustaining cooperation and high efficiency. However, the model does not include explicit peer punishment as a game design feature, so direct quantitative predictions for the effect of enabling punishment in public goods games are limited. The results do support the qualitative prediction that increasing the effectiveness or salience of punishment (or penalty) responses can increase efficiency, but such strategies may be evolutionarily unstable without additional mechanisms (e.g., memory, reputation, or external enforcement).

- source: Garay, J., Csiszár, V., & Móri, T. F. (2014). Under multilevel selection: When shall you be neither spiteful nor envious?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations with multilevel selection, the evolutionary stability of strategies depends on the relative strength of group-level survival effects versus within-group competition. When group survival (due to predation or intergroup conflict) is highly sensitive to group size (i.e., total fecundity), strategies that maximize group fecundity (neutral or donator) are favored, leading to higher group efficiency. When group-level effects are weak, spiteful or envious strategies can dominate, reducing group efficiency. The model shows that increasing the benefit of donation or the cost of spite/envious behavior further promotes efficient, cooperative strategies. The results are robust to parameter changes and apply to both predation and intergroup conflict scenarios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in environments where group-level survival or payoff is highly sensitive to group size or total contribution, mechanisms that increase group efficiency (such as donation or cooperation) will be favored over spiteful or envious (punitive) strategies. While the model does not directly implement a public goods game with explicit punishment, it is adjacent: 'spiteful' and 'envious' strategies are analogous to costly punishment, and 'donator' is analogous to cooperation or reward. The findings suggest that increasing the importance of group-level outcomes (e.g., by increasing the marginal per-capita return or the impact of group payoff on survival) will make punishment less stable and cooperation more stable, thus increasing efficiency. However, the model is not a direct PGG and does not include explicit punishment technology or cost parameters as in standard PGG experiments.

- source: Lee, S., Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2021). Local stability of cooperation in a continuous model of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity depends on the sensitivity of the assessment and behavioral rules (social norms). It derives explicit conditions (in terms of derivatives of the assessment and behavioral functions) under which a cooperative norm is stable against local mutants, and shows that the benefit-to-cost ratio required for stability increases if reputation is insensitive to observed behavior or if cooperation is insensitive to recipient reputation. The analysis is supported by numerical simulations, which confirm the theoretical predictions about payoff differences between resident and mutant norms. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard PGGs, but reputation loss functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the expected payoff (efficiency) of the group under different norms and parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence about how the structure of social norms (in terms of sensitivity to behavior and reputation) affects the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity. While not a direct PGG or explicit punishment game, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is enforced by reputation-based indirect sanctions rather than direct punishment. The findings suggest that the efficiency of cooperation depends on the responsiveness of the reputation system and the behavioral rule, and that certain parameter regimes (high benefit-to-cost ratio, high sensitivity) are required for stable, efficient cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where indirect reciprocity is the main enforcement mechanism, and less so to direct punishment or reward treatments in PGGs.

- source: Forges, F., Horst, U., & Salomon, A. (2016). Feasibility and individual rationality in two-person Bayesian games. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in two-person Bayesian games with a single informed player, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy (UPS) for the uninformed player guarantees the existence of feasible, individually rational (i.e., efficient/welfare-maximizing) solutions. These solutions can be implemented as Nash equilibria of appropriately designed noncooperative games. The set of such payoffs coincides with the set of joint plan equilibrium payoffs in the infinitely repeated game. When UPS does not hold, feasible, individually rational solutions may not exist. The results generalize to cases where both players have private information, under certain conditions. The paper provides theoretical foundations for how punishment strategies can support efficient outcomes in games with incomplete information, but does not provide empirical estimates or experimental results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the ability to punish (specifically, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy) is crucial for achieving efficient, individually rational outcomes in two-person Bayesian games with incomplete information. For the downstream prediction task, the main implication is that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (punishment cost, punishment tech, and the ability to credibly threaten punishment) are key moderators of whether efficiency gains from punishment are possible. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates, and its results are not specific to public goods games or experimental designs. It is most useful for understanding the theoretical boundary conditions under which punishment can support efficiency, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency changes in experimental PGG-like environments.

- source: Bravo, G. (2011). Agents' beliefs and the evolution of institutions for common-pool resource management. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The key finding is that enabling agents to build management institutions in a CPR environment leads to much higher efficiency and group payoffs compared to scenarios where agents act only on individual beliefs. In the base (no-institution) model, payoffs are 12–35% of the optimum; with institution building, payoffs rise to 72–91% of the optimum. The effect is robust to changes in cost, resource abundance, and agent tolerance. The models suggest that the feedback loop between beliefs and institutions is critical: institutions prevent the selection of purely self-interested, over-exploiting agents, allowing cooperation and sustainable resource use to persist. The results are consistent with empirical findings from CPR experiments and field studies, though the models do not include explicit punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that the introduction of effective, collectively agreed-upon management institutions in CPR/public-goods-like environments can transform group efficiency from low (tragedy of the commons) to high (near-optimal). While the models do not include explicit punishment, the institution acts as a binding rule, functionally similar to enforced cooperation. The results suggest that, in designs where institution-building is possible and effective, one should predict a large positive jump in efficiency relative to the control. However, the absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means the findings are most directly relevant to institution-building or rule-setting treatments, not to punishment per se.

- source: Bear, A., & Rand, D. G. (2019). Can Strategic Ignorance Explain the Evolution of Love?. *Topics in Cognitive Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a partner-choice game where both agents can choose to search for better partners (look) or commit (not look), and can allow or disallow their partner to look, the only robust equilibrium is mutual looking and allowing. Commitment (strategic ignorance) does not evolve except when the cost of being abandoned is extremely high. The model directly analyzes the efficiency (expected payoff) of different equilibria and shows that, under most parameterizations, the efficiency of the 'punishment' (commitment-demanding) equilibrium is not higher and is not evolutionarily stable. The findings suggest that, in this class of games, enabling punishment-like mechanisms (demanding commitment) does not increase group efficiency and is not selected for, except in rare, extreme cases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical counterpoint to the idea that punishment or commitment mechanisms generally increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. In the context of partner-choice games with bilateral temptation and the ability to condition on partner behavior, the model predicts that enabling punishment (demanding commitment or non-looking) does not robustly increase efficiency and is not evolutionarily stable. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in games with similar structure (bilateral temptation, partner choice, visible actions), enabling punishment or commitment mechanisms is unlikely to increase efficiency unless there are very large costs to being abandoned. The paper is most informative for adjacent settings rather than standard public goods games, but its payoff-based analysis is directly relevant to efficiency predictions.

- source: Gokhale, C. S., Frean, M., & Rainey, P. B. (2023). Eco-evolutionary Logic of Mutualisms. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability and persistence of mutualisms (modeled as multiplayer snowdrift games) are strongly influenced by the balance between intra- and inter-species interactions, as well as by ecological factors such as seasonality and population density. The inclusion of within-species interactions and eco-evolutionary feedbacks can stabilize mutualisms and maintain high group-level productivity (efficiency) even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The model shows that nonlinearities, group size, and environmental fluctuations can qualitatively change the evolutionary outcomes, sometimes allowing coexistence of cooperative and selfish types, and sometimes leading to dominance or extinction. The framework is general and extensible, but the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the efficiency of multiplayer public-goods-like games (here, mutualisms modeled as snowdrift games) can be maintained or stabilized by ecological and evolutionary feedbacks, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. It suggests that predictions of efficiency should account for intra- and inter-group interactions, group size, seasonality, and density-dependence. However, because the paper does not model or analyze punishment or sanctioning directly, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its main value for the prediction task is in highlighting the importance of ecological and evolutionary context, and in providing a general modeling framework that could be extended to include punishment or reward.

- source: MacLeod, W. B. (2007). Reputations, relationships, and contract enforcement. *Journal of Economic Literature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that informal enforcement mechanisms—such as reputational punishment for breach—can support efficient outcomes in repeated interactions, especially when formal legal enforcement is costly or unavailable. The efficiency of punishment depends on contract form: warranty and bonus contracts can achieve first-best efficiency under certain conditions, while simple fixed-price contracts relying on reputation alone are often inefficient due to risk of reputation loss even when performance is adequate. The paper predicts that warranty contracts are more efficient for 'normal' goods (where bad outcomes are rare), while bonus contracts are more efficient for 'innovative' goods (where good outcomes are rare). The efficiency of punishment/reward mechanisms is moderated by the observability of breach, the frequency of trade, the quality of law, and the allocation of reputational capital. The paper also discusses how formal and informal enforcement can be substitutes or complements, and how the choice of contract form and enforcement mechanism affects overall surplus and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment and reward mechanisms in repeated games and contract-like environments. It suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the contract form (e.g., whether punishment is triggered by observable breach, whether rewards are available), the type of good or task (frequency and likelihood of good/bad outcomes), and the quality of formal enforcement. For public-goods-game-like environments, the results imply that informal punishment (reputation loss) can improve efficiency, but only under certain conditions—especially when breach is easily observed and when contract terms (such as warranties or bonuses) are used to align incentives. The paper also highlights that the efficiency gains from punishment may be limited or negative if punishment is triggered by events outside the agent's control, or if the risk of reputation loss is high even for compliant agents. Thus, for the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms and the information structure are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Ille, S. (2021). The evolution of sectarianism. *Communications in Nonlinear Science and Numerical Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of coercive (punishment-like) strategies in a repeated bargaining game can lead to either efficient egalitarian outcomes or inefficient sectarian contracts, depending on the relative costs and benefits of coercion and the structure of social learning. When the cost of coercion is low and potential gains from inegalitarian contracts are high, societies are more likely to settle into inefficient, conflictual, or sectarian equilibria. When both groups have similar coercive capacities and the cost of coercion is high, egalitarian and efficient outcomes are more likely. The model also shows that collective memory, identity politics, and initial asymmetries can tip the balance toward persistent conflict or sectarianism, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (coercion) on efficiency is highly context-dependent. If punishment is symmetric and costly, it can support efficient cooperation (egalitarian contracts). If punishment is asymmetric or the gains from inegalitarian outcomes are high, punishment can entrench inefficient, conflictual, or exploitative equilibria. The model suggests that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the symmetry of player power, and the structure of social learning critically moderate the efficiency impact of punishment. However, the model is not a direct PGG or public goods game, but rather a repeated bargaining/coordination game with endogenous punishment-like coercion. Thus, its findings are most relevant for adjacent settings where punishment can be used to enforce either efficient or inefficient group outcomes.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2016). Indirect reciprocity can overcome free-rider problems on costly moral assessment. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with costly assessment, cooperation is not stable unless there is a mechanism to sanction or exclude those who evade the cost of assessment. By introducing a pre-assessment stage that labels non-payers as 'nasty' and excludes them from receiving help, the model shows that a cooperative equilibrium (where everyone pays for assessment and helps others) can be locally stable if the benefit of helping exceeds the cost of assessment. This mechanism is analogous to pool punishment in public goods games and is effective under leading social norms. The findings are theoretical but directly address efficiency and the conditions for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional mechanisms to sanction or exclude cost-evaders (such as pre-assessment or pool punishment) can significantly improve efficiency in social dilemma games with costly monitoring or punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of a pre-assessment or institutionalized punishment mechanism is likely to shift efficiency from low (collapse of cooperation) to high (stable cooperation), provided the net benefit of cooperation is positive. The results are robust across different social norms and suggest that the design of the punishment/sanctioning mechanism is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2006). Unifying the theories of inclusive fitness and reciprocal altruism. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that the evolution of cooperation (altruism) in both kin and non-kin contexts can be predicted by a generalized Hamilton's rule that incorporates the covariance between genotypes and the cooperative behaviors of others, as well as nonadditive (synergistic) effects. In iterated prisoner's dilemma models, conditional strategies like tit-for-tat can increase in frequency when the benefit received from others' cooperation exceeds the cost, as predicted by Queller's rule. The same logic applies to mutualistic interactions between species. The paper shows that synergy (nonadditivity) and conditional behavior can both facilitate the evolution of cooperation, even in the absence of kinship or repeated interactions. Punishment is discussed as a possible mechanism for positive assortment but is not modeled. The findings are theoretical and do not provide empirical estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background for understanding how mechanisms like punishment, synergy, and conditional behavior can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency, but it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that any mechanism (including punishment) that increases positive assortment or synergy can increase the likelihood that cooperation evolves, but does not specify the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes in experimental settings. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful for mechanistic context but not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhu, Y. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2020). Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems on Multiple Community Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Systems Man Cybernetics-systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the interaction network (community structure and interaction probabilities) and the payoff matrix jointly determine which strategies dominate and what equilibrium payoffs are achieved. In dominating games (like the prisoner's dilemma), the system converges to full defection or cooperation, mirroring well-mixed results. In coexistence games (like the snowdrift game), the system can reach an interior equilibrium with mixed strategies, provided certain conditions on the interaction matrix are met. In coordination games (like the stag-hunt), multiple stable equilibria are possible, and the final state depends on initial conditions and network structure. The paper provides sufficient conditions for the stability of equilibria and shows that higher payoff strategies tend to dominate. However, it does not analyze the effect of punishment, reward, or other institutional interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and payoff matrices affect equilibrium efficiency and welfare in multiagent evolutionary games. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it model public goods games directly. Its results are most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in structured populations without institutional interventions. It does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from punishment or reward mechanisms, but it does clarify how efficiency depends on game type and network structure in the absence of such mechanisms.

- source: Golubski, A. J., & Klausmeier, C. A. (2010). Control in mutualisms Combined implications of partner choice and bargaining roles. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to appropriate surplus (analogous to control over punishment/reward) and the ability to choose partners (partner choice) interact to determine the evolution of mutualism and the distribution of payoffs in mutualistic associations. When the guild with partner choice also receives the surplus, mutualistic strategies are favored and average payoffs are high. However, the presence of empty slots or high frequencies of exploiters can lead to cyclical dynamics where exploitative strategies temporarily increase, reducing average payoffs. The model predicts that partner choice is most effective at selecting against moderately exploitative partners, but highly exploitative partners can persist under some conditions. The overall effect on efficiency is mixed: partner choice and surplus control can increase efficiency, but not always, especially if exploiters are present or if association opportunities are limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (partner choice, surplus appropriation) can increase efficiency (group payoff) in mutualism-like bargaining games, especially when combined. However, the effect is not universally positive: the presence of exploiters and the structure of association opportunities (e.g., empty slots) can lead to reduced efficiency or cyclical dynamics. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling partner choice and surplus control generally increases efficiency, but the effect may be moderated or reversed if exploiters can persist or if association opportunities are limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its predictions should be transferred with caution, but it provides informative indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment-like mechanisms improve or fail to improve efficiency.

- source: Ognedal, T. (2016). Morality in the market. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing the fraction of honest sellers can reduce or increase social surplus depending on the initial prevalence of honesty and the elasticity of demand. When honesty is rare, more honesty can increase production costs and reduce welfare; when honesty is common, more honesty can improve efficiency. (2) Sanctions (punishment) are a more reliable tool for improving efficiency, as they do not distort the ranking of private and social costs. (3) The effects of honesty among buyers and sellers are asymmetric: honesty among sellers affects production efficiency, while honesty among buyers affects allocation efficiency. (4) Policies that increase individual compliance (e.g., moral suasion) may have no effect or even negative effects on market-level efficiency, depending on market structure and the distribution of honesty. (5) The relationship between individual-level compliance and market-level efficiency is complex and non-linear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency is more reliably positive than the effect of increasing intrinsic motivation (morality/honesty). In environments analogous to public goods games with market entry/exit and endogenous prices, punishment that is uniform across agents improves efficiency by aligning private and social costs, while increasing the fraction of intrinsically honest agents can have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially when honesty is rare. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (extrinsic sanctions) is more likely to increase efficiency than relying on intrinsic motivation, and that the effect of punishment may depend on the distribution of compliance and market structure. However, the model is not a direct PGG or experimental result, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Reiersen, J. (2017). TRUST AS A BOOSTER. *Journal of Business Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that trust is essential for enabling players to leave secure networks and engage in market transactions that offer higher potential payoffs but also greater risk. The model shows that the willingness to transact in markets depends on the expected probability of encountering trustworthy partners, the relative payoffs from network versus market transactions, and the cost of being cheated. When trust is high and/or the gains from market transactions are large relative to networks, players are more likely to leave networks and realize higher efficiency (total payoff). Conversely, when trust is low or the cost of being cheated is high, players remain in networks, foregoing potential efficiency gains. The model highlights the distinction between trust (needed for market transactions) and assurance (provided by networks through repeated interaction and informal sanctions).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how efficiency (expected group payoff) in environments with and without trust/assurance mechanisms depends on the structure of payoffs, risk, and expectations about others' behavior. While it does not directly model public goods games or experimental punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where players can choose between secure, repeated interactions (with informal sanctions) and riskier, anonymous interactions (requiring trust). The model suggests that efficiency gains from moving to market-like environments depend on the level of trust and the relative payoffs, and that mechanisms providing assurance (such as punishment or repeated interaction) can substitute for trust in supporting cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment in public goods games, so its relevance for predicting the effect of enabling punishment is indirect and primarily theoretical.

- source: Wang, R. W., & Shi, L. (2010). The evolution of cooperation in asymmetric systems. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric cooperative systems, the net benefit (efficiency) to the recipient depends critically on the intrinsic growth rates of benefit and cost, the strength of density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors, and the availability of common resources. Stronger density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors and higher initial relatedness (intrinsic contribution ratio) increase the probability of maintaining cooperation (positive efficiency). However, changes in these parameters or resource saturation can lead to a transition from cooperation to conflict, with net benefit becoming negative. The model also shows that recipient-enforced discrimination (punishing cheaters, rewarding cooperators) can increase initial relatedness and favor cooperation, but does not guarantee global stability. The system can exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics between cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how efficiency in public-goods-like asymmetric systems is affected by the number of actors, resource saturation, and density-dependent competition. It suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation are not guaranteed by punishment or sanctions alone, but depend on ecological and structural parameters such as the strength of interference competition and resource availability. For prediction tasks, the model implies that enabling punishment (in the form of recipient-enforced discrimination) can increase efficiency only when it effectively increases initial relatedness and when density-dependent competition among actors is strong enough to prevent resource overexploitation. However, efficiency can decrease or oscillate if these conditions are not met. The findings are most relevant for systems with asymmetric roles and may be less directly transferable to standard symmetric PGG lab games.

- source: Nowak, M. A. (2006). Five rules for the evolution of cooperation. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr
  findings: The paper derives and summarizes simple mathematical rules for when cooperation can evolve and be maintained in populations, based on five mechanisms: kin selection, direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, network reciprocity, and group selection. For each, a threshold condition is given (e.g., benefit-to-cost ratio exceeding a critical value) that determines whether cooperation can increase average fitness or group payoff. The paper notes that, without such mechanisms, natural selection favors defection and reduces average fitness. Punishment is discussed as a factor that can enhance cooperation within these frameworks, but is not itself a primary mechanism for the evolution of cooperation. The paper does not provide direct predictions or data on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background on the conditions under which cooperation can increase group efficiency or average payoff, but does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment in public goods games or the impact of specific game design dimensions. It is useful for understanding the broader theoretical context and for mapping how punishment may interact with other mechanisms (e.g., indirect reciprocity, group selection), but does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or specific design features. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as high-level theoretical context rather than as a source of empirical effect sizes or moderator effects.

- source: Szabó, G., & Bunth, G. (2018). Social dilemmas in multistrategy evolutionary potential games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that in symmetric potential games, efficiency (measured as average payoff or contentment) is maximized when the symmetric part of the payoff matrix dominates, aligning individual and collective interests. The introduction of antisymmetric (deluding) components can create social dilemmas, leading to inefficient Nash equilibria and lower average payoffs. The likelihood of inefficiency increases with the number of strategies. However, if the antisymmetric component supports the same equilibrium as the symmetric part, it can help maintain efficiency, especially under noise. The paper provides explicit mappings of parameter regions where efficiency is high or low, but does not study punishment or public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the structure of payoff matrices in multi-strategy evolutionary games affects efficiency, especially the role of antisymmetric (deluding) components in creating or resolving social dilemmas. While it does not study public goods games or punishment directly, it offers a framework for predicting when efficiency will be high or low based on the alignment of individual and collective incentives. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that efficiency is sensitive to the presence of payoff components that create conflicts between individual and group interests, and that the probability of inefficiency increases with the number of available strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or specific public goods game design dimensions.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Santos, F. C., & Chalub, F. A. C. C. (2006). Stern-judging: A simple, successful norm which promotes cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that, in a multilevel evolutionary model of indirect reciprocity, the stern-judging norm—where helping good individuals or refusing help to bad individuals is rewarded, and the opposite is punished—emerges as the dominant and most robust social norm. This norm leads to maximized average payoffs for groups, indicating high efficiency in sustaining cooperation. The result is robust to different forms of group selection and inter-tribe conflict. The model suggests that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms are sufficient to promote and maintain high levels of cooperation and group welfare, even in the presence of errors and migration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms (like stern-judging) can robustly increase group efficiency and cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is adjacent: it involves costly helping, reputation, and punishment via social norms. The findings suggest that, in environments where indirect reciprocity and reputation are salient, enabling punishment (in the form of social norm enforcement) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines. However, the model is abstract and does not manipulate most game design dimensions directly, so its predictive value is strongest for settings with similar indirect reciprocity structure and less for standard PGGs.

- source: Morsky, B., Plotkin, J. B., & Akcay, E. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with Bayesian reasoning and biases. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that Bayesian reasoning about errors in action and observation generally reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff in indirect reciprocity games, compared to models where agents do not reason about errors. This effect is robust across most social norms (Shunning, Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), but under the Scoring norm, Bayesian reasoning can actually promote cooperation and support a mixed equilibrium with all strategy types. The impact of Bayesian reasoning is strongest when error rates are low. Optimism bias (overestimating the frequency of good individuals) generally undermines cooperation, while mild pessimism bias can sometimes promote it, but strong biases of either type destroy cooperation. The model provides heatmaps and bifurcation diagrams showing how cooperation and payoffs change with error rates and biases. The findings are relevant for predicting how changes in information processing, error rates, and social norms affect group efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting efficiency in public-goods-like games with punishment/reward mechanisms based on reputation. It shows that the cognitive sophistication of agents (Bayesian reasoning about errors) can reduce group efficiency, except under certain social norms (Scoring). The results suggest that, in environments where punishment/reward is reputation-based and subject to errors, enabling more sophisticated reasoning may not always increase efficiency. The effect of punishment (via conditional defection) on efficiency is highly sensitive to the social norm, error rates, and the presence of optimism/pessimism bias. For downstream prediction, the paper supports the view that punishment mechanisms relying on reputation can backfire or be less effective if agents reason about errors, unless the norm is simple (Scoring). The findings are most transferable to settings where punishment is implemented as conditional cooperation/defection based on reputation, rather than direct costly punishment.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2015). Reputation Effects in Public and Private Interactions. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperative strategies in indirect reciprocity depends critically on the observability of private actions (q), the frequency of public interactions (p), and the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c). Honest cooperation (leading to full group efficiency) is only stable when private actions are sufficiently observable and b/c is high. When private actions are rarely observed or b/c is low, only partial cooperation or defection is stable, resulting in lower group efficiency. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for these transitions. The presence of public observation increases the incentive for cooperation, but if private actions are unobserved, individuals may defect in private, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how observability (analogous to information feedback or monitoring in public goods games) and the benefit-to-cost ratio moderate the effect of reputation-based incentives on group efficiency. While not a direct PGG or punishment study, its results suggest that increasing the probability that actions are observed (or reputationally consequential) increases the likelihood of stable, high-efficiency cooperation. In prediction tasks, similar mechanisms (e.g., transparency, monitoring, or reputation updating) should be expected to increase the efficiency gains from reputation or sanctioning systems, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high. However, if observability is low, the efficiency impact of such systems will be limited.

- source: Han, T. A., & Tran-Thanh, L. (2018). Cost-effective external interference for promoting the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: This work provides analytical and numerical results showing that an external decision-maker can promote high levels of cooperation in a population of self-interested agents by using a cost-efficient, state-dependent rewarding scheme. Rather than always rewarding cooperators, it is more cost-effective to reward only when the number of cooperators is below a threshold. The optimal threshold depends on the per-individual reward cost, the intensity of selection, and the desired cooperation level. For a wide range of parameters, this targeted approach achieves the same or higher cooperation at a lower total cost than always-on institutional incentives. The model does not directly analyze group payoff or efficiency in the public goods sense, but the results are closely related to maximizing cooperation frequency while minimizing resource expenditure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for predicting the effect of reward-based interventions (not punishment) on cooperation and cost-efficiency in Prisoner's Dilemma-like settings. It suggests that targeted, conditional incentives can be more cost-effective than unconditional ones, especially under weak selection. However, the model is not a public goods game and does not analyze punishment, so direct quantitative predictions for PGG efficiency with punishment are not possible. The findings support the idea that the design of incentive schemes (e.g., when and whom to reward) strongly moderates the cost-effectiveness of cooperation promotion, and that always-on incentives may be suboptimal. For downstream prediction, the results are most informative for settings with external, state-dependent rewards, and less so for punishment or standard PGGs.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2004). The logic of reprobation: assessment and action rules for indirect reciprocation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that indirect reciprocity models with assessment rules that distinguish justified from unjustified defections (STANDING) are more effective at sustaining cooperation and suppressing defectors than simple scoring rules, especially in the presence of errors. However, even simple scoring can support cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and players have enough interactions. The model demonstrates that the structure of the population, the number of observed interactions, and the error rates in action and perception all influence the stability of cooperation. Analytical results show that, under certain assumptions about the distribution of the number of rounds, stable mixtures of discriminating and non-discriminating altruists can exist, leading to high average payoffs. The findings suggest that costly punishment (refusing to help) can sustain cooperation, but is less robust than non-costly forms (STANDING).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus efficiency) depends on whether punishment is costly, the benefit-to-cost ratio, error rates, and the number of interactions. While the model is not a standard PGG, the parallels are strong: refusing to help is analogous to punishing defectors, and the group payoff is analogous to efficiency. The results imply that costly punishment can increase efficiency, but is less robust than non-costly punishment, and that the details of the assessment and action rules, as well as the game structure (e.g., number of rounds, observation probability), are important moderators. For prediction, the paper supports the view that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and errors are not too frequent, but the effect is sensitive to the costliness and implementation of punishment.

- source: Phillips, T., Li, J. W., & Kendall, G. (2014). The Effects of Extra-Somatic Weapons on the Evolution of Human Cooperation towards Non-Kin. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Simulating a population of 40 players using 25 well-known IPD strategies, the authors compare two conditions: a standard evolutionary IPD and a 'weapons use' IPD where repeated defections trigger a risk of elimination (interpreted as a proxy for lethal punishment or high-cost disputes). Cooperative strategies, on average, survive significantly longer under weapons use, while non-cooperative strategies survive for fewer generations. The 'Always Cooperate' strategy, typically the least successful, becomes the most successful cooperative strategy as the risk of elimination increases. The model suggests that high costs for repeated defection (analogous to punishment) can shift evolutionary success toward cooperation, even in the absence of explicit monitoring or repeated interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that increasing the cost of within-group conflict (modeled as a risk of elimination after repeated defections) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperative strategies and decrease that of non-cooperative strategies. While the model is not a public goods game and does not report group efficiency per se, the findings are relevant for predicting that introducing or increasing the severity of punishment in repeated social dilemmas is likely to increase the relative payoff or evolutionary success of cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is severe and unavoidable, and less so to standard PGGs with explicit, voluntary punishment stages. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, chat), but supports the general principle that costly sanctions can promote cooperation and group-level success.

- source: Li, S. J., Bao, W., Dai, Y. Y., Ye, Y., & Xie, N. G. (2022). Emotional game and the evolution of cooperation considering the effects of reputation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that adding a reputation mechanism based on emotional profiles to a voluntary prisoner's dilemma game (VPDG) substantially increases both the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of the population. The reputation mechanism works by making individuals more likely to cooperate with those who have good reputations and less likely to cooperate with those who have bad reputations, effectively integrating both reward and punishment effects. Compared to previous models (VPDG alone and VPDG with emotion but no reputation), the model with reputation achieves higher average payoffs and more stable cooperation, especially in regions of the parameter space where cooperation would otherwise be low. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is not too high (T < 1.5), but even at higher temptations, the reputation mechanism maintains higher cooperation and payoff than the baselines.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that mechanisms integrating both reward and punishment via reputation can substantially increase group efficiency in voluntary public-goods-like games. While the model is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure (VPDG with loner option) and the explicit reporting of average payoff as a welfare/efficiency measure make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of adding punishment/reward/reputation mechanisms in similar environments. The results suggest that, when the control (no reputation) game has moderate or low efficiency, enabling a reputation mechanism (which includes both positive and negative sanctions) can drive efficiency close to the cooperative maximum, especially when the temptation to defect is not extreme. However, the findings are based on simulation, not human experiments, and the specific implementation of reputation is more complex than simple punishment. The model does not directly manipulate all design dimensions (e.g., player count, chat, etc.), so predictions should be adjusted accordingly.

- source: Milinski, M. (2022). Extortion - A voracious prosocial strategy. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that extortionate strategies in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games can enforce high cooperation from coplayers, but at the cost of an unequal payoff distribution favoring the extortioner. When coplayers attempt to punish extortioners by withholding cooperation, both parties' payoffs decrease, demonstrating a form of costly punishment. In symmetric settings, this can discipline extortioners, but in asymmetric settings (e.g., with replacement power or extra incentives), extortioners can persist and maintain higher payoffs. The review highlights that the efficiency of the group (total payoff) may not increase with extortion, as the gains are captured by the extortioner and punishment reduces total surplus. The findings are based on a synthesis of theory and several experimental studies, but the review itself does not report new experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, costly punishment via withholding cooperation) on efficiency depends on the symmetry of the game and the power structure. In symmetric games, punishment can reduce the extortioner's advantage but at a cost to both players, lowering efficiency. In asymmetric games, extortioners can avoid being disciplined and extract higher payoffs, further reducing the coplayer's share. The review does not directly address public goods games or classic punishment institutions, but the mechanisms discussed (costly punishment, power asymmetry, incentive structures) are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve or reduce efficiency in related environments. The evidence is more about distributional outcomes and the limits of punishment than about average efficiency increases from punishment.
